[2606.29203] Bayesian Best-Arm Identification with Abstention: A Polynomial-to-Exponential Phase Transition Bayesian Best-Arm Identification with Abstention: A Polynomial-to-Exponential Phase Transition Yuqi Huang Affiliation: Department of Mathematics, National University of Singapore Yunlong Hou Affiliation: Department of Mathematics, National University of Singapore Vincent Y. F. Tan Affiliation: Department of Mathematics, National University of Singapore Affiliation: Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, National University of Singapore Abstract We study the Bayesian fixed-budget best-arm identification problem in which a learner can abstain from making a terminal recommendation. Subject to an abstention budget α \alpha , we analyze the probability of undetected error–the risk of recommending a suboptimal arm without abstaining. Our central finding is that abstention induces a phase transition: without abstention, the error probability decays polynomially in the sampling budget T T ; in contrast, introducing any small positive abstention budget shifts this to an exponential decay. For Gaussian priors and rewards, in the regime T → ∞ T\to\infty followed by α ↓ 0 \alpha\downarrow 0 , we establish exact matching information-theoretic lower bounds and algorithmic upper bounds on the optimal error exponent, which takes the form exp ⁡ ( − α 2 ​ T 8 ​ κ ν 2 ) \exp(-\frac{\alpha^{2}T}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}) . The hardness parameter κ ν \kappa_{\nu} represents the prior density of the top-two gap at zero, highlighting that nearly tied instances drive the fundamental error. We introduce an adaptive algorithm, PGWS , that successfully achieves this optimal exponent by expending its abstention budget on statistically ambiguous instances. We further demonstrate that this polynomial-to-exponential improvement is exclusively a Bayesian phenomenon–in the frequentist setting, abstention only affects lower-order exponent terms. We also extend our results beyond the Gaussian model. 1 Introduction 1.1 Problem Overview and Motivation Fixed-Budget Best-Arm Identification (FB-BAI) ( 3 ; 16 ; 6 ) is a canonical pure exploration problem in sequential decision making. A learner is given a sampling budget, adaptively collects noisy observations from a set of K K arms with unknown mean rewards, and finally recommends the arm believed to have the largest mean. This formulation captures the statistical core of ranking and selection, online experimentation, and treatment-selection problems, where samples are costly and the objective is to make a reliable terminal recommendation rather than to maximize reward during the experiment ( 3 ; 17 ; 26 ) . Typical FB-BAI formulations require a firm terminal recommendation after the sampling budget is exhausted. In many applications, however, this forced decision, in which the decision maker commits to one of the K K arms, may pose undue risks. When the collected evidence is ambiguous, the decision maker may defer the decision, run a follow-up experiment, escalate the case to an expert, or choose a conservative default action. This motivates a FB-BAI model with terminal abstention option: after sampling, the learner may either recommend an arm or output an inconclusive decision. Abstention changes the notion of error. A policy that always abstains never makes an incorrect recommendation, but it is not useful. We therefore treat abstention as a scarce resource: the learner must keep the probability of abstention below a prescribed level α \alpha . Subject to this constraint, the performance criterion is the probability of an undetected error , namely the probability that the learner does not abstain but recommends a suboptimal arm. Informally, let a ⋆ a^{\star} denote the best arm, ? ? be the abstention decision, and a ^ ∈ [ K ] ∪ { ? } \widehat{a}\in[K]\cup\{?\} be the terminal output. The objective is to minimize ℙ ⁡ ( a ^ ∉ { a ⋆ , ? } ) , subject to ℙ ⁡ ( a ^ = ? ) ≤ α . \mathbb{P}\big(\widehat{a}\notin\{a^{\star},?\}\big),\quad\textrm{subject to}\quad\mathbb{P}(\widehat{a}=?)\leq\alpha. (1.1) This paper studies the problem in a Bayesian fixed-budget setting, where the unknown reward means are drawn from known priors. The Bayesian formulation is natural when the same experimental protocol is repeatedly applied to related instances, or when historical data provide a prior model for plausible reward configurations. It is also the setting in which the effect of abstention is particularly pronounced. Under a continuous prior, the Bayes error probability of forced-decision (i.e., without abstention) FB-BAI is dominated by instances in which the two best arms are nearly tied. These instances are rare, but they are also the hardest to resolve with a finite sampling budget. The central observation of this paper is that a small abstention budget allows the learner to spend its inconclusive decisions precisely on these statistically ambiguous near-tie cases. We show that this fundamentally results in a phase transition of the behavior of Bayesian BAI: even a small positive abstention budget substantially reduces the forced-decision polynomially small Bayes error probability into an exponentially small Bayes undetected error probability. We derive this exponent and show that it is governed by the amount of prior probability near top-two ties. The rest of the paper formalizes this principle, develops an adaptive algorithm that attains the optimal exponent, and contrasts the Bayesian phenomenon with the frequentist setting. 1.2 Problem Formulation and Top-Two Gap Complexity We now introduce the formal model. For an integer K ≥ 2 K\geq 2 , let [ K ] := { 1 , … , K } [K]:=\{1,\ldots,K\} . The learner is given a fixed sampling budget T T and a collection of K K arms. Arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] has an unknown mean reward μ i \mu_{i} . We study a Bayesian model in which the mean vector μ = ( μ 1 , … , μ K ) \mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}) is drawn from a known product Gaussian prior P ν ​ ( d ​ μ ) := ⨂ i = 1 K 𝒩 ⁡ ( ν i , σ 0 2 ) ​ ( d ​ μ i ) , P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu):=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{K}\mathcal{N}(\nu_{i},\sigma_{0}^{2})(\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}), where the prior means ν 1 , … , ν K ∈ ℝ \nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{K}\in\mathbb{R} and prior variance (which is common to all arms) σ 0 2 &gt; 0 \sigma_{0}^{2}&gt;0 are fixed and known. We define the best arm a ⋆ = a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) := argmax i ∈ [ K ] μ i a^{\star}=a^{\star}(\mu):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{i\in[K]}\mu_{i} which is unique almost surely since the prior P ν P_{\nu} is continuous. Conditional on μ \mu , the rewards from the arms are independent (across arms and time) and normally distributed: Y i , s ∣ μ i ∼ 𝒩 ( μ i , 1 ) , s ∈ ℕ , i ∈ [ K ] . Y_{i,s}\mid\mu_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},1),\qquad s\in\mathbb{N},~i\in[K]. Policies and Probability Laws. A policy π \pi consists of a sequence of sampling rules and a terminal decision rule. At each round t = 1 , … , T t=1,\ldots,T , the policy selects an arm A t ∈ [ K ] A_{t}\in[K] . Conditional on A t = i A_{t}=i and the mean vector μ \mu , the learner observes a random sample X t = Y i , N i ​ ( t ) X_{t}=Y_{i,N_{i}(t)} where N i ( t ) := ∑ s = 1 t 𝟙 { A s = i } N_{i}(t):=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{s}=i\} denotes the number of samples collected from arm i i up to and including time t t . The choice of the arm to be pulled A t A_{t} depends on the previous arm choices { A s } s = 1 t − 1 \{A_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1} and observations { X s } s = 1 t − 1 \{X_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1} . At the terminal time T T , the policy recommends an arm from [ K ] [K] as the estimated best arm or an abstention decision denoted as ? ? . The class of all such policies is denoted as Π T \Pi_{T} . For a fixed mean vector μ \mu , we write ℙ μ π \mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi} and 𝔼 μ π \mathbb{E}_{\mu}^{\pi} for the probability and expectation over the reward realizations and the potential randomization of the policy. The joint Bayesian law is the mixture ℙ ν π ​ ( ⋅ ) := ∫ ℝ K ℙ μ π ​ ( ⋅ ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) , \mathbb{P}_{\nu}^{\pi}(\cdot):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\cdot)\,P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu), and 𝔼 ν π \mathbb{E}_{\nu}^{\pi} is defined analogously. When the policy (resp. probability law) is clear from context, we suppress the superscript π \pi (resp. subscript ν \nu ). Terminal Decisions with Abstention. At the end of the experiment, the learner outputs a ^ T ∈ [ K ] ∪ { ? } \widehat{a}_{T}\in[K]\cup\{?\} , where ? ? denotes abstention, an inconclusive decision. The learner makes an undetected error if it recommends an arm and the recommended arm is not the true best arm, i.e., the event { a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ , ? } } = { a ^ T ∈ [ K ] , a ^ T ≠ a ⋆ } \big\{\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star},?\}\big\}=\big\{\widehat{a}_{T}\in[K],\ \widehat{a}_{T}\neq a^{\star}\big\} occurs. For a policy π ∈ Π T \pi\in\Pi_{T} , define its Bayes abstention probability and Bayes undetected error probability respectively as 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) := ℙ ν π ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) and ℰ T ​ ( π ) := ℙ ν π ​ ( a ^ T ∈ [ K ] , a ^ T ≠ a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) ) . \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi):=\mathbb{P}_{\nu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi):=\mathbb{P}_{\nu}^{\pi}\bigl(\widehat{a}_{T}\in[K],\ \widehat{a}_{T}\neq a^{\star}(\mu)\bigr). Given an abstention budget α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , let Π T ​ ( α ) := { π ∈ Π T : 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α } . \Pi_{T}(\alpha):=\{\pi\in\Pi_{T}:\mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha\}. (1.3) Top-Two Gap Complexity. We define the top-two gap Γ := μ ( 1 ) − μ ( 2 ) , \Gamma:=\mu_{(1)}-\mu_{(2)}, (1.4) where μ ( 1 ) &gt; μ ( 2 ) &gt; ⋯ &gt; μ ( K ) \mu_{(1)}&gt;\mu_{(2)}&gt;\cdots&gt;\mu_{(K)} denote the means of the arms arranged in decreasing order. Theorems 2.1 and 3.1 identify the density of Γ \Gamma at zero as the fundamental hardness parameter for the Bayesian BAI problem with abstention. For each arm i i , we denote the prior density and distribution function of μ i \mu_{i} as f i ​ ( x ) := 1 σ 0 ​ ϕ ​ ( x − ν i σ 0 ) , and F i ​ ( x ) := Φ ⁡ ( x − ν i σ 0 ) , f_{i}(x):=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\phi\left(\frac{x-\nu_{i}}{\sigma_{0}}\right),\quad\mbox{and}\quad F_{i}(x):=\Phi\left(\frac{x-\nu_{i}}{\sigma_{0}}\right), where ϕ ( x ) = 1 2 ​ π e − x 2 / 2 \phi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2} and Φ ⁡ ( x ) = ∫ − ∞ x ϕ ⁡ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u \Phi(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\phi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u are the standard normal density and cumulative distribution function, respectively. For a pair of distinct arms ( i , j ) ∈ [ K ] 2 (i,j)\in[K]^{2} , define their average midpoint variable as X i ​ j ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( ν i + ν j 2 , σ 0 2 2 ) X_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}}{2},\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2}\right) , and define the top-relevance weight w i ​ j := 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∏ k ≠ i , j Φ ⁡ ( X i ​ j − ν k σ 0 ) ] w_{ij}:=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\prod_{k\neq i,j}\Phi\!\left(\frac{X_{ij}-\nu_{k}}{\sigma_{0}}\right)\right] . The quantity w i ​ j w_{ij} is the prior probability, averaged over a local tie value of arms i i and j j , that this tied value lies above all remaining arms. Define the hardness parameter for Bayesian BAI with abstention problem as κ ν = ∑ i ≠ j ∫ ℝ f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F k ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = 1 σ 0 ​ π ​ ∑ 1 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ K w i ​ j ​ exp ⁡ ( − ( ν i − ν j ) 2 4 ​ σ 0 2 ) . \kappa_{\nu}=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}F_{k}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{1\leq i&lt;j\leq K}w_{ij}\exp\!\left(-\frac{(\nu_{i}-\nu_{j})^{2}}{4\sigma_{0}^{2}}\right). In Lemma A.1 , we show that the top-two gap Γ \Gamma is related to the hardness parameter κ ν \kappa_{\nu} as follows: ℙ ν ​ ( Γ ≤ ε ) = κ ν ​ ε + o ⁡ ( ε ) as ε ↓ 0 . \mathbb{P}_{\nu}(\Gamma\leq\varepsilon)=\kappa_{\nu}\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon)\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\downarrow 0. (1.5) In view of ( 1.5 ), we see that κ ν \kappa_{\nu} measures the amount of prior probability assigned to locally ambiguous instances. Specifically, pairs with similar prior means and a realistic chance of being the top two arms contribute more to κ ν \kappa_{\nu} , whereas pairs that are unlikely to be optimal contribute little. 1.3 Main Contributions and Results We formulate the fixed-budget BAI with abstention problem under the Bayesian setting. We study this problem under the large-budget and small-abstention probability regime through the iterated limit T → ∞ T\to\infty followed by α ↓ 0 \alpha\downarrow 0 . Our main result is a sharp characterization of the exponential rate of decay of the optimal Bayes undetected error probability under abstention. In the Gaussian prior and Gaussian reward model, we prove the information-theoretic lower bound lim inf α ↓ 0 lim inf T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ 𝔈 T ​ ( α ) ≥ − 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 . \liminf_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathfrak{E}_{T}(\alpha)\geq-\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}. We also propose an adaptive algorithm, Posterior Gap Weighted Sampling with Abstention ( PGWS ( α \alpha )), whose abstention probability is calibrated exactly to α \alpha and whose undetected error probability ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) \mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha)) satisfies lim sup α ↓ 0 lim sup T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ − 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 . \limsup_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))\leq-\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}. Combining these results, we see that the fundamental hardness of the Bayesian BAI with abstention problem depends on the prior through κ ν \kappa_{\nu} , the density of top-two gap at zero. In other words, the complexity is intimately related to the prior probability of instances in which the means of two best arms are nearly the same. Subsequently, we compare the abstention problem with ordinary (forced-decision) Bayesian BAI, where the learner is compelled to recommend an arm. We establish that the optimal forced-decision Bayes probability of misidentification satisfies lim inf T → ∞ T ​ 𝔈 T ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 2 π ​ κ ν . \liminf_{T\to\infty}\sqrt{T}\,\mathfrak{E}_{T}(0)\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\kappa_{\nu}. Therefore, allowing an α \alpha fraction of abstentions transits the Bayes probability of error from being on the polynomial scale to the exponential scale. We further demonstrate that this phase transition is inherently Bayesian. In contrast, for any fixed frequentist instance with a positive gap, the error probability of conventional forced-decision FB-BAI already exhibits exponential decay in T T ; see, e.g., 16 . As a result, terminal abstention cannot improve the leading error exponent and affects only lower-order terms. Finally, the analysis is extended beyond the Gaussian model. For regular one-parameter reward models, the same exponent takes the form 1 / ( 8 ​ κ 2 ) 1/(8\kappa^{2}) , where κ \kappa is the top-two gap density evaluated in Fisher–Rao information coordinates; see Eqn. ( 4.1 ). We also provide numerical experiments illustrating the benefit of abstention and the practical advantage of our adaptive algorithm which we call PGWS . 1.4 Related work Best-arm Identification and Ranking and Selection. Best-arm identification is a central pure-exploration problem in multi-armed bandits. In the fixed-confidence setting, the objective is to identify the best arm with prescribed confidence using as few samples as possible ( 20 ; 10 ; 17 ) . In the fixed-budget setting, which is our focus, the learner is given a sampling budget and aims to minimize the probability of an incorrect terminal recommendation ( 3 ; 16 ; 6 ; 19 ; 29 ) . For a fixed instance with a positive gap, this probability typically decays exponentially in the budget, with the exponent determined by instance-dependent complexity. Our work instead studies a Bayesian average over instances and allows terminal abstention. The problem is also related to ranking and selection and ordinal optimization, where the objective is often to maximize the probability of correct selection under a simulation budget ( 7 ; 12 ; 14 ) . In contrast to most of that literature, our performance criterion is an abstention-constrained Bayes undetected error probability. Bayesian Best Arm Identification. Bayesian BAI involves a prior distribution over the unknown reward means. Bayesian algorithms such as Thompson sampling and top-two Thompson sampling have been studied for posterior convergence and BAI ( 26 ; 27 ) . Recent work develops prior-sensitive guarantees for Bayesian BAI: 2 study Gaussian fixed-budget Bayesian BAI, while 18 characterize Bayesian simple-regret rates under continuity assumptions and identify near-tie instances as the leading source of error. Related work also studies prior-dependent allocations in structured Bayesian fixed-budget BAI ( 24 ) and fixed-confidence BAI in the Bayesian setting ( 15 ) . Our contribution is to add a terminal abstention option and provide a full characterization of the undetected error exponent under an abstention budget. Abstention and Reject-Option Decisions. Abstention has a long history in statistical decision theory and classification. Classical reject-option rules trade prediction error against rejection ( 8 ) , and selective prediction studies risk-coverage tradeoffs in supervised learning ( 4 ; 9 ) . These settings are typically passive, whereas our learner actively allocates a fixed sampling budget before deciding whether to recommend or abstain. Bandit models with abstention-like actions include selective sampling for online BAI ( 5 ) and regret minimization with per-round abstention ( 30 ) . In contrast, our model permits abstention only at the terminal recommendation stage. Erasure Decoding and Undetected Error. An essential motivation for considering this setup arises from erasure decoding in information theory. In channel coding with an erasure option, the decoder may either output a message or declare an erasure; performance is then measured by the tradeoff between the total and undetected error probabilities. This line of work dates back to Forney’s exponential bounds for erasure, list, and decision-feedback decoding ( 11 ) , with later refinements for erasure/list exponents, exact random-coding exponents, and asymmetric erasure-error regimes ( 23 ; 28 ; 13 ) . Our abstention option plays an analogous role to erasure, but the BAI setting is an active sequential experiment, in contrast to the non-sequential nature of channel coding (without feedback). 2 Algorithm and Error Analysis 2.1 The Algorithm PGWS We now present Posterior Gap Weighted Sampling with Abstention ( PGWS ). This policy has two components, a sampling rule and a terminal decision rule. During the sampling phase, it maintains Gaussian posterior distributions of the arm means and samples arms according to posterior mean gaps. At the terminal time, it either recommends the posterior mean leader or abstains, with the abstention rule calibrated to the prescribed budget α \alpha . Let ℱ t := σ ⁡ ( A 1 , X 1 , … , A t , X t ) \mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(A_{1},X_{1},\ldots,A_{t},X_{t}) denote the history up to time t t , with ℱ 0 := { ∅ , Ω } \mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{\varnothing,\Omega\} , augmented to include any external randomization used by the policy. Under the Gaussian prior and Gaussian reward model, the posterior distributions after t t samples are independent across arms: μ i ∣ ℱ t ∼ 𝒩 ( M i ( t ) , V i ( t ) ) , i ∈ [ K ] , \mu_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(M_{i}(t),V_{i}(t)\big),\qquad i\in[K], where the posterior variance V i ​ ( t ) V_{i}(t) and mean M i ​ ( t ) M_{i}(t) are, respectively, V i ​ ( t ) = 1 σ 0 − 2 + N i ​ ( t ) , and M i ​ ( t ) = V i ​ ( t ) ​ ( ν i σ 0 2 + ∑ s = 1 N i ​ ( t ) Y i , s ) . V_{i}(t)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}^{-2}+N_{i}(t)},\quad\mbox{and}\quad M_{i}(t)=V_{i}(t)\left(\frac{\nu_{i}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{i}(t)}Y_{i,s}\right). (2.1) At each time t t , define the posterior mean leader b ^ t := arg ⁡ max i ∈ [ K ] ​ M i ​ ( t ) \widehat{b}_{t}:=\arg\max_{i\in[K]}M_{i}(t) . For i ≠ b ^ t i\neq\widehat{b}_{t} , define the posterior mean gap Δ ^ i ​ ( t ) := M b ^ t ​ ( t ) − M i ​ ( t ) , \widehat{\Delta}_{i}(t):=M_{\widehat{b}_{t}}(t)-M_{i}(t), with Δ ^ b ^ t ​ ( t ) := min j ≠ b ^ t ⁡ Δ ^ j ​ ( t ) \widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{b}_{t}}(t):=\min_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{t}}\widehat{\Delta}_{j}(t) . Algorithm 1 Posterior Gap Weighted Sampling with Abstention ( PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) ) 1: Budget T T , abstention level α \alpha , prior parameters ( ν 1 , … , ν K ) (\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{K}) and σ 0 2 \sigma_{0}^{2} . 2: Initialize N i ​ ( 0 ) = 0 N_{i}(0)=0 , M i ​ ( 0 ) = ν i M_{i}(0)=\nu_{i} , and V i ​ ( 0 ) = σ 0 2 V_{i}(0)=\sigma_{0}^{2} for all i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] . 3: for t = 0 , 1 , … , T − 1 t=0,1,\ldots,T-1 do 4: if there exists i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] such that N i ​ ( t ) &lt; t + 1 N_{i}(t)&lt;\sqrt{t+1} then 5: Sample one such arm, using deterministic tie-breaking. 6: else 7: Compute b ^ t \widehat{b}_{t} , the gaps { Δ ^ i ​ ( t ) } i ∈ [ K ] \{\widehat{\Delta}_{i}(t)\}_{i\in[K]} , and the sampling probabilities { p i ​ ( t ) } i ∈ [ K ] \{p_{i}(t)\}_{i\in[K]} in ( 2.2 ). 8: Sample arm i i with probability p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) . 9: end if 10: Observe the reward and update N i ​ ( t + 1 ) N_{i}(t+1) , M i ​ ( t + 1 ) M_{i}(t+1) , and V i ​ ( t + 1 ) V_{i}(t+1) according to ( 2.1 ). 11: end for 12: Set b ^ T = arg ⁡ max i ∈ [ K ] ​ M i ​ ( T ) \widehat{b}_{T}=\arg\max_{i\in[K]}M_{i}(T) . 13: Compute the terminal statistic R T R_{T} in ( 2.3 ). 14: Output a ^ T \widehat{a}_{T} according to the abstention rule ( 2.6 ). The forced-exploration step in Lines 3–4 ensures that every arm is sufficiently sampled to yield an accurate estimate of the mean. On all remaining rounds, PGWS samples arm i i with probability p i ​ ( t ) = Δ ^ i ​ ( t ) − 2 ∑ ℓ = 1 K Δ ^ ℓ ​ ( t ) − 2 , i ∈ [ K ] . p_{i}(t)=\frac{\widehat{\Delta}_{i}(t)^{-2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\widehat{\Delta}_{\ell}(t)^{-2}},\qquad i\in[K]. (2.2) Consequently, the leader and the closest challenger receive equal sampling probability, while arms with larger posterior mean gaps receive smaller probabilities. At the end of the sampling phase, PGWS measures the strength of the posterior evidence in favor of the final leader b ^ T \widehat{b}_{T} . Define R T := min j ≠ b ^ T ⁡ ( M b ^ T ​ ( T ) − M j ​ ( T ) ) 2 2 ​ ( V b ^ T ​ ( T ) + V j ​ ( T ) ) , R_{T}:=\min_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{T}}\frac{\bigl(M_{\widehat{b}_{T}}(T)-M_{j}(T)\bigr)^{2}}{2\bigl(V_{\widehat{b}_{T}}(T)+V_{j}(T)\bigr)}, (2.3) which represents the smallest pairwise posterior Gaussian exponent separating the final leader from its challengers. A small value of R T R_{T} indicates that at least one challenger remains statistically difficult to be distinguished from the leader. To calibrate abstention, let r T , α r_{T,\alpha} be the lower α \alpha -quantile of R T R_{T} under the joint Bayesian law induced by the sampling rule, i.e., r T , α := inf { r ≥ 0 : ℙ ⁡ ( R T ≤ r ) ≥ α } . r_{T,\alpha}:=\inf\bigl\{r\geq 0:\mathbb{P}(R_{T}\leq r)\geq\alpha\bigr\}. (2.4) If the distribution of R T R_{T} has an atom at the threshold, define θ T , α := { α − ℙ ⁡ ( R T &lt; r T , α ) ℙ ⁡ ( R T = r T , α ) , ℙ ⁡ ( R T = r T , α ) &gt; 0 , 0 , ℙ ⁡ ( R T = r T , α ) = 0 . \theta_{T,\alpha}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-\mathbb{P}(R_{T}&lt;r_{T,\alpha})}{\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})},&amp;\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})&gt;0,\\ 0,&amp;\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})=0.\end{cases} (2.5) The terminal decision is a ^ T = { ? , R T &lt; r T , α , ? , R T = r T , α ​ with probability ​ θ T , α , b ^ T , otherwise . \widehat{a}_{T}=\begin{cases}?,&amp;R_{T}&lt;r_{T,\alpha},\\ ?,&amp;R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha}\text{ with probability }\theta_{T,\alpha},\\ \widehat{b}_{T},&amp;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} (2.6) Thus PGWS abstains on the least confident posterior histories, up to boundary randomization which is used only to obtain exact calibration when R T R_{T} has an atom at its α \alpha -quantile. Remark 2.1 (Computing the quantile) . The quantile r T , α r_{T,\alpha} is deterministic once the prior, the sampling rule, T T , and α \alpha are fixed. For the purpose of implementation, it can be estimated offline by Monte Carlo simulation from the prior. A large- T T , small- α \alpha approximation is r T , α asy = 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 ​ α 2 ​ T . r_{T,\alpha}^{\mathrm{asy}}=\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}\alpha^{2}T. 2.2 Upper Bound Analysis We now derive theoretical guarantees for PGWS . In this subsection, all probabilities are under the joint Bayesian law induced by Algorithm 1 . Theorem 2.1 (Upper Bound for PGWS ) . For any T T and any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , PGWS ( α \alpha ) with the terminal rule ( 2.6 ) satisfies 𝒜 T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) = α \mathcal{A}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))=\alpha . Furthermore, asymptotically, lim sup α ↓ 0 lim sup T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ − 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 . \limsup_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))\leq-\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}. (2.7) The proof uses two key lemmas. In particular, the first lemma gives a finite-sample posterior certification bound: conditional on the sampled arms and observed rewards up to time step T T , the statistic R T R_{T} controls the posterior probability that the final leader is not the true best arm. Lemma 2.1 (Posterior Certification Error Bound) . For every T T and every α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ ( K − 1 ) ​ e − r T , α . \mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))\leq(K-1)e^{-r_{T,\alpha}}. Lemma 2.2 (Asymptotic Lower Bound on the Calibration Threshold) . For every ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \varepsilon\in(0,1) , there exists α 0 &gt; 0 \alpha_{0}&gt;0 such that for every 0 &lt; α ≤ α 0 0&lt;\alpha\leq\alpha_{0} , there exists T 0 ​ ( α , ε ) T_{0}(\alpha,\varepsilon) such that for all T ≥ T 0 ​ ( α , ε ) T\geq T_{0}(\alpha,\varepsilon) , r T , α ≥ ( 1 − ε ) ​ 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 ​ α 2 ​ T . r_{T,\alpha}\geq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}\alpha^{2}T. The proofs of both Lemmas are deferred to Appendix B . Proof of Theorem 2.1 . By the definition of the lower α \alpha -quantile, ℙ ⁡ ( R T &lt; r T , α ) ≤ α ≤ ℙ ⁡ ( R T ≤ r T , α ) . \mathbb{P}(R_{T}&lt;r_{T,\alpha})\leq\alpha\leq\mathbb{P}(R_{T}\leq r_{T,\alpha}). The boundary randomization in ( 2.6 ) is chosen so that ℙ ⁡ ( a ^ T = ? ) = ℙ ⁡ ( R T &lt; r T , α ) + θ T , α ​ ℙ ​ ( R T = r T , α ) = α . \mathbb{P}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?)=\mathbb{P}(R_{T}&lt;r_{T,\alpha})+\theta_{T,\alpha}\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})=\alpha. Thus 𝒜 T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) = α \mathcal{A}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))=\alpha . For the error probability, Lemma 2.1 gives ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ ( K − 1 ) ​ e − r T , α . \mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))\leq(K-1)e^{-r_{T,\alpha}}. By Lemma 2.2 , for every ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \varepsilon\in(0,1) , all sufficiently small α \alpha , and all sufficiently large T T , r T , α ≥ ( 1 − ε ) ​ 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 ​ α 2 ​ T . r_{T,\alpha}\geq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}\alpha^{2}T. Therefore ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ ( K − 1 ) ​ exp ⁡ { − ( 1 − ε ) ​ 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 ​ α 2 ​ T } . \mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))\leq(K-1)\exp\left\{-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}\alpha^{2}T\right\}. This then yields the final result in ( 2.7 ). ∎ 3 Lower Bounds This section presents three lower bounds. The first is the main impossibility result for Bayesian BAI with abstention and matches the upper bound presented in Section 2.2 . The second shows that forced-decision Bayesian BAI has only polynomial decay in the error probability, which refines the lower bound established by 2 . The third considers the frequentist (non-Bayesian) formulation and shows that, when the instance has a positive gap, abstention improves only lower-order exponential terms. Together, these results identify both the value of abstention and the role of the Bayesian formulation. 3.1 Bayesian BAI with Abstention Theorem 3.1 (Lower Bound for Bayesian BAI with Abstention) . In the Gaussian prior and Gaussian reward model, lim inf α ↓ 0 lim inf T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ 𝔈 T ​ ( α ) ≥ − 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 . \liminf_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathfrak{E}_{T}(\alpha)\geq-\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}. Theorem 3.1 applies to every adaptive sampling and terminal decision rule satisfying the abstention constraint 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha . The proof, which is deferred to Appendix C , proceeds by decomposing the parameter space into disjoint regions Ω i ​ j \Omega_{ij} where arms i i and j j are the two arms with the largest means. Since the undetected error is dominated by near-tie instances, we focus on small neighborhoods of the tie surfaces inside these regions and introduce a locally flat subprior that is a submeasure of the original prior. This reduction transforms the problem into a two-arm hypothesis testing problem under a convenient approximately uniform prior. Combining a Neyman–Pearson-like argument with the aggregation over all pairs yields the exponent 1 / ( 8 ​ κ ν 2 ) 1/(8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}) that matches that of the upper bound. Combining Theorem 3.1 with the achievability guarantee for PGWS gives the tight asymptotics on the exponential scale: Corollary 3.2 (Tight Asymptotics and Optimality of PGWS ) . The following holds: lim sup α ↓ 0 lim sup T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T ​ ( PGWS ​ ( α ) ) = lim inf α ↓ 0 lim inf T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ 𝔈 T ​ ( α ) = − 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 . \limsup_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}({\textnormal{{PGWS}}}(\alpha))=\liminf_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathfrak{E}_{T}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}. (3.1) Corollary 3.2 identifies κ ν \kappa_{\nu} , the density at zero of the prior top-two gap Γ \Gamma , as the prior-dependent hardness parameter on the leading exponential scale. 3.2 Bayesian BAI without Abstention To characterize the role of the abstention formulation, we next consider the forced-decision Bayesian BAI problem ( 2 ) , in which the learner must recommend an arm at the end of the experiment. Recall that we defined the forced-decision Bayes error probability as in ( 1.3 ). The following theorem shows that, without abstention, the Bayes probability of misidentification cannot decay exponentially fast. Theorem 3.3 (No-Abstention Lower Bound) . For the Gaussian prior and Gaussian reward model, lim inf T → ∞ T ​ 𝔈 T ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 2 π ​ κ ν . \liminf_{T\to\infty}\sqrt{T}\,\mathfrak{E}_{T}(0)\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\kappa_{\nu}. The proof is deferred to Appendix D . Theorem 3.3 refines the two-arm Bayesian fixed-budget BAI lower bound of 2 . They show that, for K = 2 K=2 Gaussian arms with Gaussian priors, the Bayes probability of misidentification is Ω ( ( T log T ) − 1 / 2 ) \Omega((T\log T)^{-1/2}) , with the multiplicative factor depending on the prior as exp ⁡ { − ( ν 1 − ν 2 ) 2 4 ​ σ 0 2 } \exp\big\{\!-\!\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{4\sigma_{0}^{2}}\big\} . For K = 2 K=2 , our hardness parameter is κ ν = 2 ​ ∫ ℝ f 1 ​ ( x ) ​ f 2 ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = 1 σ 0 ​ π ​ exp ⁡ { − ( ν 1 − ν 2 ) 2 4 ​ σ 0 2 } \kappa_{\nu}=2\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(x)f_{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}\sqrt{\pi}}\exp\big\{\!-\!\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{4\sigma_{0}^{2}}\big\} . Thus Theorem 3.3 improves the dependence on T T , recovers the same prior-separation dependence through κ ν \kappa_{\nu} , and extends the result from two arms to general K ≥ 2 K\geq 2 . For K &gt; 2 K&gt;2 , κ ν \kappa_{\nu} represents a weighted sum (with weights { w i ​ j } \{w_{ij}\} ) over all distinct pairs of arms ( i , j ) (i,j) of the likelihood of i i and j j being equal. Additionally, this theorem is also materially different from the lower bound analysis of the Bayesian simple regret in 18 . That work studies the simple regret , rather than the probability of error, and establishes a decay rate of Θ ⁡ ( T − 1 ) \Theta(T^{-1}) . Theorem 3.3 identifies the mechanism behind the polynomial Bayes error probability in standard forced-decision Bayesian FB-BAI. The dominant error comes from instances whose top-two gap, Γ = μ ( 1 ) − μ ( 2 ) \Gamma=\mu_{(1)}-\mu_{(2)} , is of order T − 1 / 2 T^{-1/2} . The prior probability of these instances is of order κ ν / T \kappa_{\nu}/\sqrt{T} , and on these instances, the two leading arms cannot be reliably separated with T T samples. Thus the same local near-tie density κ ν \kappa_{\nu} that governs the abstention exponent also determines the constant in the forced-decision problem. This contrasts with the results for Bayesian BAI with abstention in Theorem 3.1 . By allowing an α \alpha -fraction of inconclusive decisions on the most ambiguous posterior histories, the learner removes the near-tie region that dominates the forced-decision error probability. Consequently, the error probability exhibits a fundamental phase transition from polynomial decay (i.e., Ω ( T − 1 / 2 ) \Omega(T^{-1/2}) ) to exponential decay (i.e., exp ⁡ ( − Θ ⁡ ( α 2 ​ T ) ) \exp(-\Theta(\alpha^{2}T)) ). 3.3 Frequentist BAI with Abstention The preceding results concern the Bayesian formulation, where the error probability is averaged over a prior. We now contrast this behavior with the frequentist (fixed-instance) setting, and we show that the polynomial-to-exponential improvement is specific to the Bayesian formulation. In particular, for a fixed positive-gap instance, the error probability of ordinary frequentist FB-BAI decays exponentially with T T ( 3 ; 6 ; 17 ) . We show that the terminal abstention can improve only lower-order (i.e., sublinear in T T ) terms in the exponent. We exemplify this point with a symmetric pair of two-arm Gaussian instances which we call the mirror experiments . Pulling arm i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} produces an independent reward from 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ i , 1 ) \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},1) . A strategy π \pi adaptively samples the arms for T T rounds and then outputs a ^ T ∈ { 1 , 2 , ? } \widehat{a}_{T}\in\{1,2,?\} , where ? ? denotes abstention. For an instance μ \mu , let a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) = arg ⁡ max i ∈ { 1 , 2 } ⁡ μ i a^{\star}(\mu)=\arg\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\mu_{i} be the optimal arm, and define ℰ T , μ ​ ( π ) := ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) , ? } ) , and 𝒜 T , μ ​ ( π ) := ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) . \mathcal{E}_{T,\mu}(\pi):=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}\!\left(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(\mu),?\}\right),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{A}_{T,\mu}(\pi):=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?). For α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , let Π T freq ​ ( α ) := { π : sup μ ∈ ℝ 2 𝒜 T , μ ​ ( π ) ≤ α } \Pi_{T}^{\rm freq}(\alpha):=\left\{\pi:\sup_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{A}_{T,\mu}(\pi)\leq\alpha\right\} be the class of strategies with abstention probability uniformly bounded above by α \alpha . Let z α := Φ − 1 ​ ( 1 + α 2 ) z_{\alpha}:=\Phi^{-1}\!\left(\frac{1+\alpha}{2}\right) , so that ℙ ⁡ ( | Z | ≤ z α ) = α \mathbb{P}(|Z|\leq z_{\alpha})=\alpha for Z ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1) . Fix x ∈ ℝ x\in\mathbb{R} and Δ &gt; 0 \Delta&gt;0 , and consider the following two mirror instances μ + := ( x + Δ 2 , x − Δ 2 ) , and μ − := ( x − Δ 2 , x + Δ 2 ) . \mu^{+}:=\left(x+\frac{\Delta}{2},x-\frac{\Delta}{2}\right),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu^{-}:=\left(x-\frac{\Delta}{2},x+\frac{\Delta}{2}\right). Under μ + \mu^{+} , arm 1 1 is optimal; under μ − \mu^{-} , arm 2 2 is optimal. Theorem 3.4 (Two-Arm Frequentist Lower Bound with Abstention) . Given T ∈ ℕ T\in\mathbb{N} , α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , x ∈ ℝ x\in\mathbb{R} , Δ &gt; 0 \Delta&gt;0 , and any strategy π ∈ Π T freq ​ ( α ) \pi\in\Pi_{T}^{\rm freq}(\alpha) , we have max ⁡ { ℰ T , μ + ​ ( π ) , ℰ T , μ − ​ ( π ) } ≥ Φ ⁡ ( − Δ ​ T 2 − z α ) . \max\left\{\mathcal{E}_{T,\mu^{+}}(\pi),\mathcal{E}_{T,\mu^{-}}(\pi)\right\}\geq\Phi\!\left(-\frac{\Delta\sqrt{T}}{2}-z_{\alpha}\right). The proof is deferred to Appendix E . The theorem is a minimax statement over the mirror instances ( μ + , μ − ) (\mu^{+},\mu^{-}) : any strategy whose abstention probability is uniformly bounded by α \alpha necessarily incurs the stated undetected error on at least one of the two instances. This bound is sharp for the two-arm Gaussian mirror experiment: when T T is even, sampling each arm T / 2 T/2 times and abstaining when | μ ^ 1 − μ ^ 2 | ≤ 2 ​ z α T |\widehat{\mu}_{1}-\widehat{\mu}_{2}|\leq\frac{2z_{\alpha}}{\sqrt{T}} attains the lower bound. Theorem 3.4 shows that abstention does not change the leading fixed-gap exponential rate. Without abstention, the policy that pulls each arm an equal number of times results in the error probability Φ ⁡ ( − Δ ​ T 2 ) = exp ⁡ { − Δ 2 8 ​ T + O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) } . \Phi\!\left(-\frac{\Delta\sqrt{T}}{2}\right)=\exp\!\left\{-\frac{\Delta^{2}}{8}T+O(\log T)\right\}. With an abstention budget α \alpha , the undetected error probability becomes Φ ⁡ ( − Δ ​ T 2 − z α ) = exp ⁡ { − Δ 2 8 ​ T − Δ ​ z α 2 ​ T − z α 2 2 + O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) } . \Phi\!\left(-\frac{\Delta\sqrt{T}}{2}-z_{\alpha}\right)=\exp\!\left\{-\frac{\Delta^{2}}{8}T-\frac{\Delta z_{\alpha}}{2}\sqrt{T}-\frac{z_{\alpha}^{2}}{2}+O(\log T)\right\}. Hence, abstention only improves the exponent by a lower order term. For small α \alpha , z α = π / 2 ​ α + O ⁡ ( α 3 ) z_{\alpha}=\sqrt{\pi/2}\,\alpha+O(\alpha^{3}) , so the logarithmic improvement is of order O ⁡ ( α ​ T ) O(\alpha\sqrt{T}) . In contrast to the Bayesian formulation, the error probability under the frequentist setup is already decaying exponentially without abstention, and the additional option to abstain yields only a lower-order improvement in the error probability, characterized by a moderate-deviation term. 4 Extensions Beyond the Gaussian Model In the preceding sections, we established the polynomial-to-exponential phase transition under the Gaussian model. In this section, we show that this transition extends beyond the Gaussian setting. Specifically, the same exponent arises for regular one-parameter reward models after measuring gaps in the Fisher–Rao information scale. Let ℐ ⊆ ℝ \mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R} be an open interval, called the quality parameter space . At the beginning of each experiment, the quality parameters μ 1 , … , μ K ∈ ℐ \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\in\mathcal{I} are drawn from their prior distributions and remain fixed throughout the experiment. The best arm is a ⋆ := arg ⁡ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ μ i a^{\star}:=\arg\max_{i\in[K]}\mu_{i} . Conditional on μ 1 , … , μ K \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K} , each pull of arm i i produces an independent reward with distribution P μ i P_{\mu_{i}} . We assume that each P μ i P_{\mu_{i}} comes from a regular one-parameter exponential family, defined as follows. Assumption 4.1 (Regular One-Parameter Exponential Family) . The family { P μ : μ ∈ ℐ } \{P_{\mu}:\mu\in\mathcal{I}\} is a regular full one-parameter exponential family, possibly after a smooth monotone reparameterization (which we call θ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \theta(\cdot) ); see, e.g., ( 20 , Sec. 34.3.1) . Specifically, there exists an open natural parameter space Θ ⊆ ℝ \Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R} , a dominating measure λ \lambda , a natural statistic t ⁡ ( ⋅ ) t(\cdot) , a base distribution h ⁡ ( ⋅ ) h(\cdot) , a C 4 C^{4} log-partition function A : Θ → ℝ A:\Theta\to\mathbb{R} with A ′′ ​ ( θ ) &gt; 0 A^{\prime\prime}(\theta)&gt;0 , and a C 4 C^{4} map θ : ℐ → Θ \theta:\mathcal{I}\to\Theta onto Θ \Theta with θ ′ ​ ( μ ) &gt; 0 \theta^{\prime}(\mu)&gt;0 such that θ ⁡ ( μ ) \theta(\mu) is the natural parameter corresponding to the quality parameter μ \mu , and d ​ P μ d ​ λ ​ ( w ) = h ⁡ ( w ) ​ exp ​ { θ ⁡ ( μ ) ​ t ​ ( w ) − A ⁡ ( θ ⁡ ( μ ) ) } , μ ∈ ℐ . \frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}(w)=h(w)\exp\big\{\theta(\mu)t(w)-A(\theta(\mu))\big\},\qquad\mu\in\mathcal{I}. Moreover, the image of every compact subset of ℐ \mathcal{I} under θ \theta is compact in Θ \Theta . Under Assumption 4.1 , the Fisher information for the quality parameter μ \mu is I ⁡ ( μ ) := A ′′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ ) ) ​ ( θ ′ ​ ( μ ) ) 2 . I(\mu):=A^{\prime\prime}(\theta(\mu))\big(\theta^{\prime}(\mu)\big)^{2}. We then define the Fisher–Rao information coordinate s ⁡ ( μ ) := ∫ μ ∘ μ I ⁡ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v , s(\mu):=\int_{\mu_{\circ}}^{\mu}\sqrt{I(v)}\,\mathrm{d}v, (4.1) where μ ∘ ∈ ℐ \mu_{\circ}\in\mathcal{I} is arbitrary and all quantities and results below are independent of the choice of μ ∘ \mu_{\circ} , as changing μ ∘ \mu_{\circ} shifts s ⁡ ( ⋅ ) s(\cdot) by an additive constant, leaving κ \kappa and gap-based results unchanged. This is the arc-length coordinate induced by the Fisher–Rao metric ( 25 ; 1 ) . Since s ⁡ ( ⋅ ) s(\cdot) is strictly increasing, the best arm is unchanged by the transformation μ i ↦ η i := s ⁡ ( μ i ) \mu_{i}\mapsto\eta_{i}:=s(\mu_{i}) . Assumption 4.2 (Regular Prior in Information Coordinates) . The arms’ quality parameters μ 1 , … , μ K \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K} are independent under the prior. In the information coordinate η i = s ⁡ ( μ i ) \eta_{i}=s(\mu_{i}) , the induced prior distribution of arm i i has density f ¯ i \bar{f}_{i} and distribution function F ¯ i \bar{F}_{i} on ℋ := s ⁡ ( ℐ ) \mathcal{H}:=s(\mathcal{I}) . For each i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , f ¯ i ​ is continuous and bounded on ​ ℋ , f ¯ i ​ ( η ) &gt; 0 ∀ η ∈ ℋ . \bar{f}_{i}\text{ is continuous and bounded on }\mathcal{H},\qquad\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)&gt;0\quad\forall\,\eta\in\mathcal{H}. Define the information-scaled top-two tie density κ := ∑ i ≠ j ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F ¯ k ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \kappa:=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{F}_{k}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u. Equivalently, if f i f_{i} and F i F_{i} respectively denote the prior density and distribution function of μ i \mu_{i} in the original coordinate system, then κ = ∑ i ≠ j ∫ ℐ f i ​ ( μ ) ​ f j ​ ( μ ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F k ​ ( μ ) I ⁡ ( μ ) ​ 𝑑 μ . \kappa=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{I}}\frac{f_{i}(\mu)f_{j}(\mu)\prod_{k\neq i,j}F_{k}(\mu)}{\sqrt{I(\mu)}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu. Thus κ \kappa is the density at zero of the top-two gap after transforming the quality parameters to Fisher–Rao coordinates via ( 4.1 ). In the Gaussian model with unit variance, θ ⁡ ( μ ) ≡ μ \theta(\mu)\equiv\mu and I ⁡ ( μ ) ≡ 1 I(\mu)\equiv 1 , so this reduces to the top-two gap density used in the preceding sections. For the achievability result we also assume a global quadratic lower bound on the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence. Let D ( μ , z ) := D ( P μ ∥ P z ) . D(\mu,z):=D(P_{\mu}\,\|\,P_{z}). Assumption 4.3 (Quadratic KL Growth in the Fisher–Rao Information Coordinate) . There exists c KL &gt; 0 c_{\rm KL}&gt;0 such that, for all μ , z ∈ ℐ \mu,z\in\mathcal{I} , D ⁡ ( μ , z ) ≥ c KL ​ ( s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) ) 2 . D(\mu,z)\geq c_{\rm KL}\big(s(\mu)-s(z)\big)^{2}. (4.2) Assumption 4.3 holds for several one-parameter exponential families when s ⁡ ( ⋅ ) s(\cdot) is the Fisher–Rao information coordinate. In particular, Gaussians with unit variance, Bernoulli, and Poisson satisfy ( 4.2 ) with c KL = 1 / 4 c_{\rm KL}=1/4 . Assumption 4.3 can be viewed as a global version of the local quadratic approximation D ⁡ ( μ , z ) ≈ 1 2 ​ ( s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) ) 2 D(\mu,z)\approx\frac{1}{2}\big(s(\mu)-s(z)\big)^{2} where | s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) | |s(\mu)-s(z)| is the Fisher–Rao distance ( 1 ) . Theorem 4.1 (Extension to General Distributions) . Suppose Assumptions 4.1 – 4.3 hold, then lim sup α ↓ 0 lim sup T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T ​ ( IC-PGWS ​ ( α ) ) = lim inf α ↓ 0 lim inf T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ 𝔈 T ​ ( α ) = − 1 8 ​ κ 2 . \limsup_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}(\text{{IC-PGWS}}(\alpha))=\liminf_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathfrak{E}_{T}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{8\kappa^{2}}. The lower bound holds for all adaptive policies satisfying the abstention constraint, and the upper bound is attained by an information-coordinate version of PGWS , namely, IC-PGWS . The proof is postponed to Appendix F . We now provide a concrete example of applying Theorem 4.1 to Beta-Bernoulli arms. Example 4.2 (Beta–Bernoulli arms) . Consider Bernoulli rewards with unknown success probabilities μ i ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \mu_{i}\in(0,1) that follow Beta distributions, i.e., Y i , s | μ i ∼ Bernoulli ⁡ ( μ i ) , and μ i ∼ Beta ⁡ ( a i , b i ) , Y_{i,s}\mid\mu_{i}\sim{\rm Bernoulli}(\mu_{i}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu_{i}\sim{\rm Beta}(a_{i},b_{i}), independently across arms and time, where a i , b i ≥ 1 / 2 a_{i},b_{i}\geq 1/2 for i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] . We show that the Beta–Bernoulli arms satisfy Assumptions 4.1 – 4.3 . Firstly, the Bernoulli family is a regular exponential family with θ ( μ ) = log μ 1 − μ , A ( θ ) = log ( 1 + e θ ) , and t ( w ) = w . \theta(\mu)=\log\frac{\mu}{1-\mu},\quad A(\theta)=\log(1+e^{\theta}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad t(w)=w. Its Fisher information is I ⁡ ( μ ) = 1 μ ⁡ ( 1 − μ ) I(\mu)=\frac{1}{\mu(1-\mu)} . Hence, s ⁡ ( μ ) = ∫ 0 μ I ⁡ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v = 2 ​ arcsin ⁡ μ s(\mu)=\int_{0}^{\mu}\sqrt{I(v)}\,\mathrm{d}v=2\arcsin\sqrt{\mu} , and ℋ = ( 0 , π ) \mathcal{H}=(0,\pi) . Secondly, if η = s ⁡ ( μ ) \eta=s(\mu) , then μ = sin 2 ⁡ ( η / 2 ) \mu=\sin^{2}(\eta/2) , and the prior density of η i \eta_{i} is f ¯ i ​ ( η ) = sin 2 ​ a i − 1 ⁡ ( η / 2 ) ​ cos 2 ​ b i − 1 ⁡ ( η / 2 ) Beta ⁡ ( a i , b i ) , 0 &lt; η &lt; π . \bar{f}_{i}(\eta)=\frac{\sin^{2a_{i}-1}(\eta/2)\cos^{2b_{i}-1}(\eta/2)}{{\rm Beta}(a_{i},b_{i})},\qquad 0&lt;\eta&lt;\pi. Because a i , b i ≥ 1 / 2 a_{i},b_{i}\geq 1/2 , this density is bounded, continuous, and strictly positive on ( 0 , π ) (0,\pi) . Hence, Assumption 4.2 holds. Lastly, a routine calculation shows that the Bernoulli KL divergence satisfies D ⁡ ( μ , z ) ≥ 1 4 ​ ( s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) ) 2 , μ , z ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , D(\mu,z)\geq\frac{1}{4}\big(s(\mu)-s(z)\big)^{2},\qquad\mu,z\in(0,1), so Assumption 4.3 also holds with c KL = 1 / 4 c_{\rm KL}=1/4 . Therefore, the conditions of Theorem 4.1 hold and the theorem applies. Define κ BB := ∑ i ≠ j ∫ 0 π f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F ¯ k ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u = ∑ i ≠ j ∫ 0 1 f i ​ ( μ ) ​ f j ​ ( μ ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F k ​ ( μ ) ​ μ ⁡ ( 1 − μ ) ​ 𝑑 μ \kappa_{\rm BB}:=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{0}^{\pi}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{F}_{k}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{0}^{1}f_{i}(\mu)f_{j}(\mu)\prod_{k\neq i,j}F_{k}(\mu)\sqrt{\mu(1-\mu)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu where F ¯ i \bar{F}_{i} is the cumulative distribution function of 2 ​ arcsin ⁡ μ i 2\arcsin\sqrt{\mu_{i}} , and f i f_{i} and F i F_{i} are, respectively, the Beta ( a i , b i ) (a_{i},b_{i}) density and CDF. So by Theorem 4.1 , we have lim sup α ↓ 0 lim sup T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T BB ​ ( IC-PGWS ​ ( α ) ) = lim inf α ↓ 0 lim inf T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ 𝔈 T BB ​ ( α ) = − 1 8 ​ κ BB 2 . \limsup_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\rm BB}(\text{{IC-PGWS}}(\alpha))=\liminf_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathfrak{E}_{T}^{\rm BB}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{8\kappa^{2}_{\rm{BB}}}. in the Beta-Bernoulli case. 5 Numerical Experiments We conduct numerical experiments to illustrate the benefit brought by the abstention option and the superior design of the sampling rule in PGWS . First, we evaluate the practical benefit of abstention by comparing the undetected error probability for several abstention levels. Second, we isolate the value of the PGWS allocation by comparing it with uniform allocation under the same posterior abstention threshold. 5.1 Experimental Setup We adopt the Gaussian prior and reward model from Section 1.2 . The prior is μ i ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( ν i , σ 0 2 ) \mu_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\nu_{i},\sigma_{0}^{2}) for i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , independently across arms, and rewards satisfy Y i , s | μ i ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ i , 1 ) Y_{i,s}\mid\mu_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},1) . We consider the arm prior with K = 5 K=5 , σ 0 = 1 \sigma_{0}=1 , and ν = [ 5 , 5 , 3 , 3 , 2 ] \nu=[5,5,3,3,2] . The following methods are involved in the comparison: • PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) : PGWS with the quantile-calibrated posterior abstention threshold r ^ T , α \widehat{r}_{T,\alpha} , so that the abstention probability is α \alpha . • Unif ( α ) (\alpha) : Uniform allocation, followed by recommending the posterior leader. Use the same terminal statistic and calibration procedure as PGWS . • BayesElim ( 2 ) : Bayesian elimination without abstention, used as a forced-decision Bayesian BAI baseline. For PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) and Unif ( α ) (\alpha) , the abstention threshold is calibrated separately for each sampling rule, budget T T , so that the abstention probability is upper bounded by α \alpha . Specifically, the abstention threshold is the lower α \alpha -quantile r T , α r_{T,\alpha} defined in ( 2.4 ). For PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) , for each given budget T T and abstention level α \alpha , we apply the given sampling rule and generate terminal statistic samples { R T ( m ) } m ∈ [ M cal ] \{R_{T}^{(m)}\}_{m\in[M_{\rm cal}]} of independent M cal = 10 5 M_{\rm cal}=10^{5} simulations from the prior. We approximate the abstention threshold r T , α r_{T,\alpha} by its empirical estimate r ^ T , α := min { r : 1 M cal ∑ m = 1 M cal 𝟙 { R T ( m ) ≤ r } ≥ α } . \widehat{r}_{T,\alpha}:=\min\left\{r:\frac{1}{M_{\rm cal}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{\rm cal}}\mathbbm{1}\{R_{T}^{(m)}\leq r\}\geq\alpha\right\}. For α = 0 \alpha=0 , there is no abstention and the algorithm always recommends the posterior mean leader. The performance is evaluated on an independent test sample of M test = 10 6 M_{\rm test}=10^{6} prior-generated experiments. For each method, budget T T , and abstention level α \alpha , we compute the empirical abstention probability and empirical undetected error probability as follows: 𝒜 ^ T := 1 M test ∑ m = 1 M test 𝟙 { a ^ T ( m ) = ? } , and ℰ ^ T := 1 M test ∑ m = 1 M test 𝟙 { a ^ T ( m ) ∉ { a ⋆ , ( m ) , ? } } , \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{T}:=\frac{1}{M_{\rm test}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{\rm test}}\mathbbm{1}\big\{\widehat{a}_{T}^{(m)}=?\big\},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{T}:=\frac{1}{M_{\rm test}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{\rm test}}\mathbbm{1}\big\{\widehat{a}_{T}^{(m)}\notin\{a^{\star,(m)},?\}\big\}, where a ⋆ , ( m ) a^{\star,(m)} denotes the true best arm in the m m th prior-generated experiment, and a ^ T ( m ) ∈ [ K ] ∪ { ? } \widehat{a}_{T}^{(m)}\in[K]\cup\{?\} is the terminal output of the algorithm. 5.2 Effect of Abstention In the first experiment, we aim to demonstrate the value of abstention. We run PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) for α ∈ { 0 , 0.01 , 0.03 , 0.05 } \alpha\in\{0,0.01,0.03,0.05\} over a range of budgets T T , and report the empirical undetected error probability ℰ ^ T \widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{T} . The empirical abstention rate 𝒜 ^ T \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{T} is reported separately to verify the correctness of threshold calibration. The results are shown in Figure 1 : • Significant reduction in undetected error probability (Figure 1 (a)): The forced-decision baselines ( PGWS ( 0 ) (0) and BayesElim ( 2 ) ) exhibit a significantly slower decay in the error probability compared to the case where abstention is allowed, i.e., PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) for positive α \alpha . This corroborates our main result in Theorem 2.1 : spending a small fraction of inconclusive decisions on the most ambiguous instances yields an exponential reduction in terminal misidentifications. In addition, Figure 1 (a) also shows that the curves for PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) when α \alpha is positive are approximately affine, demonstrating exponential decay in the undetected error probability. • Abstention calibration (Figure 1 (b)): Across all budgets T T , PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) adheres to the prescribed abstention targets without being overly conservative. This validates the practical reliability of our calibrated abstention threshold. (a) ℰ ^ T \widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{T} against T T (b) 𝒜 ^ T \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{T} against T T Figure 1 : Plots of the undetected and abstention probabilities as functions of the budget T T 5.3 Effect of Adaptive Sampling in PGWS The second experiment illustrates the effectiveness of the sampling strategy of PGWS as in ( 2.2 ), which adapts to the instance. We compare PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) with uniform allocation Unif ( α ) (\alpha) for the abstention levels α ∈ { 0 , 0.01 , 0.05 } \alpha\in\{0,0.01,0.05\} , and the results are shown in Figure 2 . We observe that for every abstention level, PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) achieves a significantly lower undetected error probability than Unif ( α ) (\alpha) across all budgets. The performance gap highlights the practical superiority of our algorithm’s adaptive sampling design. By dynamically concentrating the sampling budget on the posterior mean leader and its most relevant challengers, rather than wasting pulls uniformly across all arms, the algorithm more efficiently resolves the ambiguous near-tie instances that dominate the Bayes error probability. Figure 2 : Comparison of PGWS to the algorithm which samples arms uniformly at random. 6 Conclusion In this paper, we studied Bayesian FB-BAI with an abstention option. We characterized the sharp small-abstention, large-budget exponent: 𝔈 T ​ ( α ) = exp ⁡ { − α 2 ​ T 8 ​ κ ν 2 + o ⁡ ( α 2 ​ T ) } . \mathfrak{E}_{T}(\alpha)=\exp\!\left\{-\frac{\alpha^{2}T}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}+o(\alpha^{2}T)\right\}. The exponent is governed by the density κ ν \kappa_{\nu} of the prior top-two gap at zero. We proposed PGWS , an adaptive algorithm that allocates samples to the posterior mean leader and its challengers. PGWS samples arms according to the empirical allocation distribution which is proportional to the reciprocal of the squared posterior mean gaps. It achieves the same exponent as the information-theoretic lower bound. Hence it is asymptotically optimal on the α 2 ​ T \alpha^{2}T logarithmic scale. Numerical experiments support the practicality of our theoretical findings. Our results clarify the benefits of abstention. Without abstention, Bayesian BAI has only a polynomial error decay, driven by prior probability on instances with top-two gap of order T − 1 / 2 T^{-1/2} . With abstention, the learner can spend its abstention budget on these near-tie instances, leading to exponentially decaying undetected error. In contrast, in the frequentist fixed-gap setting, ordinary BAI is already exponentially accurate, and abstention improves only lower-order terms in the exponent. Finally, we showed that the same mechanism extends beyond the Gaussian model under continuous prior, one parameter exponential family reward, and quadratic KL growth conditions, with the Gaussian top-two gap density replaced by an information-scaled top-two gap density. Lemma B.2 (Posterior Consistency) . For every arm i i , M i ​ ( t ) → μ i almost surely . M_{i}(t)\to\mu_{i}\qquad\text{almost surely}. (B.2) Proof. First, recall that the algorithm’s forced-exploration samples arm i i whenever N i ​ ( t ) &lt; t + 1 N_{i}(t)&lt;\sqrt{t+1} , this guarantees that as t → ∞ t\to\infty , N i ​ ( t ) → ∞ N_{i}(t)\to\infty for every i i . Second, conditional on a specific realization of the true mean μ i \mu_{i} , the reward sequence ( Y i , s ) s ≥ 1 (Y_{i,s})_{s\geq 1} consists of mutually independent draws from 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ i , 1 ) \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},1) . Therefore, by the Strong Law of Large Numbers, the conditional probability of the empirical average converging to μ i \mu_{i} is 1 1 . Integrating this over the prior of μ i \mu_{i} , we have 1 N i ​ ( t ) ​ ∑ s = 1 N i ​ ( t ) Y i , s → μ i almost surely . \frac{1}{N_{i}(t)}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{i}(t)}Y_{i,s}\to\mu_{i}\qquad\text{almost surely}. Finally, recall our algorithm’s update for the posterior mean: M i ​ ( t ) = σ 0 − 2 ​ ν i + ∑ s = 1 N i ​ ( t ) Y i , s σ 0 − 2 + N i ​ ( t ) . M_{i}(t)=\frac{\sigma_{0}^{-2}\nu_{i}+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{i}(t)}Y_{i,s}}{\sigma_{0}^{-2}+N_{i}(t)}. Combining the previous two displayed equations yields ( B.2 ). ∎ Next, we show that the empirical allocation of arm pulls converges to the sampling proportions. Lemma B.3 (Convergence of Empirical Sampling Proportions) . For every arm i i , N i ​ ( T ) T → p i † ​ ( μ ) almost surely . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\mu)\qquad\text{almost surely}. (B.3) Proof. Fix an arm i i . We proceed in four steps: Step 1: Forced exploration is negligible. Let F i ​ ( T ) F_{i}(T) be the number of forced-exploration pulls of arm i i up to time T T . If the m m -th forced pull of arm i i occurs before time T T , then immediately before that pull, N i ​ ( t ) ≥ m − 1 N_{i}(t)\geq m-1 . But a forced pull of arm i i can occur only if N i ​ ( t ) &lt; t + 1 ≤ T + 1 N_{i}(t)&lt;\sqrt{t+1}\leq\sqrt{T+1} . Hence m − 1 &lt; T + 1 m-1&lt;\sqrt{T+1} , and therefore F i ​ ( T ) ≤ ⌈ T + 1 ⌉ + 1 F_{i}(T)\leq\lceil\sqrt{T+1}\rceil+1 . Consequently, F i ​ ( T ) T → 0 . \frac{F_{i}(T)}{T}\to 0. Let F ⁡ ( T ) F(T) be the total number of forced-exploration rounds up to time T T . Since F ⁡ ( T ) = ∑ ℓ = 1 K F ℓ ​ ( T ) F(T)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}F_{\ell}(T) , we also have F ⁡ ( T ) T ≤ K ⁡ ( ⌈ T + 1 ⌉ + 1 ) T → 0 . \frac{F(T)}{T}\leq\frac{K(\lceil\sqrt{T+1}\rceil+1)}{T}\to 0. Step 2: Martingale concentration on non-forced rounds. Let ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} be the history before round t + 1 t+1 , and define B t := 𝟙 ​ { round ​ t + 1 ​ is non-forced } B_{t}:=\mathbbm{1}\{\text{round }t+1\text{ is non-forced}\} . The event { B t = 1 } \{B_{t}=1\} is ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} -measurable. On non-forced rounds, the algorithm samples arm i i with conditional probability p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) . Define D i , t + 1 := B t ( 𝟙 { A t + 1 = i } − p i ( t ) ) D_{i,t+1}:=B_{t}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t+1}=i\}-p_{i}(t)\bigr) . Then 𝔼 ⁡ [ D i , t + 1 ∣ ℱ t ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[D_{i,t+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]=0 , so ( D i , t + 1 ) t ≥ 0 (D_{i,t+1})_{t\geq 0} is a martingale difference sequence. Also, | D i , t + 1 | ≤ 1 |D_{i,t+1}|\leq 1 . Therefore, by Azuma’s inequality, for every ε &gt; 0 \varepsilon&gt;0 , ℙ ⁡ ( | 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 D i , t + 1 | ≥ ε ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − ε 2 ​ T 2 ) . \mathbb{P}\left(\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}D_{i,t+1}\bigg|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}T}{2}\right). The right-hand side is summable in T T . Hence, by the Borel–Cantelli lemma, 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 D i , t + 1 → 0 almost surely . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}D_{i,t+1}\to 0\qquad\text{almost surely}. Step 3: Decomposition of the empirical pull count. The total number of pulls of arm i i can be decomposed as N i ( T ) = F i ( T ) + ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t 𝟙 { A t + 1 = i } . N_{i}(T)=F_{i}(T)+\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t+1}=i\}. Using the definition of D i , t + 1 D_{i,t+1} , we have B t 𝟙 { A t + 1 = i } = D i , t + 1 + B t p i ( t ) B_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t+1}=i\}=D_{i,t+1}+B_{t}p_{i}(t) . Thus N i ​ ( T ) T = F i ​ ( T ) T + 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 D i , t + 1 + 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}=\frac{F_{i}(T)}{T}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}D_{i,t+1}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t). By Steps 1 and 2, N i ​ ( T ) T − 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) → 0 almost surely . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t)\to 0\qquad\text{almost surely}. Step 4: Cesàro convergence of the target probabilities. By Lemma B.2 , M ⁡ ( t ) → μ M(t)\to\mu almost surely. Since the prior is continuous, the true best arm and true second-best arm are unique almost surely. On this probability-one event, the map m ↦ p ⁡ ( m ) m\mapsto p(m) is continuous at m = μ m=\mu . Therefore, as t → ∞ t\to\infty , p i ​ ( t ) → p i † ​ ( μ ) almost surely . p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\mu)\qquad\text{almost surely}. Hence, the sequence { p i ​ ( t ) } \{p_{i}(t)\} is also Cesàro convergent, i.e., 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 p i ​ ( t ) → p i † ​ ( μ ) almost surely . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\mu)\qquad\text{almost surely}. Now write 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) = 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 p i ​ ( t ) − 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 ( 1 − B t ) ​ p i ​ ( t ) . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t)=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}p_{i}(t)-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-B_{t})p_{i}(t). Since 0 ≤ p i ​ ( t ) ≤ 1 0\leq p_{i}(t)\leq 1 , 0 ≤ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 ( 1 − B t ) ​ p i ​ ( t ) ≤ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 ( 1 − B t ) = F ⁡ ( T ) T → 0 . 0\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-B_{t})p_{i}(t)\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-B_{t})=\frac{F(T)}{T}\to 0. Hence 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) → p i † ​ ( μ ) almost surely . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\mu)\qquad\text{almost surely}. Combining this with Step 3 gives ( B.3 ). ∎ Finally, we show that the statistic R T / T R_{T}/T converges to C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) . Lemma B.4 (Convergence of the Terminal Statistic) . Under the joint Bayesian law, R T T → C † ​ ( μ ) almost surely . \frac{R_{T}}{T}\to C^{\dagger}(\mu)\qquad\text{almost surely}. (B.4) Proof. By Lemma B.2 , M i ​ ( T ) → μ i M_{i}(T)\to\mu_{i} for every i i . Since the true best arm is unique almost surely, b ^ T = b ⋆ \widehat{b}_{T}=b^{\star} for T T large enough. So for every j ≠ b ⋆ j\neq b^{\star} , M b ⋆ ​ ( T ) − M j ​ ( T ) → Δ j M_{b^{\star}}(T)-M_{j}(T)\to\Delta_{j} . Recall V i ​ ( T ) = 1 σ 0 − 2 + N i ​ ( T ) V_{i}(T)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}^{-2}+N_{i}(T)} . Furthermore, by Lemma B.3 , N i ​ ( T ) T → p i † ​ ( μ ) \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\mu) . Hence T ​ V i ​ ( T ) = T σ 0 − 2 + N i ​ ( T ) → 1 p i † ​ ( μ ) . T\ V_{i}(T)=\frac{T}{\sigma_{0}^{-2}+N_{i}(T)}\to\frac{1}{p_{i}^{\dagger}(\mu)}. Therefore, for every j ≠ b ⋆ j\neq b^{\star} , 1 T ​ ( M b ⋆ ​ ( T ) − M j ​ ( T ) ) 2 2 ​ ( V b ⋆ ​ ( T ) + V j ​ ( T ) ) → Δ j 2 2 ​ ( 1 / p b ⋆ † ​ ( μ ) + 1 / p j † ​ ( μ ) ) . \frac{1}{T}\frac{\bigl(M_{b^{\star}}(T)-M_{j}(T)\bigr)^{2}}{2\bigl(V_{b^{\star}}(T)+V_{j}(T)\bigr)}\to\frac{\Delta_{j}^{2}}{2\left(1/p_{b^{\star}}^{\dagger}(\mu)+1/p_{j}^{\dagger}(\mu)\right)}. Taking the minimum over j ≠ b ⋆ j\neq b^{\star} proves ( B.4 ) as desired. ∎ B.3 Lower Tail of the Exponent We now analyze the lower tail of C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) . We first bound the event that the means of three or more arms are ε \varepsilon -close. Lemma B.5 (Triple Near-Tie Probability) . Let Γ 3 := μ ( 1 ) − μ ( 3 ) \Gamma_{3}:=\mu_{(1)}-\mu_{(3)} when K ≥ 3 K\geq 3 . If K = 2 K=2 , set Γ 3 := + ∞ \Gamma_{3}:=+\infty . We have that as ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 , ℙ ⁡ ( Γ 3 ≤ ε ) = O ⁡ ( ε 2 ) . \mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{3}\leq\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon^{2}). Proof. For K = 2 K=2 the statement is trivial. Assume K ≥ 3 K\geq 3 . If Γ 3 ≤ ε \Gamma_{3}\leq\varepsilon , then there exist three distinct arms i , j , k i,j,k such that all three means lie within an interval of length ε \varepsilon around the largest of the three. By a union bound over ordered triples, ℙ ⁡ ( Γ 3 ≤ ε ) ≤ ∑ i ≠ j ≠ k ∫ ℝ f i ​ ( x ) ​ [ ∫ x − ε x f j ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 y ] ​ [ ∫ x − ε x f k ​ ( z ) ​ 𝑑 z ] ​ 𝑑 x . \mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{3}\leq\varepsilon)\leq\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)\left[\int_{x-\varepsilon}^{x}f_{j}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right]\left[\int_{x-\varepsilon}^{x}f_{k}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z\right]\mathrm{d}x. Gaussian densities are bounded uniformly by ( σ 0 ​ 2 ​ π ) − 1 (\sigma_{0}\sqrt{2\pi})^{-1} , so each inner integral is O ⁡ ( ε ) O(\varepsilon) uniformly in x x . Since ∫ f i ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = 1 \int f_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1 , the whole expression is O ⁡ ( ε 2 ) O(\varepsilon^{2}) . ∎ Remark B.1 . This lemma reveals why asymptotically the leading-order complexity of the abstention problem depends only on pairwise gaps. While a two-arm near-tie forms the primary statistical bottleneck (occurring with probability O ⁡ ( ε ) O(\varepsilon) ), the event that three or more arms are simultaneously tied is one order rarer (namely, O ⁡ ( ε 2 ) O(\varepsilon^{2}) ). Consequently, in the small-abstention regime, the asymptotic error exponent is perfectly captured by the closest challenger alone; the statistical cost of separating third-best (and worse) arms vanishes. Next, we show that W † ​ ( μ ) W^{\dagger}(\mu) , defined in ( B.1 ), is approximately 2 2 when C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) is small, because it is unlikely (by Lemma B.5 ) that many other arms are close to the optimal. Consequently, Γ 2 / 8 \Gamma^{2}/8 and C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) behave similarly. Lemma B.6 (Lower Tail of C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) ) . As x ↓ 0 x\downarrow 0 , ℙ ⁡ ( C † ​ ( μ ) ≤ x ) = κ ν ​ 8 ​ x + o ⁡ ( x ) . \mathbb{P}(C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq x)=\kappa_{\nu}\sqrt{8x}+o(\sqrt{x}). (B.5) Proof. On the other hand, to derive an upper bound, set δ x := x 3 / 8 \delta_{x}:=x^{3/8} . Thus, on { C † ​ ( μ ) ≤ x , Γ 3 &gt; δ x } \{C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq x,\ \Gamma_{3}&gt;\delta_{x}\} , we have W † ​ ( μ ) = ∑ ℓ = 1 K w ℓ † ​ ( μ ) = 1 + ∑ j ≠ b ⋆ ( Γ Δ j ) 2 = 2 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) , W^{\dagger}(\mu)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}w_{\ell}^{\dagger}(\mu)=1+\sum_{j\neq b^{\star}}\left(\frac{\Gamma}{\Delta_{j}}\right)^{2}=2+o(1), uniformly as x ↓ 0 x\downarrow 0 . Therefore, for every fixed η &gt; 0 \eta&gt;0 and all sufficiently small x x , C † ( μ ) ≤ x , Γ 3 &gt; δ x ⟹ Γ = 2 C † ​ ( μ ) ​ W † ​ ( μ ) ≤ ( 1 + η ) 8 ​ x . By Lemma B.5 , ℙ ⁡ ( Γ 3 ≤ δ x ) = O ⁡ ( δ x 2 ) = O ⁡ ( x 3 / 4 ) = o ⁡ ( x ) . \mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{3}\leq\delta_{x})=O(\delta_{x}^{2})=O(x^{3/4})=o(\sqrt{x}). Thus lim sup x ↓ 0 ℙ ⁡ ( C † ​ ( μ ) ≤ x ) x ≤ ( 1 + η ) ​ κ ν ​ 8 . \limsup_{x\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{P}(C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq x)}{\sqrt{x}}\leq(1+\eta)\kappa_{\nu}\sqrt{8}. Letting η ↓ 0 \eta\downarrow 0 gives the matching upper bound, and hence we obtain ( B.5 ). ∎ B.4 Lower Bound on the Calibration Threshold r T , α r_{T,\alpha} Proof of Lemma 2.2 . Fix ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \varepsilon\in(0,1) and set x α , ε := ( 1 − ε ) ​ 1 8 ​ κ ν 2 ​ α 2 x_{\alpha,\varepsilon}:=(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{8\kappa_{\nu}^{2}}\alpha^{2} .
