We begin by expressing these global probabilities over the full parameter (mean of the arms) space ℝ K \mathbb{R}^{K} , i.e., 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) = ∫ ℝ K ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ = ? ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) , and ℰ T ​ ( π ) = ∫ ℝ K ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) , ? } ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) . \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}=?)\,P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}\bigl(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(\mu),?\}\bigr)\,P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu). Inspired by the form of the prior complexity κ ν \kappa_{\nu} , it is natural to partition the parameter space ℝ K \mathbb{R}^{K} according to the top pair of arms. For any pair i &lt; j i&lt;j , define the region Ω i ​ j \Omega_{ij} where arms i i and j j strictly dominate all the other arms, i.e., Ω i ​ j := { μ ∈ ℝ K : min ⁡ { μ i , μ j } &gt; max k ≠ i , j ⁡ μ k } . \Omega_{ij}:=\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\min\{\mu_{i},\mu_{j}\}&gt;\max_{k\neq i,j}\mu_{k}\right\}. Because the boundaries between these regions correspond to tie events and hence have P ν P_{\nu} -measure zero, the sets Ω i ​ j \Omega_{ij} form a partition of the parameter space up to P ν P_{\nu} -null sets. Thus, we can decompose the abstention and undetected error probability as: 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) = ∑ i &lt; j ∫ Ω i ​ j ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ = ? ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) , and ℰ T ​ ( π ) = ∑ i &lt; j ∫ Ω i ​ j ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) , ? } ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) . \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)=\sum_{i&lt;j}\int_{\Omega_{ij}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}=?)\,P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi)=\sum_{i&lt;j}\int_{\Omega_{ij}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}\bigl(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(\mu),?\}\bigr)\,P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu). Ultimately, we want to lower bound the undetected error ℰ T ​ ( π ) \mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi) using a Neyman–Pearson lemma (cf. 21 ) style rearrangement. However, working directly with the restricted true prior 𝟙 Ω i ​ j ​ ( μ ) ​ P ν ​ ( d ​ μ ) \mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{ij}}(\mu)P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu) presents significant technical hurdles. This is overcome by the introduction of a submeasure, which we call a flat local subprior . C.1 A Submeasure: Flat Local Subprior To resolve the difficulties of integrating over Ω i ​ j \Omega_{ij} , we construct a mathematically cleaner measure Q ρ Q_{\rho} that is a submeasure of the true Gaussian prior P ν P_{\nu} , where ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \rho\in(0,1) is a fixed slack parameter. Definition C.1 (Local Change of Variable) . For a fixed pair of arms i &lt; j i&lt;j , we reparameterize the mean vector μ ∈ ℝ K \mu\in\mathbb{R}^{K} into ( i , j , x , δ , h , ζ − i ​ j ) (i,j,x,\delta,h,\zeta_{-ij}) , a “local” coordinate system defined by the top two arms, their gap, and the other arms. Specifically, • x ∈ ℝ x\in\mathbb{R} : the midpoint of the top two arms’ means. • δ ≥ 0 \delta\geq 0 : the absolute gap between the top two arms. • h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } h\in\{+1,-1\} : the sign indicating which arm is better ( h = + 1 h=+1 for arm i i , h = − 1 h=-1 for arm j j ). • ζ − i ​ j = ( ζ k ) k ≠ i , j \zeta_{-ij}=(\zeta_{k})_{k\neq i,j} : the means of the remaining K − 2 K-2 arms. We consolidate the background variables, i , j , x , ζ − i ​ j i,j,x,\zeta_{-ij} , into one tuple ξ = ( i , j , x , ζ − i ​ j ) \xi=(i,j,x,\zeta_{-ij}) . Given ξ \xi , δ \delta , and h h , the inverse mapping μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ∈ ℝ K \mu(\xi,\delta,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{K} is defined as: μ i = x + h δ 2 , μ j = x − h δ 2 , and μ k = ζ k for k ≠ i , j . \mu_{i}=x+h\frac{\delta}{2},\quad\mu_{j}=x-h\frac{\delta}{2},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu_{k}=\zeta_{k}\text{ for }k\neq i,j. (C.1) Note the absolute value of the determinant of the Jacobian of the map ( x , δ ) ↦ ( μ i , μ j ) (x,\delta)\mapsto(\mu_{i},\mu_{j}) is 1 1 . Remark C.1 . To see why the restricted true prior is difficult to work with, let ψ : ℝ K → ℝ ≥ 0 \psi:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} be any non-negative measurable function. Applying the local coordinate transformation in ( C.1 ) to the true prior over the partitioned space yields: ∫ ℝ K ψ ⁡ ( μ ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) = ∑ i &lt; j ∫ Ω i ​ j ψ ⁡ ( μ ) ​ p ν ​ ( μ ) ​ 𝑑 μ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\psi(\mu)P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu)=\sum_{i&lt;j}\int_{\Omega_{ij}}\psi(\mu)p_{\nu}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu = ∑ i &lt; j ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 0 ∞ ∫ 𝒟 i ​ j ​ ( δ ) ψ ⁡ ( μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) ​ [ f i ​ ( x + h ​ δ 2 ) ​ f j ​ ( x − h ​ δ 2 ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ​ ( ζ k ) ] ​ d ξ ​ d δ , \displaystyle=\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{ij}(\delta)}\psi\bigl(\mu(\xi,\delta,h)\bigr)\bigg[f_{i}\Big(x+h\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)f_{j}\Big(x-h\frac{\delta}{2}\Big)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k})\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi\,\mathrm{d}\delta, where d ​ ξ = d ​ x ​ d ​ ζ − i ​ j \mathrm{d}\xi=\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij} , and the domain of integration 𝒟 i ​ j ​ ( δ ) := { ( x , ζ − i ​ j ) ∈ ℝ K − 1 : max k ≠ i , j ⁡ ζ k &lt; x − δ 2 } \mathcal{D}_{ij}(\delta):=\left\{(x,\zeta_{-ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}:\max_{k\neq i,j}\zeta_{k}&lt;x-\frac{\delta}{2}\right\} depends on the gap δ \delta . The expansion of ∫ ℝ K ψ ⁡ ( μ ) ​ P ν ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) \int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\psi(\mu)P_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\mu) above reveals the technical hurdles: the domain of integration 𝒟 i ​ j ​ ( δ ) \mathcal{D}_{ij}(\delta) shrinks as the gap δ \delta grows, and the densities f i f_{i} and f j f_{j} fluctuate with both the gap δ \delta and the sign h h . To overcome these hurdles, we construct Q ρ Q_{\rho} to eliminate these dependencies by restricting ( x , ζ − i ​ j ) (x,\zeta_{-ij}) to a smaller set, and by lower bounding f i ​ ( x + h ​ δ 2 ) ​ f j ​ ( x − h ​ δ 2 ) f_{i}\left(x+h\frac{\delta}{2}\right)f_{j}\left(x-h\frac{\delta}{2}\right) with ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) (1-\rho)f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x) , which depends on neither δ \delta nor h h . For ℓ &gt; 0 \ell&gt;0 and M &gt; 0 M&gt;0 , and for each pair 1 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ K 1\leq i&lt;j\leq K , define the ( ℓ , M ) (\ell,M) -restricted set 𝒞 i ​ j ( ℓ , M ) := { ( x , ζ − i ​ j ) : | x | ≤ M , | ζ k | ≤ M , ζ k ≤ x − ℓ for all k ≠ i , j } . \mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M):=\left\{(x,\zeta_{-ij}):|x|\leq M,\ |\zeta_{k}|\leq M,\ \zeta_{k}\leq x-\ell\text{ for all }k\neq i,j\right\}. Let 𝒞 ρ = ∪ i &lt; j 𝒞 i ​ j ( ℓ , M ) \mathcal{C}_{\rho}=\cup_{i&lt;j}\mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M) denote the disjoint union of 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) \mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M) over all arm pairs i &lt; j i&lt;j . Recall that we defined κ ν = ∑ i ≠ j ∫ ℝ f i ( x ) f j ( x ) ∏ k ≠ i , j F k ( x ) d x = 2 ∑ i &lt; j ∫ − ∞ ∞ [ ∫ − ∞ x ⋯ ∫ − ∞ x f i ( x ) f j ( x ) ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ( ζ k ) d ζ − i ​ j ] d x . \kappa_{\nu}=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}F_{k}(x)\ \mathrm{d}x=2\sum_{i&lt;j}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{x}\cdots\int_{-\infty}^{x}f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij}\right]\ \mathrm{d}x. By the monotone convergence theorem, for any ρ &gt; 0 \rho&gt;0 , we can choose ℓ &gt; 0 \ell&gt;0 sufficiently small and M &gt; 0 M&gt;0 sufficiently large such that: 2 ​ ∑ i &lt; j ∫ 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ​ ( ζ k ) ​ d ​ ζ − i ​ j ​ 𝑑 x ≥ ( 1 − ρ ) ​ κ ν . 2\sum_{i&lt;j}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M)}f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij}\,\mathrm{d}x\geq(1-\rho)\kappa_{\nu}. We now define a measure Λ ρ \Lambda_{\rho} whose support is the set 𝒞 ρ \mathcal{C}_{\rho} . Since 𝒞 ρ \mathcal{C}_{\rho} is the disjoint union of 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) \mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M) , we only need to specify Λ ρ \Lambda_{\rho} for each pair i &lt; j i&lt;j . For the pair i &lt; j i&lt;j , Λ ρ \Lambda_{\rho} takes the form Λ ρ ​ ( d ​ ξ ) := ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ​ ( ζ k ) ​ d ​ x ​ d ​ ζ − i ​ j \Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi):=(1-\rho)f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij} (C.2) Let c ρ := 2 ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝒞 ρ ) ≥ ( 1 − ρ ) 2 ​ κ ν c_{\rho}:=2\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{C}_{\rho})\geq(1-\rho)^{2}\kappa_{\nu} . Lemma C.1 (Uniform Lower Bound) . There exists a gap δ ρ ∈ ( 0 , 2 ​ ℓ ) \delta_{\rho}\in(0,2\ell) such that, for all δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ρ ] \delta\in[0,\delta_{\rho}] , ξ ∈ 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) \xi\in\mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M) , and h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } h\in\{+1,-1\} , the original densities satisfy the multiplicative bound: ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) ≤ f i ​ ( x + h ​ δ 2 ) ​ f j ​ ( x − h ​ δ 2 ) . (1-\rho)f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\leq f_{i}\!\left(x+h\frac{\delta}{2}\right)f_{j}\!\left(x-h\frac{\delta}{2}\right). Furthermore, for any δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ρ ] \delta\in[0,\delta_{\rho}] , arms i i and j j are the top two arms because x − δ 2 &gt; x − ℓ ≥ ζ k x-\frac{\delta}{2}&gt;x-\ell\geq\zeta_{k} . Proof. For each ( i , j ) (i,j) , because 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) \mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M) is compact, the Gaussian densities defined on 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) \mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M) are (strictly) positive and uniformly continuous. Because ℓ &gt; 0 \ell&gt;0 separates the top pair of arms i , j i,j from the other arms k ≠ i , j k\neq i,j , we can choose a sufficiently small δ ρ &lt; 2 ​ ℓ \delta_{\rho}&lt;2\ell such that the continuity of f i f_{i} and f j f_{j} guarantees the multiplicative bound over the interval [ 0 , δ ρ ] [0,\delta_{\rho}] . Since there are finitely many pairs, taking minimum of the pair-specific values of δ ρ \delta_{\rho} yields the desired result. ∎ We now define a convenient submeasure to work with later. Definition C.2 (Flat Local Subprior) . Define the flat local subprior Q ρ Q_{\rho} as Q ρ ​ ( E ) := ∑ i &lt; j ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) ∫ 0 δ ρ 𝟙 E ​ ( μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) ​ 𝑑 δ ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) ∀ E ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℝ K ) . Q_{\rho}(E):=\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M)}\int_{0}^{\delta_{\rho}}\mathbbm{1}_{E}\!\bigl(\mu(\xi,\delta,h)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\delta\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\qquad\forall\,E\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{K}). (C.3) Lemma C.2 (Domination) . The constructed measure Q ρ Q_{\rho} in ( C.3 ) is a submeasure of the true Gaussian prior P ν P_{\nu} , i.e., Q ρ ≤ P ν Q_{\rho}\leq P_{\nu} everywhere. Proof. For each i &lt; j i&lt;j , let 𝒮 i ​ j := { μ ( ξ , δ , h ) : ξ ∈ 𝒞 i ​ j ( ℓ , M ) , δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ρ ] , h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } } , \mathcal{S}_{ij}:=\left\{\mu(\xi,\delta,h):\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M),\ \delta\in[0,\delta_{\rho}],\ h\in\{+1,-1\}\right\}, and let 𝒮 := ⋃ i &lt; j 𝒮 i ​ j \mathcal{S}:=\bigcup_{i&lt;j}\mathcal{S}_{ij} . For any Borel set E ⊆ ℝ K E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K} , substituting the definition of Λ ρ \Lambda_{\rho} in ( C.2 ) into ( C.3 ) gives Q ρ ​ ( E ) = ∑ i &lt; j ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 𝒞 i ​ j ​ ( ℓ , M ) ∫ 0 δ ρ 𝟙 E ​ ( μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) ​ ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ​ ( ζ k ) ​ 𝑑 δ ​ 𝑑 x ​ d ​ ζ − i ​ j . Q_{\rho}(E)=\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{ij}(\ell,M)}\int_{0}^{\delta_{\rho}}\mathbbm{1}_{E}\!\bigl(\mu(\xi,\delta,h)\bigr)(1-\rho)f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k})\,\mathrm{d}\delta\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij}. For each fixed i &lt; j i&lt;j and h h , the Jacobian determinant of the coordinate transformation in ( C.1 ) has absolute value 1 1 . Moreover, except on the null set { δ = 0 } \{\delta=0\} , the sign h h is uniquely determined by μ i − μ j \mu_{i}-\mu_{j} , and the top-two pair { i , j } \{i,j\} is uniquely determined on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . Hence Q ρ Q_{\rho} has Lebesgue density q ρ ​ ( μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) = ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f i ​ ( x ) ​ f j ​ ( x ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ​ ( ζ k ) q_{\rho}\bigl(\mu(\xi,\delta,h)\bigr)=(1-\rho)f_{i}(x)f_{j}(x)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k}) for Lebesgue-almost every μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ∈ 𝒮 i ​ j \mu(\xi,\delta,h)\in\mathcal{S}_{ij} . Per the definition in ( C.3 ), Q ρ Q_{\rho} is supported on 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , hence we also have q ρ ​ ( μ ) = 0 q_{\rho}(\mu)=0 for Lebesgue-almost every μ ∉ 𝒮 \mu\notin\mathcal{S} . For μ = μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ∈ 𝒮 i ​ j \mu=\mu(\xi,\delta,h)\in\mathcal{S}_{ij} , the true prior density is p ν ​ ( μ ) = f i ​ ( x + h ​ δ 2 ) ​ f j ​ ( x − h ​ δ 2 ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f k ​ ( ζ k ) . p_{\nu}(\mu)=f_{i}\left(x+h\frac{\delta}{2}\right)f_{j}\left(x-h\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\prod_{k\neq i,j}f_{k}(\zeta_{k}). Lemma C.1 therefore gives q ρ ​ ( μ ) ≤ p ν ​ ( μ ) q_{\rho}(\mu)\leq p_{\nu}(\mu) for Lebesgue-almost every μ ∈ 𝒮 \mu\in\mathcal{S} . The same inequality holds outside 𝒮 \mathcal{S} because q ρ = 0 q_{\rho}=0 there. Consequently, for every Borel set E ⊆ ℝ K E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K} , Q ρ ​ ( E ) = ∫ E q ρ ​ ( μ ) ​ 𝑑 μ ≤ ∫ E p ν ​ ( μ ) ​ 𝑑 μ = P ν ​ ( E ) . Q_{\rho}(E)=\int_{E}q_{\rho}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\int_{E}p_{\nu}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu=P_{\nu}(E). Hence Q ρ ≤ P ν Q_{\rho}\leq P_{\nu} . ∎ Figure 3 provides a visual intuition for this result, illustrating how the constructed flat subprior bounds the true Gaussian prior from below over the compact gap interval. 0 0 δ ρ \delta_{\rho} p ν ​ ( δ ρ ) p_{\nu}(\delta_{\rho}) ( 1 − ρ ) ​ p ν ​ ( δ ρ ) (1-\rho)p_{\nu}(\delta_{\rho}) Local Pairwise Gap ( δ ) (\delta) Density (Unnormalized) q ρ ≤ p ν q_{\rho}\leq p_{\nu} ρ ↓ 0 \rho\downarrow 0 True Prior p ν ​ ( δ ) p_{\nu}(\delta) Flat Subprior q ρ ​ ( δ ) q_{\rho}(\delta) Limit lim ρ → 0 q ρ \lim_{\rho\to 0}q_{\rho} Figure 3 : The true Gaussian prior P ν P_{\nu} is bounded from below by the flat subprior Q ρ Q_{\rho} on the interval [ 0 , δ ρ ] [0,\delta_{\rho}] . In the above, for brevity, we write the density as p ν ​ ( δ ) := p ν ​ ( μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) p_{\nu}(\delta):=p_{\nu}(\mu(\xi,\delta,h)) and similarly for q ρ q_{\rho} . Lemma C.3 (Properties of Q ρ Q_{\rho} ) . Let Ξ \Xi (background oracle), Δ \Delta (gap), and H H (sign) denote random quantities under Q ρ Q_{\rho} . The construction of Q ρ Q_{\rho} results in the following joint distribution: Q ρ ​ ( d ​ μ ) = Q ρ ​ ( Ξ ∈ d ​ ξ , Δ ∈ d ​ δ , H = h ) = Λ ρ ​ ( d ​ ξ ) ​ d ​ δ , h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } . Q_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\mu)=Q_{\rho}(\Xi\in\mathrm{d}\xi,\Delta\in\mathrm{d}\delta,H=h)=\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\delta,\qquad h\in\{+1,-1\}. (C.4) Because Q ρ Q_{\rho} is not a probability measure in general, we refer to the Q ρ Q_{\rho} measure of a set as its Q ρ Q_{\rho} -mass in the following. This measure has two important properties: 1. Fair Sign: Conditional on any realization ( Ξ = ξ , Δ = δ ) (\Xi=\xi,\Delta=\delta) , the two signs have equal Q ρ Q_{\rho} -mass, i.e., Q ρ ​ ( H = + 1 ∣ ξ , δ ) = Q ρ ​ ( H = − 1 ∣ ξ , δ ) Q_{\rho}(H=+1\mid\xi,\delta)=Q_{\rho}(H=-1\mid\xi,\delta) . 2. Flat Gap: The marginal unnormalized density of the gap is uniform, i.e., Q ρ ​ ( Δ ∈ d ​ δ ) = c ρ ​ d ​ δ Q_{\rho}(\Delta\in\mathrm{d}\delta)=c_{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\delta for δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ρ ] \delta\in[0,\delta_{\rho}] . Proof. By the definition of Q ρ Q_{\rho} , the measure on the product space 𝒞 ρ × [ 0 , δ ρ ] × { + 1 , − 1 } \mathcal{C}_{\rho}\times[0,\delta_{\rho}]\times\{+1,-1\} factors as Λ ρ ​ ( d ​ ξ ) ⊗ d ​ δ ⊗ 𝟙 { h } \Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\otimes\mathrm{d}\delta\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\{h\}} . Because this is a product measure that does not depend on h h , the sign H H occurs with equal probability 1 / 2 1/2 regardless of Ξ \Xi or Δ \Delta . Furthermore, integrating out Ξ \Xi and H H leaves the measure of the gap Δ \Delta as 2 ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝒞 ρ ) ​ d ​ δ = c ρ ​ d ​ δ 2\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{C}_{\rho})\,\mathrm{d}\delta=c_{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\delta , proving the flat gap property. ∎ Lemma C.4 (Probability Domination Under Q ρ Q_{\rho} ) . For any policy π \pi , the Bayes probability evaluated under the subprior Q ρ Q_{\rho} is a lower bound of the probability under the original prior P ν P_{\nu} . Specifically, if a strategy π \pi satisfies the abstention condition 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha under P ν P_{\nu} , it also satisfies: 𝒜 T Q ρ ​ ( π ) := ∫ ℝ K ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) ​ Q ρ ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) ≤ 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α . \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\pi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?)\,Q_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\mu)\leq\mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha. In addition, the true undetected error is lower bounded by the error evaluated under the subprior: ℰ T ​ ( π ) ≥ ℰ T Q ρ ​ ( π ) := ∫ ℝ K ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ , ? } ) ​ Q ρ ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) . \mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi)\geq\mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\pi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star},?\})\,Q_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\mu). Proof. This follows directly from Lemma C.2 . ∎ This lemma explains how to use Q ρ Q_{\rho} . Any strategy satisfying 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha under the original prior also has Q ρ Q_{\rho} -abstention mass at most α \alpha . Consequently, to lower bound the original Bayes undetected error probability, it suffices to lower bound the error contributed by the flat subprior Q ρ Q_{\rho} . C.2 Oracle Reduction: Lower Bounding via a 2-Arm Hypothesis Testing Problem Next, we evaluate the probabilities under the local subprior Q ρ Q_{\rho} . To isolate the fundamental difficulty of the problem, we reduce the K K -arm task to a 2 2 -arm hypothesis testing problem by giving the learner additional side (oracle) information. Revealing Oracle Information. Before sampling begins, we reveal the background oracle Ξ = ( I , J , X , ζ − I ​ J ) \Xi=(I,J,X,\zeta_{-IJ}) , which depends on the random means μ \mu , to the learner. For a specific realization Ξ = ξ = ( i , j , x , ζ − i ​ j ) \Xi=\xi=(i,j,x,\zeta_{-ij}) , this means the learner knows the identities of the top two arms, their midpoint x x , and the means ζ − i ​ j \zeta_{-ij} of all remaining background arms. Conditional on any realization of the background oracle Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi , the only remaining random variables relevant to the decision under Q ρ Q_{\rho} are the gap magnitude and the sign, i.e., Δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ρ ] , and H ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } . \Delta\in[0,\delta_{\rho}],\quad\mbox{and}\quad H\in\{+1,-1\}. For ease of notation, we define the unknown signed gap Θ := H ​ Δ \Theta:=H\Delta . Let ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} denote the probability law of the experiment conditional on the background oracle Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi and the signed gap Θ = θ \Theta=\theta . Since any policy in the original experiment can be implemented in the oracle-revealed setting by simply ignoring the side information Ξ \Xi , any lower bound derived for this oracle-revealed experiment provides a lower bound for the original problem. Furthermore, since the background means are known, querying any arm other than i i or j j provides no information about the unknown gap Θ \Theta . We show that we can restrict our analysis to strategies that only sample the top two arms and output the decision of the sign; we call these local pair strategies . Lemma C.5 (Sufficiency of Local Pair Strategies) . Fix a realization of the background oracle Ξ = ξ = ( i , j , x , ζ − i ​ j ) \Xi=\xi=(i,j,x,\zeta_{-ij}) . For any adaptive strategy π \pi , there exists a modified strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} that samples only arms i i and j j , restricts its terminal recommendation to a ^ T ′ ∈ { + , − , ? } \widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}\in\{+,-,?\} , and satisfies for every signed-gap realization θ ∈ [ − δ ρ , δ ρ ] ∖ { 0 } \theta\in[-\delta_{\rho},\delta_{\rho}]\setminus\{0\} with true sign h = sgn ​ ( θ ) ∈ { + , − } h=\text{sgn}(\theta)\in\{+,-\} , i.e., ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ T ′ = ? ) = ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) , and ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ T ′ = − h ) ≤ ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( h ) , ? } ) , \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=?)=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=-h)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(h),?\}), (C.5) where a ⋆ ​ ( + ) = i a^{\star}(+)=i and a ⋆ ​ ( − ) = j a^{\star}(-)=j . This says that the abstention probability of π ′ \pi^{\prime} is unchanged from that of π \pi and the undetected error probability of π ′ \pi^{\prime} is less than or equal to that of π \pi . Proof. Fix ξ = ( i , j , x , ζ − i ​ j ) \xi=(i,j,x,\zeta_{-ij}) . We construct a modified strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} from π \pi that possesses the desired properties in ( C.5 ). When π \pi samples from an arm k ∈ { i , j } k\in\{i,j\} , π ′ \pi^{\prime} queries that same arm and feeds the observed reward back to π \pi . Whenever π \pi chooses to sample from an arm k ∉ { i , j } k\notin\{i,j\} , π ′ \pi^{\prime} instead queries arm i i , discards the true reward, and feeds π \pi an independent simulated reward drawn from 𝒩 ⁡ ( ζ k , 1 ) \mathcal{N}(\zeta_{k},1) . Because Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi is known, ζ k \zeta_{k} is the true mean of arm k k . Furthermore, for k ≠ i , j k\neq i,j , the reward distributions of arm k k are independent of the unknown signed gap Θ \Theta . Therefore, the simulated reward from π ′ \pi^{\prime} matches that from the true distribution of arm k k . By induction, the internal history observed by π \pi under π ′ \pi^{\prime} is identically distributed to the true history in the original experiment. Consequently, the terminal distribution of π \pi is preserved for every θ \theta . When π \pi stops and outputs a recommendation a ^ T \widehat{a}_{T} , π ′ \pi^{\prime} maps this to a sign decision a ^ T ′ ∈ { + , − , ? } \widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}\in\{+,-,?\} as follows: i ↦ + i\mapsto+ , j ↦ − j\mapsto- , ? ↦ ? ?\mapsto? , and any k ∉ { i , j , ? } k\notin\{i,j,?\} is mapped arbitrarily to + + . This map preserves the abstention probability. For the undetected error, recall that on the support of Q ρ Q_{\rho} , arms i i and j j are strictly the top two arms. Thus, any original output k ∉ { i , j , ? } k\notin\{i,j,?\} is an error. If h = − h=- , mapping k k to + + yields a ^ ′ T = + \widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=+ , which is an incorrect sign, thereby preserving the error probability. If h = + h=+ , mapping k k to + + converts an original error into a correct guess, reducing the error probability. Thus, across all cases, the probability of an incorrect sign under π ′ \pi^{\prime} is less than or equal to the probability of an undetected error under π \pi , showing ( C.5 ). ∎ C.3 Transformed Observations and Sufficient Statistics: To establish clean analytical expressions for the abstention and undetected error probabilities, we now demonstrate that any decision rule acting on the full history of this restricted two-arm game can be reduced to a randomized decision rule acting on a scalar sufficient statistic, without altering any terminal-output probability. Fix the revealed background oracle realization Ξ = ξ = ( i , j , x , ζ − i ​ j ) \Xi=\xi=(i,j,x,\zeta_{-ij}) , and consider strategies that sample only from arms i i and j j . Define the sign indicator for the arms: sgn ⁡ ( i ) := + 1 \mathrm{sgn}(i):=+1 and sgn ⁡ ( j ) := − 1 \mathrm{sgn}(j):=-1 . At time t t , if the learner samples A t ∈ { i , j } A_{t}\in\{i,j\} and observes the reward X t X_{t} , we define the transformed observation : Z t := sgn ⁡ ( A t ) ​ ( X t − x ) . Z_{t}:=\mathrm{sgn}(A_{t})(X_{t}-x). For any realization of the signed gap Θ = H ​ Δ = θ \Theta=H\Delta=\theta , the means of the top two arms are: μ i = x + θ 2 , and μ j = x − θ 2 . \mu_{i}=x+\frac{\theta}{2},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu_{j}=x-\frac{\theta}{2}. Let ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} denote the probability law of the experiment conditional on Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi and Θ = θ \Theta=\theta . Furthermore, let: ℋ t ξ := ( ξ , A 1 , Z 1 , … , A t , Z t ) \mathcal{H}_{t}^{\xi}:=(\xi,A_{1},Z_{1},\dots,A_{t},Z_{t}) denote the transformed oracle history up to time t t . Lemma C.6 (Transformed Observations are i.i.d.) . Fix an oracle realization Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi and a signed-gap realization θ ∈ [ − δ ρ , δ ρ ] \theta\in[-\delta_{\rho},\delta_{\rho}] . Under ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} , conditionally on ℋ t − 1 ξ \mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{\xi} and the chosen arm A t ∈ { i , j } A_{t}\in\{i,j\} , the transformed observation satisfies: Z t ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( θ 2 , 1 ) . Z_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2},1\right). i.e., under the full joint Bayesian law, Z t | ℋ t − 1 ξ , A t = i , Θ = θ , Ξ = ξ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( θ 2 , 1 ) . Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{\xi},A_{t}=i,\Theta=\theta,\Xi=\xi\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2},1\right). (C.6) Because this conditional law does not depend on the chosen arm A t A_{t} , the sequence Z 1 , … , Z T Z_{1},\dots,Z_{T} is identically and independently distributed as 𝒩 ⁡ ( θ / 2 , 1 ) \mathcal{N}(\theta/2,1) under ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} . Proof. If A t = i A_{t}=i , the true observation follows X t | ℋ t − 1 ξ , A t = i , Θ = θ , Ξ = ξ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( x + θ 2 , 1 ) . X_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{\xi},A_{t}=i,\Theta=\theta,\Xi=\xi\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(x+\frac{\theta}{2},1\right). Applying the transformation Z t = X t − x Z_{t}=X_{t}-x yields ( C.6 ). While if A t = j A_{t}=j , the true observation follows X t | ℋ t − 1 ξ , A t = j , Θ = θ , Ξ = ξ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( x − θ 2 , 1 ) X_{t}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{\xi},A_{t}=j,\Theta=\theta,\Xi=\xi\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(x-\frac{\theta}{2},1\right) . Similarly, applying the transformation yields ( C.6 ). Thus, the conditional law of Z t Z_{t} is 𝒩 ⁡ ( θ / 2 , 1 ) \mathcal{N}(\theta/2,1) regardless of which of the two arms is chosen. Next we prove independence. Let γ θ \gamma_{\theta} denote the measure corresponding to 𝒩 ⁡ ( θ / 2 , 1 ) \mathcal{N}(\theta/2,1) . For any Borel set B B , we have: ℙ θ ξ ​ ( Z t ∈ B ∣ ℋ t − 1 ξ , A t ) = γ θ ​ ( B ) . \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(Z_{t}\in B\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{\xi},A_{t})=\gamma_{\theta}(B). Iterating this identity using the tower property shows that for any sequence of Borel sets B 1 , … , B T B_{1},\dots,B_{T} : ℙ θ ξ ​ ( Z 1 ∈ B 1 , … , Z T ∈ B T ) = ∏ t = 1 T γ θ ​ ( B t ) . \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(Z_{1}\in B_{1},\dots,Z_{T}\in B_{T})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{\theta}(B_{t}). Hence, given ξ \xi and θ \theta , Z 1 , … , Z T Z_{1},\dots,Z_{T} are i.i.d. over the transformed history. ∎ We consolidate the sequence of observations into a sum and its time-scaled counterpart. Define: W := ∑ t = 1 T Z t , and V := W T . W:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t},\quad\mbox{and}\quad V:=\frac{W}{\sqrt{T}}. Lemma C.7 (Likelihood Ratios Depend Only on W W ) . For every ξ \xi and every θ ∈ [ − δ ρ , δ ρ ] \theta\in[-\delta_{\rho},\delta_{\rho}] , the measure ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} is absolutely continuous with respect to ℙ 0 ξ \mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi} (i.e., ℙ θ ξ ≪ ℙ 0 ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}\ll\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi} ), and the likelihood ratio of the transformed oracle history ℋ T ξ \mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi} is: d ​ ℙ θ ξ d ​ ℙ 0 ξ ​ ( ℋ T ξ ) = exp ⁡ ( θ 2 ​ W − T ​ θ 2 8 ) = exp ⁡ ( θ ​ T 2 ​ V − T ​ θ 2 8 ) . \frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi}}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi})=\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2}W-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right)=\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\sqrt{T}}{2}V-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right). Consequently, W W (and equivalently V V ) is a sufficient statistic for θ \theta . Proof. Let h T = ( ξ , a 1 , z 1 , … , a T , z T ) h_{T}=(\xi,a_{1},z_{1},\dots,a_{T},z_{T}) be the realized transformed oracle history, and let ϕ \phi be the standard normal density as usual. Under ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} , the density of h T h_{T} factors as: p θ ξ ​ ( h T ) = [ ∏ t = 1 T π t ​ ( a t ∣ ξ , a 1 , z 1 , … , a t − 1 , z t − 1 ) ] ​ ∏ t = 1 T ϕ ⁡ ( z t − θ 2 ) , p_{\theta}^{\xi}(h_{T})=\left[\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid\xi,a_{1},z_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},z_{t-1})\right]\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi\!\left(z_{t}-\frac{\theta}{2}\right), where π t \pi_{t} is the strategy’s sampling kernel at time t t . Because the action probabilities are identical functions of the realized history under both ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} and ℙ 0 ξ \mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi} , they cancel in the likelihood ratio, i.e., p θ ξ ​ ( h T ) p 0 ξ ​ ( h T ) = ∏ t = 1 T ϕ ⁡ ( z t − θ / 2 ) ϕ ⁡ ( z t ) = ∏ t = 1 T exp ⁡ ( θ 2 ​ z t − θ 2 8 ) . \frac{p_{\theta}^{\xi}(h_{T})}{p_{0}^{\xi}(h_{T})}=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\phi(z_{t}-\theta/2)}{\phi(z_{t})}=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2}z_{t}-\frac{\theta^{2}}{8}\right). Aggregating the sum inside the exponential yields: p θ ξ ​ ( h T ) p 0 ξ ​ ( h T ) = exp ⁡ ( θ 2 ​ ∑ t = 1 T z t − T ​ θ 2 8 ) = exp ⁡ ( θ 2 ​ W − T ​ θ 2 8 ) . \frac{p_{\theta}^{\xi}(h_{T})}{p_{0}^{\xi}(h_{T})}=\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}z_{t}-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right)=\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2}W-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right). ∎ Lemma C.8 (Sufficiency of V V for Terminal Decisions) . Fix the revealed background oracle realization Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi . Let ψ ~ ξ = ( ψ ~ + ξ , ψ ~ − ξ , ψ ~ ? ξ ) \widetilde{\psi}^{\xi}=(\widetilde{\psi}_{+}^{\xi},\widetilde{\psi}_{-}^{\xi},\widetilde{\psi}_{?}^{\xi}) be any randomized terminal rule based on the transformed oracle history ℋ T ξ \mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi} , and let a ^ ∈ { + , − , ? } \widehat{a}\in\{+,-,?\} be its random output. There exists a modified terminal rule ψ ξ \psi^{\xi} depending on the history only through V V , with random output a ^ V ∈ { + , − , ? } \widehat{a}^{V}\in\{+,-,?\} , such that for every θ ∈ [ − δ ρ , δ ρ ] \theta\in[-\delta_{\rho},\delta_{\rho}] and every decision d ∈ { + , − , ? } d\in\{+,-,?\} : ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ V = d ) = ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ = d ) . \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}^{V}=d)=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}=d). Proof. By Lemma C.7 , the likelihood ratio d ​ ℙ θ ξ / d ​ ℙ 0 ξ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi} is σ ⁡ ( W ) \sigma(W) -measurable. Since σ ⁡ ( V ) = σ ⁡ ( W ) \sigma(V)=\sigma(W) , it is also σ ⁡ ( V ) \sigma(V) -measurable. Let ψ ~ d ξ ​ ( h T ) \widetilde{\psi}_{d}^{\xi}(h_{T}) denote the probability that the original terminal rule outputs decision d ∈ { + , − , ? } d\in\{+,-,?\} after observing transformed history h T h_{T} . Let ℚ ξ ​ ( v , d ​ h T ) \mathbb{Q}^{\xi}(v,\mathrm{d}h_{T}) be a regular conditional distribution of ℋ T ξ \mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi} given V = v V=v under ℙ 0 ξ \mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi} . i.e., ℚ ξ ​ ( V , B ) \mathbb{Q}^{\xi}(V,B) is a version of ℙ 0 ξ ​ ( ℋ T ξ ∈ B ∣ V ) \mathbb{P}_{0}^{\xi}\left(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi}\in B\mid V\right) . By Lemma C.7 , ℙ θ ξ ​ ( V ∈ d ​ v ) = exp ⁡ ( θ ​ T 2 ​ v − T ​ θ 2 8 ) ​ ℙ 0 ξ ​ ( V ∈ d ​ v ) \mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{\theta}(V\in dv)=\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\sqrt{T}}{2}v-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right)\mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{0}(V\in dv) . Therefore, for every measurable set B B and C ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℝ ) C\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}) , ℙ θ ξ ​ ( ℋ T ξ ∈ B , V ∈ C ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}\left(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi}\in B,V\in C\right) = 𝔼 ξ 0 [ exp ( θ ​ T 2 V − T ​ θ 2 8 ) 𝟙 { ℋ T ξ ∈ B } 𝟙 { V ∈ C } ] \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\xi}_{0}\left[\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\sqrt{T}}{2}V-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi}\in B\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{V\in C\}}\right] = ∫ C exp ⁡ ( θ ​ T 2 ​ v − T ​ θ 2 8 ) ​ Q ξ ​ ( v , B ) ​ ℙ 0 ξ ​ ( V ∈ d v ) \displaystyle=\int_{C}\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\sqrt{T}}{2}v-\frac{T\theta^{2}}{8}\right)Q^{\xi}(v,B)\mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{0}(V\in\mathrm{d}v) = ∫ C Q ξ ​ ( v , B ) ​ ℙ θ ξ ​ ( V ∈ d v ) . \displaystyle=\int_{C}Q^{\xi}(v,B)\mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{\theta}(V\in\mathrm{d}v). This implies that the kernel ℚ ξ ​ ( v , d ​ h T ) \mathbb{Q}^{\xi}(v,\mathrm{d}h_{T}) is a conditional law under ℙ θ ξ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi} . Hence, for every bounded measurable f f , 𝔼 θ ξ ​ [ f ⁡ ( ℋ T ξ ) ∣ V ] = ∫ f ⁡ ( h T ) ​ Q ξ ​ ( V , d ​ h T ) ℙ θ ξ ​ -a.s., \mathbb{E}^{\xi}_{\theta}\big[f(\mathcal{H}^{\xi}_{T})\mid V\big]=\int f(h_{T})Q^{\xi}(V,\mathrm{d}h_{T})\quad\mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{\theta}\text{-a.s.,} (C.7) Define the terminal rule ψ ξ = ( ψ + ξ , ψ − ξ , ψ ? ξ ) \psi^{\xi}=(\psi_{+}^{\xi},\psi_{-}^{\xi},\psi_{?}^{\xi}) via the kernel ℚ ξ ​ ( v , d ​ h T ) \mathbb{Q}^{\xi}(v,\mathrm{d}h_{T}) as follows: ψ d ξ ​ ( v ) := ∫ ψ ~ d ξ ​ ( h T ) ​ ℚ ξ ​ ( v , d ​ h T ) , d ∈ { + , − , ? } . \psi_{d}^{\xi}(v):=\int\widetilde{\psi}_{d}^{\xi}(h_{T})\,\mathbb{Q}^{\xi}(v,\mathrm{d}h_{T}),\qquad d\in\{+,-,?\}. By definition, ψ ( ⋅ ) ξ ​ ( v ) \psi_{(\cdot)}^{\xi}(v) is a probability distribution on { + , − , ? } \{+,-,?\} . For every signed-gap realization θ \theta and every decision d ∈ { + , − , ? } d\in\{+,-,?\} , taking f = ψ ~ d ξ f=\widetilde{\psi}_{d}^{\xi} in ( C.7 ), the tower property guarantees: ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ V = d ) = 𝔼 θ ξ ​ [ ψ d ξ ​ ( V ) ] = 𝔼 θ ξ ​ [ 𝔼 θ ξ ​ [ ψ ~ d ξ ​ ( ℋ T ξ ) ∣ V ] ] = 𝔼 θ ξ ​ [ ψ ~ d ξ ​ ( ℋ T ξ ) ] = ℙ θ ξ ​ ( a ^ = d ) . \mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}^{V}=d)=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}^{\xi}[\psi_{d}^{\xi}(V)]=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}^{\xi}\big[\mathbb{E}_{\theta}^{\xi}[\widetilde{\psi}_{d}^{\xi}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi})\mid V]\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}^{\xi}[\widetilde{\psi}_{d}^{\xi}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\xi})]=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\xi}(\widehat{a}=d). Thus, the V V -based rule preserves all probabilities under every realization of the signed-gap θ \theta . ∎ C.4 The Resulting Bayesian Flat Sign Experiment We are now ready to express the abstention and undetected error probability by integrating over the local subprior Q ρ Q_{\rho} . Recall from Lemma C.3 that on the local coordinate space, the subprior measure factors as in ( C.4 ). Under the measure Q ρ Q_{\rho} , conditional on ( Ξ , Δ ) (\Xi,\Delta) , the two signs have equal mass. The unnormalized marginal measure of the gap is Q ρ ​ ( Δ ∈ d ​ δ ) = c ρ ​ d ​ δ Q_{\rho}(\Delta\in\mathrm{d}\delta)=c_{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\delta , where c ρ = 2 ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝒞 ρ ) c_{\rho}=2\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{C}_{\rho}) . Define the rescaled gaps: G := Δ ​ T 2 G:=\frac{\Delta\sqrt{T}}{2} and L T := δ ρ ​ T 2 L_{T}:=\frac{\delta_{\rho}\sqrt{T}}{2} . Under Q ρ Q_{\rho} , the support of the rescaled gap G G is [ 0 , L T ] [0,L_{T}] . For any realization G = g G=g on the interval [ 0 , L T ] [0,L_{T}] , the corresponding absolute gap is δ = 2 ​ g T \delta=\frac{2g}{\sqrt{T}} , yielding the differential change of variables as follows: d ​ δ = 2 T ​ d ​ g . \mathrm{d}\delta=\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\,\mathrm{d}g. Applying Lemma C.6 , the conditional distribution of V V given the rescaled gap and sign is Gaussian and independent of the background oracle Ξ \Xi , namely, V | ( G = g , H = h , Ξ = ξ ) ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( h ​ g , 1 ) . V\mid(G=g,H=h,\Xi=\xi)\sim\mathcal{N}(hg,1). (C.8) This reduces the given experiment to one that has the following properties under Q ρ Q_{\rho} : • The unnormalized marginal density of G G is constant on [ 0 , L T ] [0,L_{T}] (and zero elsewhere). • H ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } H\in\{+1,-1\} is uniform. • V | ( G = g , H = h ) ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( h ​ g , 1 ) V\mid(G=g,H=h)\sim\mathcal{N}(hg,1) . As established in Lemma C.8 , any terminal decision can be represented by a V V -based rule. Thus it suffices to consider rules that only depend on V V . We represent this rule via the tuple of measurable functions ψ ξ = ( ψ + ξ , ψ − ξ , ψ ? ξ ) \psi^{\xi}=(\psi_{+}^{\xi},\psi_{-}^{\xi},\psi_{?}^{\xi}) , mapping the scalar v v to the probability simplex on { + , − , ? } \{+,-,?\} . In other words, ψ + ξ ​ ( v ) + ψ − ξ ​ ( v ) + ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) = 1 . \psi_{+}^{\xi}(v)+\psi_{-}^{\xi}(v)+\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)=1. The superscript ξ \xi indicates that the decision rule is allowed to depend on the revealed background oracle ξ \xi . It follows that we can write ℙ h ​ δ ξ ​ ( a ^ V = ? ) = 𝔼 h ​ δ ξ ​ [ ψ ? ξ ​ ( V ) ] = ∫ ℝ ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ​ ϕ ​ ( v − h ​ δ ​ T 2 ) ​ 𝑑 v \mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{h\delta}\left(\widehat{a}^{V}=?\right)=\mathbb{E}^{\xi}_{h\delta}\left[\psi^{\xi}_{?}(V)\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{\xi}_{?}(v)\phi\left(v-h\frac{\delta\sqrt{T}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}v (C.9) For ease of notation, we define the unnormalized marginal density of V V corresponding to ( H = + 1 H=+1 ) and ( H = − 1 H=-1 ) sign realizations as follows: A T ​ ( v ) := ∫ 0 L T ϕ ⁡ ( v − g ) ​ 𝑑 g , and B T ​ ( v ) := ∫ 0 L T ϕ ⁡ ( v + g ) ​ 𝑑 g . A_{T}(v):=\int_{0}^{L_{T}}\phi(v-g)\,\mathrm{d}g,\quad\mbox{and}\quad B_{T}(v):=\int_{0}^{L_{T}}\phi(v+g)\,\mathrm{d}g. Then we can express the total Q ρ Q_{\rho} -abstention mass of any rule ψ \psi as: 𝒜 T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi) = ∫ ℝ K ℙ μ ​ ( a ^ V = ? ) ​ Q ρ ​ ( d μ ) \displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(\widehat{a}^{V}=?\right)Q_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\mu) = ( a ) ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 0 δ ρ ℙ h ​ δ ξ ​ ( a ^ V = ? ) ​ d δ ​ Λ ρ ​ ( d ξ ) \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int^{\delta_{\rho}}_{0}\mathbb{P}^{\xi}_{h\delta}\left(\widehat{a}^{V}=?\right)\,\mathrm{d}\delta\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi) = ( b ) ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 0 δ ρ ∫ ℝ ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ​ ϕ ​ ( v − h ​ δ ​ T 2 ) ​ d v ​ d δ ​ Λ ρ ​ ( d ξ ) \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int^{\delta_{\rho}}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{\xi}_{?}(v)\phi\left(v-h\frac{\delta\sqrt{T}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\delta\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi) = ( c ) ∫ 𝒞 ρ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) ​ ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 0 L T ( ∫ ℝ ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ​ ϕ ​ ( v − h ​ g ) ​ 𝑑 v ) ​ 2 T ​ 𝑑 g \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int_{0}^{L_{T}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\phi(v-hg)\,\mathrm{d}v\right)\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\,\mathrm{d}g = 2 T ​ ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∫ ℝ ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ​ [ A T ​ ( v ) + B T ​ ( v ) ] ​ d v ​ Λ ρ ​ ( d ξ ) , \displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\bigl[A_{T}(v)+B_{T}(v)\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi), where ( a ) (a) is due to the fair sign property and factorization of Lemma C.3 , ( b ) (b) is due to ( C.9 ) and ( c ) (c) is due to change of variables with d ​ δ = 2 T ​ d ​ g . \mathrm{d}\delta=\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\,\mathrm{d}g. Similarly, an undetected error occurs when the rule outputs − - when H = + 1 H=+1 , or outputs + + when H = − 1 H=-1 . Hence the Q ρ Q_{\rho} -undetected error mass can be expressed as: ℰ T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) = 2 T ​ ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∫ ℝ [ ψ − ξ ​ ( v ) ​ A T ​ ( v ) + ψ + ξ ​ ( v ) ​ B T ​ ( v ) ] ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) . \mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\psi_{-}^{\xi}(v)A_{T}(v)+\psi_{+}^{\xi}(v)B_{T}(v)\right]\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi). C.5 Solving the Bayesian Flat Sign Experiment We are now ready to solve the core optimization problem: establishing a lower bound on the undetected error probability ℰ T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) \mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi) for any terminal decision rule ψ \psi that satisfies the abstention budget 𝒜 T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)\leq\alpha . Lemma C.9 (Pointwise Optimal Sign Decision) . For every terminal decision rule ψ \psi , the undetected error mass is bounded below as: ℰ T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) ≥ 2 T ​ ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∫ ℝ ( 1 − ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ) ​ min ⁡ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) . \mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)\geq\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(1-\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\bigr)\min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi). Proof. Fix ( ξ , v ) (\xi,v) . Since ψ + ξ ​ ( v ) + ψ − ξ ​ ( v ) = 1 − ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) \psi_{+}^{\xi}(v)+\psi_{-}^{\xi}(v)=1-\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v) , and both ψ + ξ ​ ( v ) \psi_{+}^{\xi}(v) and ψ − ξ ​ ( v ) \psi_{-}^{\xi}(v) are non-negative, ψ − ξ ​ ( v ) ​ A T ​ ( v ) + ψ + ξ ​ ( v ) ​ B T ​ ( v ) ≥ ( ψ − ξ ​ ( v ) + ψ + ξ ​ ( v ) ) ​ min ​ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } = ( 1 − ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ) ​ min ​ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } . \psi_{-}^{\xi}(v)A_{T}(v)+\psi_{+}^{\xi}(v)B_{T}(v)\geq\bigl(\psi_{-}^{\xi}(v)+\psi_{+}^{\xi}(v)\bigr)\min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}=\bigl(1-\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\bigr)\min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}. Integrating over v v and ξ \xi proves the claim. ∎ Lemma C.10 (Uniform Central Bound) . Fix η ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \eta\in(0,1) . For all sufficiently large T T , the following bounds hold for all | v | ≤ L T 2 |v|\leq\frac{L_{T}}{2} : A T ​ ( v ) + B T ​ ( v ) ≥ 1 − η , and min ⁡ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } ≥ ( 1 − η ) ​ Φ ​ ( − | v | ) . A_{T}(v)+B_{T}(v)\geq 1-\eta,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}\geq(1-\eta)\Phi(-|v|). Proof. First, evaluating the integrals of the standard normal density ϕ \phi yields: A T ​ ( v ) = ∫ 0 L T ϕ ⁡ ( v − g ) ​ 𝑑 g = Φ ⁡ ( v ) − Φ ⁡ ( v − L T ) , and ​ B T ​ ( v ) = ∫ 0 L T ϕ ⁡ ( v + g ) ​ 𝑑 g = Φ ⁡ ( v + L T ) − Φ ⁡ ( v ) . A_{T}(v)=\int_{0}^{L_{T}}\phi(v-g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\Phi(v)-\Phi(v-L_{T}),\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;B_{T}(v)=\int_{0}^{L_{T}}\phi(v+g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\Phi(v+L_{T})-\Phi(v). Adding these yields: A T ​ ( v ) + B T ​ ( v ) = Φ ⁡ ( v + L T ) − Φ ⁡ ( v − L T ) . A_{T}(v)+B_{T}(v)=\Phi(v+L_{T})-\Phi(v-L_{T}). For | v | ≤ L T / 2 |v|\leq L_{T}/2 , this domain restriction implies v + L T ≥ L T / 2 v+L_{T}\geq L_{T}/2 and v − L T ≤ − L T / 2 v-L_{T}\leq-L_{T}/2 . Since Φ \Phi is strictly increasing, we can lower bound the sum by evaluating it at these extremes: A T ( v ) + B T ( v ) ≥ Φ ( L T / 2 ) − Φ ( − L T / 2 ) = 1 − 2 Φ ( − L T / 2 ) . A_{T}(v)+B_{T}(v)\geq\Phi(L_{T}/2)-\Phi(-L_{T}/2)=1-2\Phi(-L_{T}/2). As T → ∞ T\to\infty , L T → ∞ L_{T}\to\infty , so 1 − 2 Φ ( − L T / 2 ) → 1 1-2\Phi(-L_{T}/2)\to 1 . Thus, for sufficiently large T T , the sum is bounded below by 1 − η 1-\eta . For the second inequality, observe that ϕ \phi is symmetric and strictly decreasing away from zero. Thus, the relationship between A T ​ ( v ) A_{T}(v) and B T ​ ( v ) B_{T}(v) depends only on the sign of v v . For v ≥ 0 v\geq 0 , the integration interval for A T ​ ( v ) A_{T}(v) is [ v − L T , v ] [v-L_{T},v] , which is closer to the origin than the interval [ v , v + L T ] [v,v+L_{T}] for B T ​ ( v ) B_{T}(v) . Hence, A T ​ ( v ) ≥ B T ​ ( v ) A_{T}(v)\geq B_{T}(v) , yielding: min ⁡ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } = B T ​ ( v ) = Φ ⁡ ( v + L T ) − Φ ⁡ ( v ) = Φ ⁡ ( − v ) − Φ ⁡ ( − ( v + L T ) ) \min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}=B_{T}(v)=\Phi(v+L_{T})-\Phi(v)=\Phi(-v)-\Phi(-(v+L_{T})) since Φ ⁡ ( x ) = 1 − Φ ⁡ ( − x ) \Phi(x)=1-\Phi(-x) . By identical symmetric reasoning for v &lt; 0 v&lt;0 , we find min ⁡ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } = A T ​ ( v ) \min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}=A_{T}(v) . Consolidating both cases yields a single identity for all v v : min ⁡ { A T ​ ( v ) , B T ​ ( v ) } = Φ ⁡ ( − | v | ) − Φ ⁡ ( − ( | v | + L T ) ) . \min\{A_{T}(v),B_{T}(v)\}=\Phi(-|v|)-\Phi(-(|v|+L_{T})). (C.10) We wish to show that Φ ⁡ ( − | v | ) − Φ ⁡ ( − ( | v | + L T ) ) ≥ ( 1 − η ) ​ Φ ​ ( − | v | ) \Phi(-|v|)-\Phi(-(|v|+L_{T}))\geq(1-\eta)\Phi(-|v|) , which is equivalent to bounding the ratio: Φ ⁡ ( − ( | v | + L T ) ) Φ ⁡ ( − | v | ) ≤ η . \frac{\Phi(-(|v|+L_{T}))}{\Phi(-|v|)}\leq\eta. Since 0 ≤ | v | ≤ L T / 2 0\leq|v|\leq L_{T}/2 , the numerator is bounded above by Φ ⁡ ( − L T ) \Phi(-L_{T}) and the denominator is bounded below by Φ ( − L T / 2 ) \Phi(-L_{T}/2) . Therefore: Φ ⁡ ( − ( | v | + L T ) ) Φ ⁡ ( − | v | ) ≤ Φ ⁡ ( − L T ) Φ ( − L T / 2 ) → 0 \frac{\Phi(-(|v|+L_{T}))}{\Phi(-|v|)}\leq\frac{\Phi(-L_{T})}{\Phi(-L_{T}/2)}\to 0 uniformly as T → ∞ T\to\infty . Thus, the bound holds for sufficiently large T T . ∎ To complete the lower bound, we use the following specialized consequence of the Bathtub Principle (cf. Theorem 1.14 of 22 ). The following lemma states the form of this principle needed for our subsequent analysis. Lemma C.11 (Abstention Rearrangement / Bathtub Principle) . Let B &gt; 0 B&gt;0 , let Λ \Lambda be a finite measure on a space 𝒪 \mathcal{O} , and let w ⁡ ( v ) ≥ 0 w(v)\geq 0 be an even function that is non-increasing in | v | |v| on [ − B , B ] [-B,B] . Let r : 𝒪 × ℝ → [ 0 , 1 ] r:\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,1] be measurable. If there exists some a ∈ [ 0 , B ] a\in[0,B] such that: ∫ 𝒪 ∫ − B B r ⁡ ( ξ , v ) ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) ≤ 2 ​ a ​ Λ ​ ( 𝒪 ) , \int_{\mathcal{O}}\int_{-B}^{B}r(\xi,v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda(\mathrm{d}\xi)\leq 2a\,\Lambda(\mathcal{O}), then the complementary integral is bounded below as: ∫ 𝒪 ∫ − B B ( 1 − r ⁡ ( ξ , v ) ) ​ w ​ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) ≥ 2 ​ Λ ​ ( 𝒪 ) ​ ∫ a B w ⁡ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \int_{\mathcal{O}}\int_{-B}^{B}(1-r(\xi,v))w(v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda(\mathrm{d}\xi)\geq 2\,\Lambda(\mathcal{O})\,\int_{a}^{B}w(u)\,\mathrm{d}u. Proof. Define the central cylinder set C := 𝒪 × { v : | v | &lt; a } C:=\mathcal{O}\times\{v:|v|&lt;a\} . The measure of C C is 2 ​ a ​ Λ ​ ( 𝒪 ) 2a\,\Lambda(\mathcal{O}) . Because w ⁡ ( v ) w(v) is non-increasing in | v | |v| , we have w ⁡ ( v ) ≥ w ⁡ ( a ) w(v)\geq w(a) on C C and w ⁡ ( v ) ≤ w ⁡ ( a ) w(v)\leq w(a) on C c C^{c} . Thus, the integrand ( 𝟙 C ​ ( ξ , v ) − r ⁡ ( ξ , v ) ) ​ ( w ⁡ ( v ) − w ⁡ ( a ) ) \bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{C}(\xi,v)-r(\xi,v)\bigr)\bigl(w(v)-w(a)\bigr) is non-negative everywhere. Consequently: 0 ≤ ∫ 𝒪 × ( − B , B ) ( 𝟙 C − r ) ​ ( w − w ⁡ ( a ) ) . 0\leq\int_{\mathcal{O}\times(-B,B)}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{C}-r\bigr)\bigl(w-w(a)\bigr). Expanding this inequality gives: ∫ 𝒪 × ( − B , B ) r ​ w ≤ ∫ C w − w ⁡ ( a ) ​ ( ∫ C 1 − ∫ 𝒪 × ( − B , B ) r ) ≤ ∫ C w , \int_{\mathcal{O}\times(-B,B)}rw\leq\int_{C}w-w(a)\left(\int_{C}1-\int_{\mathcal{O}\times(-B,B)}r\right)\leq\int_{C}w, where the final step follows because the assumption ∫ 𝒪 × ( − B , B ) r ≤ 2 ​ a ​ Λ ​ ( 𝒪 ) = ∫ C 1 \int_{\mathcal{O}\times(-B,B)}r\leq 2a\,\Lambda(\mathcal{O})=\int_{C}1 forces the bracketed term to be non-negative. Therefore: ∫ 𝒪 × ( − B , B ) ( 1 − r ) ​ w ≥ ∫ 𝒪 × ( − B , B ) w − ∫ C w = ∫ C c w = 2 ​ Λ ​ ( 𝒪 ) ​ ∫ a B w ⁡ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \int_{\mathcal{O}\times(-B,B)}(1-r)w\geq\int_{\mathcal{O}\times(-B,B)}w-\int_{C}w=\int_{C^{c}}w=2\ \Lambda(\mathcal{O})\ \int_{a}^{B}w(u)\,\mathrm{d}u. ∎ Lemma C.12 (Flat Bayesian Sign Lower Bound) . Fix η ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \eta\in(0,1) and suppose α &lt; ( 1 − η ) ​ c ρ ​ δ ρ 4 \alpha&lt;\frac{(1-\eta)c_{\rho}\delta_{\rho}}{4} . Define the threshold parameter: a T , η := α ​ T 2 ​ ( 1 − η ) ​ c ρ . a_{T,\eta}:=\frac{\alpha\sqrt{T}}{2(1-\eta)c_{\rho}}. (C.11) Then, for every terminal rule ψ \psi satisfying 𝒜 T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)\leq\alpha , we have that as T → ∞ T\to\infty : ℰ T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) ≥ ( 1 − o T ​ ( 1 ) ) ​ 2 ​ ( 1 − η ) ​ c ρ T ​ ∫ a T , η ∞ Φ ⁡ ( − u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)\geq(1-o_{T}(1))\frac{2(1-\eta)c_{\rho}}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{a_{T,\eta}}^{\infty}\Phi(-u)\,\mathrm{d}u. Proof. By Lemma C.10 , for all sufficiently large T T , A T ​ ( v ) + B T ​ ( v ) ≥ 1 − η A_{T}(v)+B_{T}(v)\geq 1-\eta on the interval | v | ≤ L T / 2 |v|\leq L_{T}/2 . Given the abstention constraint 𝒜 T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)\leq\alpha , we can further restrict the integration domain to this interval [ − L T / 2 , L T / 2 ] [-L_{T}/2,L_{T}/2] to obtain: 2 T ​ ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∫ | v | ≤ L T / 2 ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ​ [ A T ​ ( v ) + B T ​ ( v ) ] ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) ≤ α . \frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\int_{|v|\leq L_{T}/2}\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\bigl[A_{T}(v)+B_{T}(v)\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\leq\alpha. Substituting the uniform central bound (first bound in Lemma C.10 ) yields: ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∫ | v | ≤ L T / 2 ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) ≤ α ​ T 2 ​ ( 1 − η ) . \int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\int_{|v|\leq L_{T}/2}\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\leq\frac{\alpha\sqrt{T}}{2(1-\eta)}. Because Λ ρ ​ ( 𝒞 ρ ) = c ρ / 2 \Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{C}_{\rho})=c_{\rho}/2 , the right-hand side is 2 ​ a T , η ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝒞 ρ ) 2a_{T,\eta}\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{C}_{\rho}) . Next, we evaluate the undetected error mass. Discarding the non-negative contribution from | v | &gt; L T / 2 |v|&gt;L_{T}/2 and applying Lemma C.9 and the second bound of Lemma C.10 , we obtain: ℰ T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ) ≥ 2 ​ ( 1 − η ) T ​ ∫ 𝒞 ρ ∫ | v | ≤ L T / 2 ( 1 − ψ ? ξ ​ ( v ) ) ​ Φ ​ ( − | v | ) ​ 𝑑 v ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝑑 ξ ) . \mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi)\geq\frac{2(1-\eta)}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho}}\int_{|v|\leq L_{T}/2}\bigl(1-\psi_{?}^{\xi}(v)\bigr)\Phi(-|v|)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi). Let π \pi be any policy satisfying the global abstention budget 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha . By Lemma C.5 and Lemma C.8 , the original strategy π \pi induces a V V -based terminal rule ψ ξ \psi^{\xi} in the experiment with background oracle revealed. This reduction preserves the abstention probability and ensures the induced sign-rule error is no larger than the original undetected error. Therefore 𝒜 T Q ρ ​ ( ψ ξ ) ≤ α . \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho}}(\psi^{\xi})\leq\alpha. Because the abstention constraint is satisfied, we may apply Lemma C.12 . Let ℐ ⊆ ℝ \mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R} be an open interval, called the quality parameter space . For each arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , let μ i ∈ ℐ \mu_{i}\in\mathcal{I} denote its quality parameter . Define the best arm by a ⋆ := arg ⁡ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ μ i a^{\star}:=\arg\max_{i\in[K]}\mu_{i} . When arm i i is sampled, its reward distribution P μ i P_{\mu_{i}} belongs to a one-parameter family { P μ : μ ∈ ℐ } \{P_{\mu}:\mu\in\mathcal{I}\} . See 4.1 Under Assumption 4.1 , the Fisher information for the quality parameter μ \mu is I ⁡ ( μ ) = A ′′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ ) ) ​ { θ ′ ​ ( μ ) } 2 . I(\mu)=A^{\prime\prime}(\theta(\mu))\{\theta^{\prime}(\mu)\}^{2}. Thus I ⁡ ( μ ) &gt; 0 I(\mu)&gt;0 , and I I is bounded away from zero and infinity on every compact subset of ℐ \mathcal{I} . Fix μ ∘ ∈ ℐ \mu_{\circ}\in\mathcal{I} , and define the Fisher–Rao information coordinate s ⁡ ( μ ) := ∫ μ ∘ μ I ⁡ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v , s(\mu):=\int_{\mu_{\circ}}^{\mu}\sqrt{I(v)}\,\mathrm{d}v, where μ ∘ ∈ ℐ \mu_{\circ}\in\mathcal{I} is arbitrary. This is the arc-length coordinate induced by the Fisher–Rao metric ( 25 ; 1 ) . We remark that all our results are independent of the choice of μ ∘ \mu_{\circ} . Indeed, if another base point μ ~ ∘ ∈ ℐ \widetilde{\mu}_{\circ}\in\mathcal{I} is used, and s ~ ​ ( μ ) := ∫ μ ~ ∘ μ I ⁡ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v \widetilde{s}(\mu):=\int_{\widetilde{\mu}_{\circ}}^{\mu}\sqrt{I(v)}\,\mathrm{d}v , then s ~ ​ ( μ ) = s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( μ ~ ∘ ) \widetilde{s}(\mu)=s(\mu)-s(\widetilde{\mu}_{\circ}) . Thus all transformed arm qualities are shifted by the same constant. Consequently, the ordering of arms, all pairwise information-coordinate gaps, and the event of a near top-two tie are unchanged. The interval ℋ \mathcal{H} , the densities f ¯ i \bar{f}_{i} , and the distribution functions F ¯ i \bar{F}_{i} are merely translated, so the integral defining κ \kappa is invariant under the corresponding change of variables. The quadratic KL condition below is also unchanged because it depends only on differences s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) s(\mu)-s(z) . Also let η i := s ⁡ ( μ i ) \eta_{i}:=s(\mu_{i}) and β ⁡ ( η ) := θ ⁡ ( s − 1 ​ ( η ) ) \beta(\eta):=\theta(s^{-1}(\eta)) . Note θ ⁡ ( μ ) = β ⁡ ( s ⁡ ( μ ) ) \theta(\mu)=\beta(s(\mu)) . Since s s is strictly increasing, the best arm is unchanged by the transformation μ i ↦ η i \mu_{i}\mapsto\eta_{i} . See 4.2 Define the information-scaled top-two tie density κ := ∑ i ≠ j ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F ¯ k ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \kappa:=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{F}_{k}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u. (F.1) Equivalently, if f i f_{i} and F i F_{i} respectively denote the prior density and distribution function of μ i \mu_{i} in the original quality coordinate system, then κ = ∑ i ≠ j ∫ ℐ f i ​ ( μ ) ​ f j ​ ( μ ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F k ​ ( μ ) I ⁡ ( μ ) ​ 𝑑 μ \kappa=\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathcal{I}}\frac{f_{i}(\mu)f_{j}(\mu)\prod_{k\neq i,j}{F_{k}(\mu)}}{\sqrt{I(\mu)}}\mathrm{d}\mu Thus κ \kappa is the density at zero of the top-two gap after transforming to the Fisher–Rao information coordinate. Note that under Assumption 4.2 , 0 &lt; κ &lt; ∞ 0&lt;\kappa&lt;\infty . Remark F.1 . We note that, each arm has 3 (equivalent) descriptions: μ ↔ η := s ⁡ ( μ ) ↔ θ := θ ⁡ ( μ ) := β ⁡ ( η ) , \mu\leftrightarrow\eta:=s(\mu)\leftrightarrow\theta:=\theta(\mu):=\beta(\eta), where 1. μ \mu is the actual parameter of an arm, the value drawn from the prior. It is the quality (parameter) coordinate in which the decision problem is defined: a ∗ = argmax i μ i a^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{i}\mu_{i} , and μ \mu measures the quality of the arms. 2. θ ⁡ ( μ ) \theta(\mu) is the natural (parameter) coordinate . It is the coordinate in which the reward distribution has exponential-family form. 3. η \eta is the Fisher–Rao information (parameter) coordinate . It rescales the quality coordinate so that local Fisher information is equal to one. Note that in the Gaussian case, with μ ∘ = 0 \mu_{\circ}=0 , we have μ = s ⁡ ( μ ) = θ ⁡ ( μ ) \mu=s(\mu)=\theta(\mu) . For the achievability result we also assume a global quadratic lower bound on the Kullback–Leibler divergence. For u , v ∈ ℐ u,v\in\mathcal{I} , let D ( u , v ) := D ( P u ∥ P v ) = { θ ( u ) − θ ( v ) } A ′ ( θ ( u ) ) − A ( θ ( u ) ) + A ( θ ( v ) ) . D(u,v):=D(P_{u}\,\|\,P_{v})=\{\theta(u)-\theta(v)\}A^{\prime}(\theta(u))-A(\theta(u))+A(\theta(v)). See 4.3 The assumption holds for several common one-parameter families when s ⁡ ( ⋅ ) s(\cdot) is chosen as the information coordinate. In particular, Gaussian location, Bernoulli, and Poisson satisfy ( 4.2 ) with c KL = 1 / 4 c_{\rm KL}=1/4 . Assumption 4.3 can be viewed as a global version of the local quadratic approximation D ⁡ ( μ , z ) ≈ 1 2 ​ d FR ​ ( μ , z ) 2 D(\mu,z)\approx\frac{1}{2}d_{\mathrm{FR}}(\mu,z)^{2} where d FR d_{\mathrm{FR}} is the Fisher–Rao distance; cf. 1 . Moreover, when δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 , the sign is uniquely determined by h = sign ⁡ ( η i − η j ) h=\operatorname{sign}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j}) . The two sign representations overlap only when δ = 0 \delta=0 , which is a Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} -null set. For any Borel set B ⊆ ℋ K B\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{K} , substituting the definition of Λ ρ \Lambda_{\rho} in ( F.5 ) into ( F.9 ) gives Q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( B ) = ∑ i &lt; j ∑ h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } ∫ 𝒞 ρ , i ​ j ∫ 0 δ ¯ 𝟙 B ​ ( η ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) ​ ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f ¯ k ​ ( ζ k ) ​ d δ ​ d u ​ d ​ ζ − i ​ j . \displaystyle\bar{Q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(B)=\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{h\in\{+1,-1\}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\rho,ij}}\int_{0}^{\bar{\delta}}\mathbbm{1}_{B}\bigl(\eta(\xi,\delta,h)\bigr)(1-\rho)\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{f}_{k}(\zeta_{k})\,\mathrm{d}\delta\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij}. For each fixed pair and sign, apply change-of-variables with ( u , δ , ζ − i ​ j ) ⟼ η ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) (u,\delta,\zeta_{-ij})\longmapsto\eta(\xi,\delta,h) . The absolute Jacobian determinant is J i ​ j ​ h ​ ( u , δ ) J_{ijh}(u,\delta) . It follows that Q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ \bar{Q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} has Lebesgue density q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ \bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} satisfying q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( η ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ) = ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f ¯ k ​ ( ζ k ) J i ​ j ​ h ​ ( u , δ ) \bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\bigl(\eta(\xi,\delta,h)\bigr)=\frac{(1-\rho)\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\displaystyle\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{f}_{k}(\zeta_{k})}{J_{ijh}(u,\delta)} for Lebesgue-almost every η ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ∈ 𝒮 i , j δ ¯ \eta(\xi,\delta,h)\in\mathcal{S}_{i,j}^{\bar{\delta}} . Furthermore, the definition ( F.9 ) implies Q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( ℋ K ∖ 𝒮 ρ δ ¯ ) = 0 \bar{Q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\bigl(\mathcal{H}^{K}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{\bar{\delta}}\bigr)=0 . Hence q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( η ) = 0 \bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\eta)=0 for Lebesgue-almost every η ∉ 𝒮 ρ δ ¯ \eta\notin\mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{\bar{\delta}} . For η = η ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) ∈ 𝒮 i , j δ ¯ \eta=\eta(\xi,\delta,h)\in\mathcal{S}_{i,j}^{\bar{\delta}} , Lemma F.1 gives ( 1 − ρ ) ​ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ≤ f ¯ i ​ ( η i ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( η j ) ​ J i ​ j ​ h ​ ( u , δ ) . (1-\rho)\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\leq\bar{f}_{i}(\eta_{i})\bar{f}_{j}(\eta_{j})J_{ijh}(u,\delta). Since J i ​ j ​ h ​ ( u , δ ) &gt; 0 J_{ijh}(u,\delta)&gt;0 and η k = ζ k \eta_{k}=\zeta_{k} for k ≠ i , j k\neq i,j , q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( η ) ≤ f ¯ i ​ ( η i ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( η j ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f ¯ k ​ ( η k ) = p ¯ ​ ( η ) . \displaystyle\bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\eta)\leq\bar{f}_{i}(\eta_{i})\bar{f}_{j}(\eta_{j})\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{f}_{k}(\eta_{k})=\bar{p}(\eta). The same inequality holds outside 𝒮 ρ δ ¯ \mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{\bar{\delta}} because q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ = 0 \bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}=0 there. Thus q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ≤ p ¯ \bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\leq\bar{p} Lebesgue-almost everywhere on ℋ K \mathcal{H}^{K} . Consequently, for every Borel set B ⊆ ℋ K B\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{K} , Q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( B ) = ∫ B q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η ≤ ∫ B p ¯ ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η = P ¯ ​ ( B ) . \bar{Q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(B)=\int_{B}\bar{q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\leq\int_{B}\bar{p}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta=\bar{P}(B). Finally, for every Borel set E ⊆ ( ℐ ) K E\subseteq(\mathcal{I})^{K} , Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( E ) = Q ¯ ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( s ⁡ ( E ) ) ≤ P ¯ ​ ( s ⁡ ( E ) ) = P ⁡ ( E ) . \displaystyle Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(E)=\bar{Q}_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\bigl(s(E)\bigr)\leq\bar{P}\bigl(s(E)\bigr)=P(E). Hence Q ρ , δ ¯ ≤ P Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\leq P . ∎ Lemma F.3 (Properties of Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} ) . Except on the Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} -null tie set { Δ = 0 } \{\Delta=0\} , let Ξ \Xi , Δ \Delta , and H H denote the unique background oracle, local gap coordinate, and sign associated with the representation μ = μ ⁡ ( Ξ , Δ , H ) \mu=\mu(\Xi,\Delta,H) . Then Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( Ξ ∈ d ​ ξ , Δ ∈ d ​ δ , H = h ) = Λ ρ ​ ( d ​ ξ ) ​ d ​ δ , h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } . Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\left(\Xi\in\mathrm{d}\xi,\,\Delta\in\mathrm{d}\delta,\,H=h\right)=\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\delta,\qquad h\in\{+1,-1\}. (F.10) Consequently, Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( H = + 1 ∣ Ξ = ξ , Δ = δ ) = Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( H = − 1 ∣ Ξ = ξ , Δ = δ ) = 1 2 , Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\bigl(H=+1\mid\Xi=\xi,\Delta=\delta\bigr)=Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\bigl(H=-1\mid\Xi=\xi,\Delta=\delta\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}, and Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( Δ ∈ d ​ δ ) = c ρ ​ d ​ δ , 0 ≤ δ ≤ δ ¯ . Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\Delta\in\mathrm{d}\delta)=c_{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\delta,\qquad 0\leq\delta\leq\bar{\delta}. In particular, Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( ( ℐ ) K ) = c ρ ​ δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}((\mathcal{I})^{K})=c_{\rho}\bar{\delta} . Proof. The uniqueness of the pair follows from the strict top-two property in Lemma F.1 . When δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 , the sign is determined by h = sign ⁡ ( η i − η j ) h=\operatorname{sign}(\eta_{i}-\eta_{j}) , and the center and remaining coordinates are recovered from the inverse transformation given above. The two sign representations coincide only when δ = 0 \delta=0 , which is a Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} -null set. Define the coordinates arbitrarily on this null set. For Borel sets C ⊆ 𝒞 ρ C\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\rho} and D ⊆ [ 0 , δ ¯ ] D\subseteq[0,\bar{\delta}] , the definition ( F.9 ) gives Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( Ξ ∈ C , Δ ∈ D , H = h ) = ∫ C ∫ D d ​ δ ​ Λ ρ ​ ( d ξ ) = Λ ρ ​ ( C ) ​ Leb ⁡ ( D ) . \displaystyle Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\bigl(\Xi\in C,\,\Delta\in D,\,H=h\bigr)=\int_{C}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}\delta\,\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathrm{d}\xi)=\Lambda_{\rho}(C)\operatorname{Leb}(D). This proves ( F.10 ). Since the two signs have the same mass conditional on ( Ξ , Δ ) (\Xi,\Delta) , integrating over ξ \xi and summing over the two signs gives Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( Δ ∈ d ​ δ ) = 2 ​ Λ ρ ​ ( 𝒞 ρ ) ​ d ​ δ = c ρ ​ d ​ δ . Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\Delta\in\mathrm{d}\delta)=2\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{C}_{\rho})\,\mathrm{d}\delta=c_{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\delta. ∎ Lemma F.4 (Probability Domination Under Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} ) . For any policy π \pi , define the abstention mass 𝒜 T Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( π ) := ∫ ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) ​ Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}}(\pi):=\int\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?)\,Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\mathrm{d}\mu) and the undetected error mass ℰ T Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( π ) := ∫ ℙ μ π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( μ ) , ? } ) ​ Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( 𝑑 μ ) . \mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}}(\pi):=\int\mathbb{P}_{\mu}^{\pi}\bigl(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(\mu),?\}\bigr)\,Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}(\mathrm{d}\mu). Then 𝒜 T Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( π ) ≤ 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) , ℰ T Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( π ) ≤ ℰ T ​ ( π ) . \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}}(\pi)\leq\mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{T}^{Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}}(\pi)\leq\mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi). (F.11) In particular, any policy satisfying 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha also satisfies 𝒜 T Q ρ , δ ¯ ​ ( π ) ≤ α \mathcal{A}_{T}^{Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}}(\pi)\leq\alpha . Proof. Follows directly from Q ρ , δ ¯ ≤ P Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}}\leq P . ∎ F.2.2 Step 2. Oracle Reduction Before sampling begins, we reveal the background oracle Ξ = ( I , J , U , Z − I ​ J ) \Xi=(I,J,U,Z_{-IJ}) , which depends on the random means μ \mu , to the learner. Conditional on Ξ = ξ = ( i , j , u , ζ − i ​ j ) \Xi=\xi=(i,j,u,\zeta_{-ij}) , the learner knows the identities of the local top-two arms, the local center u u , and the information-coordinate values ζ k \zeta_{k} of all background arms. Equivalently, the learner knows that each background arm k ≠ i , j k\neq i,j has quality parameter s − 1 ​ ( ζ k ) s^{-1}(\zeta_{k}) and reward distribution P s − 1 ​ ( ζ k ) P_{s^{-1}(\zeta_{k})} . The only unknown local coordinates under Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} are Δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ¯ ] , H ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } . \Delta\in[0,\bar{\delta}],\qquad H\in\{+1,-1\}. Here H = + 1 H=+1 means that arm i i is better, whereas H = − 1 H=-1 means that arm j j is better. For ease of notation, denote the probability law of the experiment conditional on Ξ \Xi , H H and Δ \Delta ℙ h , δ ξ , π := ℙ μ ⁡ ( ξ , δ , h ) π , h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } , δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ¯ ] . \mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi}:=\mathbb{P}_{\mu(\xi,\delta,h)}^{\pi},\qquad h\in\{+1,-1\},\quad\delta\in[0,\bar{\delta}]. Since any policy in the original experiment can be implemented in the oracle-revealed setting by simply ignoring the side information Ξ \Xi , any lower bound derived for this oracle-revealed experiment provides a lower bound for the original problem. Furthermore, since the background means are known, querying any arm other than i i or j j provides no information about Δ \Delta and H H . We show that we can restrict our analysis to strategies that only sample the top two arms and output the decision of the sign; we call these local pair strategies . Lemma F.5 (Sufficiency of Local Pair Strategies) . Fix a realization of the background oracle Ξ = ξ = ( i , j , u , ζ − i ​ j ) ∈ 𝒞 ρ \Xi=\xi=(i,j,u,\zeta_{-ij})\in\mathcal{C}_{\rho} . For any adaptive strategy π \pi , there exists a modified strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} that samples only arms i i and j j , restricts its terminal recommendation to a ^ T ′ ∈ { + , − , ? } \widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}\in\{+,-,?\} , and satisfies, for every δ ∈ ( 0 , δ ¯ ] \delta\in(0,\bar{\delta}] and h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } h\in\{+1,-1\} , ℙ h , δ ξ , π ′ ​ ( a ^ T ′ = ? ) = ℙ h , δ ξ , π ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) , and ℙ h , δ ξ , π ′ ​ ( a ^ T ′ = − h ) ≤ ℙ h , δ ξ , π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( h ) , ? } ) , \mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi^{\prime}}(\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=?)=\mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi^{\prime}}(\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=-h)\leq\mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(h),?\}), (F.12) where a ⋆ ​ ( + ) = i a^{\star}(+)=i and a ⋆ ​ ( − ) = j a^{\star}(-)=j . This says that the abstention probability of π ′ \pi^{\prime} is unchanged from that of π \pi and the undetected error probability of π ′ \pi^{\prime} is less than or equal to that of π \pi . Proof. Fix ξ = ( i , j , u , ζ − i ​ j ) ∈ 𝒞 ρ \xi=(i,j,u,\zeta_{-ij})\in\mathcal{C}_{\rho} . We construct the modified strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} that satisfies ( F.12 ) by internally simulating π \pi . Whenever the internal copy of π \pi samples arm k ∈ { i , j } k\in\{i,j\} , the strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} samples the same arm and feeds the observed reward to the internal copy. Whenever the internal copy samples an arm k ∉ { i , j } k\notin\{i,j\} , the strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} instead samples arm i i , discards the resulting reward, and feeds the internal copy an independent simulated reward drawn from P s − 1 ​ ( ζ k ) P_{s^{-1}(\zeta_{k})} . Conditional on Ξ = ξ \Xi=\xi , the quality parameter of every background arm k ∉ { i , j } k\notin\{i,j\} is known to be s − 1 ​ ( ζ k ) s^{-1}(\zeta_{k}) , and its reward distribution is independent of ( H , Δ ) (H,\Delta) . Therefore, for every fixed ( h , δ ) (h,\delta) , the simulated reward has the same distribution as the reward that π \pi would have observed by sampling arm k k in the original experiment. It follows inductively over the sampling rounds that the internal history observed by π \pi under π ′ \pi^{\prime} has the same distribution as the history observed by π \pi under ℙ h , δ ξ , π \mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi} . Consequently, the terminal output of the internal copy has the same distribution as a ^ T \widehat{a}_{T} under the original strategy. At time T T , the strategy π ′ \pi^{\prime} maps the terminal output of the internal copy according to i ↦ + , j ↦ − , ? ↦ ? , i\mapsto+,\qquad j\mapsto-,\qquad?\mapsto?, and maps every recommendation k ∉ { i , j } k\notin\{i,j\} arbitrarily to one of the two signs, say + + . This mapping preserves the abstention event, and hence ℙ h , δ ξ , π ′ ​ ( a ^ T ′ = ? ) = ℙ h , δ ξ , π ​ ( a ^ T = ? ) . \mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi^{\prime}}(\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=?)=\mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi}(\widehat{a}_{T}=?). Moreover, on the support of Q ρ , δ ¯ Q_{\rho,\bar{\delta}} , arms i i and j j are the unique top-two arms, with a ⋆ ​ ( + ) = i a^{\star}(+)=i and a ⋆ ​ ( − ) = j a^{\star}(-)=j . Therefore, any original recommendation k ∉ { i , j } k\notin\{i,j\} is already an undetected error. Mapping such a recommendation to a sign either preserves that error or converts it into a correct sign decision. Since the recommendations i i and j j are mapped to their corresponding signs, the mapping cannot turn a correct original recommendation into an incorrect sign decision. Thus { a ^ T ′ = − h } ⊆ { a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ( h ) , ? } } \left\{\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=-h\right\}\subseteq\left\{\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(h),?\}\right\} under the coupled construction, and consequently ℙ h , δ ξ , π ′ ​ ( a ^ T ′ = − h ) ≤ ℙ h , δ ξ , π ​ ( a ^ T ∉ { a ⋆ ​ ( h ) , ? } ) . \mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi^{\prime}}(\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{T}=-h)\leq\mathbb{P}_{h,\delta}^{\xi,\pi}\bigl(\widehat{a}_{T}\notin\{a^{\star}(h),?\}\bigr). This proves ( F.12 ). ∎ F.2.3 Step 3. Log-Likelihood Ratio and MLE Fix D &gt; 0 D&gt;0 , to be chosen later, and set δ ¯ α := D ​ α , L T := δ ¯ α ​ T 2 = D ​ α ​ T 2 . \bar{\delta}_{\alpha}:=D\alpha,\qquad L_{T}:=\frac{\bar{\delta}_{\alpha}\sqrt{T}}{2}=\frac{D\alpha\sqrt{T}}{2}. For all sufficiently small α \alpha , we have δ ¯ α ≤ δ ρ \bar{\delta}_{\alpha}\leq\delta_{\rho} . Fix a realization of the background oracle Ξ = ξ = ( i , j , u , ζ − i ​ j ) ∈ 𝒞 ρ \Xi=\xi=(i,j,u,\zeta_{-ij})\in\mathcal{C}_{\rho} and a local-pair strategy π \pi . (F.13) This definition is unambiguous at δ = 0 \delta=0 because ℙ + 1 , 0 ξ = ℙ − 1 , 0 ξ \mathbb{P}_{+1,0}^{\xi}=\mathbb{P}_{-1,0}^{\xi} . Lemma F.6 (Local Likelihood Factorization) . For every h ∈ { + 1 , − 1 } h\in\{+1,-1\} and δ ∈ [ 0 , δ ¯ α ] \delta\in[0,\bar{\delta}_{\alpha}] , let r = h ​ δ ​ T / 2 r=h\delta\sqrt{T}/2 . (F.14) Proof. Let π t ​ ( a t ∣ ξ , ω t − 1 ) \pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid\xi,\omega_{t-1}) denote the sampling kernel used by the strategy at time t t , evaluated at the realized history ω t − 1 \omega_{t-1} . (F.16) Hence ℓ T , ξ ​ ( ⋅ , ω ) \ell_{T,\xi}(\cdot;\omega) is strictly concave on [ − L T , L T ] [-L_{T},L_{T}] , and its maximizer is unique. Conclusion Let π \pi be any original adaptive policy satisfying 𝒜 T ​ ( π ) ≤ α . \mathcal{A}_{T}(\pi)\leq\alpha. Fix ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \rho\in(0,1) and ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 10 ) \varepsilon\in(0,1/10) . After revealing the background oracle and applying Lemma F.5 , the original policy induces an oracle local-pair sign rule whose abstention probability is at most α \alpha , and whose sign-error probability is no larger than the original undetected-error probability. Therefore Lemma F.11 implies ℰ T ​ ( π ) ≥ exp ⁡ { − ε ​ α 2 ​ T − o T ​ ( α 2 ​ T ) } ​ 2 ​ ( 1 − ε ) 2 ​ c ρ T ​ ∫ a T ∞ Φ ⁡ ( − u ) ​ 𝑑 u , \mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi)\geq\exp\{-\varepsilon\alpha^{2}T-o_{T}(\alpha^{2}T)\}\frac{2(1-\varepsilon)^{2}c_{\rho}}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{a_{T}}^{\infty}\Phi(-u)\,\mathrm{d}u, where a T = α ​ T 2 ​ ( 1 − 3 ​ ε ) ​ c ρ a_{T}=\frac{\alpha\sqrt{T}}{2(1-3\varepsilon)c_{\rho}} . Thus, for every feasible policy π \pi , lim inf T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T ​ ( π ) ≥ − ε − 1 8 ​ ( 1 − 3 ​ ε ) 2 ​ c ρ 2 . \liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}(\pi)\geq-\varepsilon-\frac{1}{8(1-3\varepsilon)^{2}c_{\rho}^{2}}.
