This proves the lower-bound part of Theorem 4.1 . F.3 Algorithm and Upper Bound Next we prove the matching upper bound. Recall Assumption 4.3 assumes that there exists c KL &gt; 0 c_{\rm KL}&gt;0 such that, for all μ , z ∈ ℐ \mu,z\in\mathcal{I} , D ⁡ ( μ , z ) ≥ c KL ​ { s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) } 2 . D(\mu,z)\geq c_{\rm KL}\{s(\mu)-s(z)\}^{2}. Take c ~ := min ⁡ { c KL , 1 / 4 } \widetilde{c}:=\min\{c_{\rm KL},1/4\} . Then D ⁡ ( μ , z ) ≥ c ~ ​ { s ⁡ ( μ ) − s ⁡ ( z ) } 2 , μ , z ∈ ℐ . D(\mu,z)\geq\widetilde{c}\,\{s(\mu)-s(z)\}^{2},\qquad\mu,z\in\mathcal{I}. (F.34) F.3.1 The Algorithm We now define the information-coordinate version of PGWS, IC-PGWS . As in the Gaussian algorithm in the main paper, the policy has two components. During the sampling phase, it forms empirical centers in the information coordinate and allocates samples according to information-gap weights. At the terminal time, it recommends the empirical information-coordinate leader unless the terminal certificate is below a calibrated abstention threshold. For each arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , define N i ( t ) := ∑ r = 1 t 𝟙 { A r = i } , S i ( t ) := ∑ r ≤ t : A r = i t ( X r ) . N_{i}(t):=\sum_{r=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{r}=i\},\qquad S_{i}(t):=\sum_{r\leq t:A_{r}=i}t(X_{r}). The sampling rule uses an empirical information-coordinate center. If N i ​ ( t ) &gt; 0 N_{i}(t)&gt;0 , define t ^ i ​ ( t ) := S i ​ ( t ) N i ​ ( t ) . \widehat{t}_{i}(t):=\frac{S_{i}(t)}{N_{i}(t)}. If N i ​ ( t ) &gt; 0 N_{i}(t)&gt;0 and t ^ i ​ ( t ) ∈ A ′ ​ ( Θ ) \widehat{t}_{i}(t)\in A^{\prime}(\Theta) , set θ ^ i ​ ( t ) := ( A ′ ) − 1 ​ ( t ^ i ​ ( t ) ) , η ^ i ​ ( t ) := β − 1 ​ ( θ ^ i ​ ( t ) ) , μ ^ i ​ ( t ) := s − 1 ​ ( η ^ i ​ ( t ) ) . \widehat{\theta}_{i}(t):=(A^{\prime})^{-1}\!\left(\widehat{t}_{i}(t)\right),\qquad\widehat{\eta}_{i}(t):=\beta^{-1}\!\left(\widehat{\theta}_{i}(t)\right),\qquad\widehat{\mu}_{i}(t):=s^{-1}\!\left(\widehat{\eta}_{i}(t)\right). (F.35) If N i ​ ( t ) = 0 N_{i}(t)=0 , or if t ^ i ​ ( t ) ∉ A ′ ​ ( Θ ) \widehat{t}_{i}(t)\notin A^{\prime}(\Theta) , use the deterministic fallback η ^ i ​ ( t ) := η ∘ := s ⁡ ( μ ∘ ) , μ ^ i ​ ( t ) := μ ∘ . \widehat{\eta}_{i}(t):=\eta_{\circ}:=s(\mu_{\circ}),\qquad\widehat{\mu}_{i}(t):=\mu_{\circ}. (F.36) The fallback is only a finite-time safeguard. For example, in Bernoulli rewards, the empirical natural statistic may equal 0 0 or 1 1 after only failures or only successes. Since the true parameter lies in the interior and forced exploration gives N i ​ ( t ) → ∞ N_{i}(t)\to\infty , the strong law implies t ^ i ​ ( t ) → A ′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ i ) ) ∈ A ′ ​ ( Θ ) \widehat{t}_{i}(t)\to A^{\prime}(\theta(\mu_{i}))\in A^{\prime}(\Theta) , so the fallback is used only finitely many times almost surely for each arm. At time t t , define the empirical information-coordinate leader b ^ t := arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ η ^ i ​ ( t ) , \widehat{b}_{t}:=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{i\in[K]}\widehat{\eta}_{i}(t), (F.37) with deterministic tie-breaking. For j ≠ b ^ t j\neq\widehat{b}_{t} , define the empirical information-coordinate gap Δ ^ j , s ​ ( t ) := η ^ b ^ t ​ ( t ) − η ^ j ​ ( t ) , \widehat{\Delta}_{j,s}(t):=\widehat{\eta}_{\widehat{b}_{t}}(t)-\widehat{\eta}_{j}(t), and define the empirical top-two information gap Γ ^ s , t := min j ≠ b ^ t ⁡ Δ ^ j , s ​ ( t ) . \widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t}:=\min_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{t}}\widehat{\Delta}_{j,s}(t). When Γ ^ s , t &gt; 0 \widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t}&gt;0 , set w b ^ t ​ ( t ) := 1 , w j ​ ( t ) := min ⁡ { 1 , Γ ^ s , t 2 4 ​ c ~ ​ Δ ^ j , s ​ ( t ) 2 } , j ≠ b ^ t . w_{\widehat{b}_{t}}(t):=1,\qquad w_{j}(t):=\min\left\{1,\,\frac{\widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t}^{\,2}}{4\widetilde{c}\,\widehat{\Delta}_{j,s}(t)^{2}}\right\},\qquad j\neq\widehat{b}_{t}. (F.38) The sampling probabilities are p i ​ ( t ) := { w i ​ ( t ) ∑ ℓ = 1 K w ℓ ​ ( t ) , Γ ^ s , t &gt; 0 , 1 K , Γ ^ s , t = 0 . p_{i}(t):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{w_{i}(t)}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}w_{\ell}(t)},&amp;\widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t}&gt;0,\\[10.0pt] \displaystyle\frac{1}{K},&amp;\widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t}=0.\end{cases} (F.39) The constant c ~ ≤ 1 / 4 \widetilde{c}\leq 1/4 is the one in ( F.34 ). The cap at 1 / 4 1/4 ensures that the closest empirical challenger receives the same sampling weight ( 1 1 ) as the leader. At the terminal time, set b ^ T := arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ η ^ i ​ ( T ) , \widehat{b}_{T}:=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{i\in[K]}\widehat{\eta}_{i}(T), (F.40) again with deterministic tie-breaking. Given the terminal history, for ease of notation let Π T ( ⋅ ) := ℙ ( ⋅ ∣ ℱ T ) \varPi_{T}(\cdot):=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{T}) denote the posterior distribution induced by the prior and the observed data. The terminal certificate is R T := − log ⁡ max j ≠ b ^ T ​ Π T ​ ( μ j ≥ μ b ^ T ) = − log ⁡ max j ≠ b ^ T ​ Π T ​ ( η j ≥ η b ^ T ) , R_{T}:=-\log\max_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{T}}\varPi_{T}(\mu_{j}\geq\mu_{\widehat{b}_{T}})=-\log\max_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{T}}\varPi_{T}(\eta_{j}\geq\eta_{\widehat{b}_{T}}), (F.41) where the equality follows because s s is strictly increasing. Thus R T R_{T} is large when the final leader is well separated from all challengers under the posterior, and small when at least one challenger remains plausible. To calibrate abstention, let r T , α r_{T,\alpha} be the lower α \alpha -quantile of R T R_{T} under the joint Bayesian law induced by the sampling rule: r T , α := inf { r ≥ 0 : ℙ ⁡ ( R T ≤ r ) ≥ α } . r_{T,\alpha}:=\inf\left\{r\geq 0:\mathbb{P}(R_{T}\leq r)\geq\alpha\right\}. (F.42) All probabilities in ( F.42 ) are taken before the final terminal randomization. If the distribution of R T R_{T} has an atom at the threshold, define θ T , α := { α − ℙ ⁡ ( R T &lt; r T , α ) ℙ ⁡ ( R T = r T , α ) , ℙ ⁡ ( R T = r T , α ) &gt; 0 , 0 , ℙ ⁡ ( R T = r T , α ) = 0 . \theta_{T,\alpha}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-\mathbb{P}(R_{T}&lt;r_{T,\alpha})}{\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})},&amp;\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})&gt;0,\\[12.0pt] 0,&amp;\mathbb{P}(R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha})=0.\end{cases} (F.43) The terminal decision is a ^ T = { ? , R T &lt; r T , α , ? , R T = r T , α ​ with probability ​ θ T , α , b ^ T , otherwise . \widehat{a}_{T}=\begin{cases}?,&amp;R_{T}&lt;r_{T,\alpha},\\[3.0pt] ?,&amp;R_{T}=r_{T,\alpha}\text{ with probability }\theta_{T,\alpha},\\[3.0pt] \widehat{b}_{T},&amp;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} (F.44) Algorithm 2 Information-Coordinate Posterior Gap Weighted Sampling with Abstention IC-PGWS ( α ) (\alpha) 1: Budget T T , abstention level α \alpha , functions s s , β \beta , A A , natural statistic t t , prior densities { f ¯ i } i = 1 K \{\bar{f}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K} , and a constant c ~ ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 4 ] \widetilde{c}\in(0,1/4] satisfying ( F.34 ). 2: Initialize N i ​ ( 0 ) = 0 N_{i}(0)=0 and S i ​ ( 0 ) = 0 S_{i}(0)=0 for all i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] . 3: for t = 0 , 1 , … , T − 1 t=0,1,\ldots,T-1 do 4: if there exists i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] such that N i ​ ( t ) &lt; t + 1 N_{i}(t)&lt;\sqrt{t+1} then 5: Sample one such arm, using deterministic tie-breaking. 6: else 7: Compute the empirical centers { η ^ i ​ ( t ) , μ ^ i ​ ( t ) } i = 1 K \{\widehat{\eta}_{i}(t),\widehat{\mu}_{i}(t)\}_{i=1}^{K} using ( F.35 )–( F.36 ). 8: Compute the leader b ^ t \widehat{b}_{t} , the empirical gaps { Δ ^ j , s ​ ( t ) } j ≠ b ^ t \{\widehat{\Delta}_{j,s}(t)\}_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{t}} , and Γ ^ s , t \widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t} using ( F.37 ). 9: Compute the sampling probabilities { p i ​ ( t ) } i = 1 K \{p_{i}(t)\}_{i=1}^{K} using ( F.38 )–( F.39 ). 10: Sample arm i i with probability p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) . 11: end if 12: Observe X t + 1 X_{t+1} , and update N i ​ ( t + 1 ) N_{i}(t+1) and S i ​ ( t + 1 ) S_{i}(t+1) . 13: end for 14: Compute b ^ T \widehat{b}_{T} using ( F.40 ). 15: Compute the terminal certificate R T R_{T} in ( F.41 ). 16: Output a ^ T \widehat{a}_{T} according to the abstention rule ( F.44 ). F.3.2 Upper Bound Theorem Theorem F.2 (Upper Bound for IC-PGWS ) . Under Assumptions 4.1 , 4.2 , and the global quadratic KL assumption 4.3 , IC-PGWS ​ ( α ) \textsc{IC-PGWS}(\alpha) with terminal rule ( F.44 ) satisfies, for every T T and every α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , 𝒜 T ​ ( IC-PGWS ​ ( α ) ) = α . \mathcal{A}_{T}(\textsc{IC-PGWS}(\alpha))=\alpha. Moreover, lim sup α ↓ 0 lim sup T → ∞ 1 α 2 ​ T ​ log ⁡ ℰ T ​ ( IC-PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ − 1 8 ​ κ 2 . \limsup_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}T}\log\mathcal{E}_{T}(\textsc{IC-PGWS}(\alpha))\leq-\frac{1}{8\kappa^{2}}. We prove the theorem in several steps. Throughout this proof, all probabilities are under the joint Bayesian law induced by Algorithm 2 , unless otherwise stated. Recall that η i = s ⁡ ( μ i ) , β ⁡ ( η ) = θ ⁡ ( s − 1 ​ ( η ) ) , ℋ = s ⁡ ( ℐ ) . \eta_{i}=s(\mu_{i}),\qquad\beta(\eta)=\theta(s^{-1}(\eta)),\qquad\mathcal{H}=s(\mathcal{I}). F.3.3 Error Guarantee Lemma F.12 (Error Guarantee) . For every T T and every α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , ℰ T ​ ( IC-PGWS ​ ( α ) ) ≤ ( K − 1 ) ​ e − r T , α . \mathcal{E}_{T}(\textsc{IC-PGWS}(\alpha))\leq(K-1)e^{-r_{T,\alpha}}. Proof. Condition on the terminal history ℱ T \mathcal{F}_{T} . If the algorithm recommends b ^ T \widehat{b}_{T} , then ℙ ⁡ ( a ⋆ ≠ b ^ T ∣ ℱ T ) ≤ ∑ j ≠ b ^ T Π T ​ ( μ j ≥ μ b ^ T ) . \mathbb{P}(a^{\star}\neq\widehat{b}_{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T})\leq\sum_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{T}}\varPi_{T}(\mu_{j}\geq\mu_{\widehat{b}_{T}}). By the definition of R T R_{T} , max j ≠ b ^ T ⁡ Π T ​ ( μ j ≥ μ b ^ T ) = e − R T . \max_{j\neq\widehat{b}_{T}}\varPi_{T}(\mu_{j}\geq\mu_{\widehat{b}_{T}})=e^{-R_{T}}. Therefore ℙ ⁡ ( a ⋆ ≠ b ^ T ∣ ℱ T ) ≤ ( K − 1 ) ​ e − R T . \mathbb{P}(a^{\star}\neq\widehat{b}_{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T})\leq(K-1)e^{-R_{T}}. On the event that the algorithm does not abstain, the terminal quantile rule implies R T ≥ r T , α R_{T}\geq r_{T,\alpha} . Hence ℰ T ( IC-PGWS ( α ) ) = 𝔼 [ 𝟙 { a ^ T ≠ ? } ℙ ( a ⋆ ≠ b ^ T ∣ ℱ T ) ] ≤ ( K − 1 ) e − r T , α . \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{T}(\textsc{IC-PGWS}(\alpha))=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbbm{1}\{\widehat{a}_{T}\neq?\}\mathbb{P}(a^{\star}\neq\widehat{b}_{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T})\right]\leq(K-1)e^{-r_{T,\alpha}}. ∎ F.3.4 Consistency and Allocation Convergence. We next identify the almost-sure limit of R T / T R_{T}/T . We first introduce the limiting quantities associated with a realized parameter vector. Let b ⋆ := arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ η i = arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ μ i b^{\star}:=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{i\in[K]}\eta_{i}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{i\in[K]}\mu_{i} be the true best arm. For each j ≠ b ⋆ j\neq b^{\star} , define the information coordinate gap Δ j , s := η b ⋆ − η j \Delta_{j,s}:=\eta_{b^{\star}}-\eta_{j} , and define the true top two information gap Γ s := min j ≠ b ⋆ ⁡ Δ j , s = η ( 1 ) − η ( 2 ) \Gamma_{s}:=\min_{j\neq b^{\star}}\Delta_{j,s}=\eta_{(1)}-\eta_{(2)} , where η ( 1 ) &gt; η ( 2 ) &gt; ⋯ &gt; η ( K ) \eta_{(1)}&gt;\eta_{(2)}&gt;\cdots&gt;\eta_{(K)} are the ordered information-coordinate qualities. Define the limiting unnormalized sampling weights by w b ⋆ † ​ ( η ) := 1 , w j † ​ ( η ) := min ⁡ { 1 , Γ s 2 4 ​ c ~ ​ Δ j , s 2 } , j ≠ b ⋆ . w_{b^{\star}}^{\dagger}(\eta):=1,\qquad w_{j}^{\dagger}(\eta):=\min\left\{1,\,\frac{\Gamma_{s}^{2}}{4\widetilde{c}\,\Delta_{j,s}^{2}}\right\},\qquad j\neq b^{\star}. (F.45) Let W † ​ ( η ) := ∑ ℓ = 1 K w ℓ † ​ ( η ) , p i † ​ ( η ) := w i † ​ ( η ) W † ​ ( η ) . W^{\dagger}(\eta):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}w_{\ell}^{\dagger}(\eta),\qquad p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta):=\frac{w_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)}{W^{\dagger}(\eta)}. Because c ~ ≤ 1 / 4 \widetilde{c}\leq 1/4 , every second-best arm has limiting weight one. Consequently, 2 ≤ W † ​ ( η ) ≤ K , p i † ​ ( η ) &gt; 0 for every ​ i ∈ [ K ] . 2\leq W^{\dagger}(\eta)\leq K,\qquad p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)&gt;0\quad\text{for every }i\in[K]. For each challenger j ≠ b ⋆ j\neq b^{\star} , define its pairwise error exponent C j † ​ ( μ ) := inf z ∈ [ μ j , μ b ⋆ ] { p b ⋆ † ​ ( η ) ​ D ​ ( μ b ⋆ , z ) + p j † ​ ( η ) ​ D ​ ( μ j , z ) } . The worst-case pairwise error exponent is C † ​ ( μ ) := min j ≠ b ⋆ ⁡ C j † ​ ( μ ) . C^{\dagger}(\mu):=\min_{j\neq b^{\star}}C_{j}^{\dagger}(\mu). (F.46) Equivalently, C † ​ ( μ ) = 1 W † ​ ( η ) ​ min ⁡ inf z ∈ [ μ j , μ b ⋆ ] j ≠ b ⋆ ⁡ { D ⁡ ( μ b ⋆ , z ) + w j † ​ ( η ) ​ D ​ ( μ j , z ) } . (F.47) We now show that the empirical centers converge to the true qualities, the empirical sampling proportions converge to p † ​ ( η ) p^{\dagger}(\eta) , and the normalized terminal certificate converges to C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) . Lemma F.13 (Information Coordinate Center Consistency) . Under Algorithm 2 , for every arm i i , η ^ i ​ ( t ) → η i and μ ^ i ​ ( t ) → μ i a.s. \widehat{\eta}_{i}(t)\to\eta_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\mu}_{i}(t)\to\mu_{i}\qquad\text{a.s.} Proof. The forced-exploration rule implies N i ​ ( t ) → ∞ a.s. for every ​ i . N_{i}(t)\to\infty\qquad\text{a.s. for every }i. Conditional on μ i \mu_{i} , the observations from arm i i are i.i.d. from P μ i P_{\mu_{i}} . Since the log-partition function is finite on an open natural-parameter space, the natural statistic has finite mean under P μ i P_{\mu_{i}} , and 𝔼 μ i ​ [ t ⁡ ( X ) ] = A ′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ i ) ) . \mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}[t(X)]=A^{\prime}(\theta(\mu_{i})). Therefore, by the strong law of large numbers, t ^ i ( t ) = 1 N i ​ ( t ) ∑ r ≤ t : A r = i t ( X r ) → A ′ ( θ ( μ i ) ) a.s. \widehat{t}_{i}(t)=\frac{1}{N_{i}(t)}\sum_{r\leq t:A_{r}=i}t(X_{r})\to A^{\prime}(\theta(\mu_{i}))\qquad\text{a.s.} Because θ ⁡ ( μ i ) ∈ Θ \theta(\mu_{i})\in\Theta and Θ \Theta is open, A ′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ i ) ) ∈ A ′ ​ ( Θ ) A^{\prime}(\theta(\mu_{i}))\in A^{\prime}(\Theta) . Hence t ^ i ​ ( t ) ∈ A ′ ​ ( Θ ) \widehat{t}_{i}(t)\in A^{\prime}(\Theta) eventually almost surely. On this eventual event, θ ^ i ​ ( t ) = ( A ′ ) − 1 ​ ( t ^ i ​ ( t ) ) → θ ⁡ ( μ i ) , \widehat{\theta}_{i}(t)=(A^{\prime})^{-1}\!\left(\widehat{t}_{i}(t)\right)\to\theta(\mu_{i}), since A ′′ &gt; 0 A^{\prime\prime}&gt;0 implies that A ′ A^{\prime} is strictly increasing and continuous. Thus η ^ i ​ ( t ) = β − 1 ​ ( θ ^ i ​ ( t ) ) → β − 1 ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ i ) ) = s ⁡ ( μ i ) = η i . \widehat{\eta}_{i}(t)=\beta^{-1}(\widehat{\theta}_{i}(t))\to\beta^{-1}(\theta(\mu_{i}))=s(\mu_{i})=\eta_{i}. Continuity of s − 1 s^{-1} gives μ ^ i ​ ( t ) = s − 1 ​ ( η ^ i ​ ( t ) ) → μ i . \widehat{\mu}_{i}(t)=s^{-1}(\widehat{\eta}_{i}(t))\to\mu_{i}. ∎ Lemma F.14 (Sampling Proportions) . Under Algorithm 2 , N i ​ ( T ) T → p i † ​ ( η ) a.s. for every ​ i ∈ [ K ] . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)\qquad\text{a.s. for every }i\in[K]. Proof. By Lemma F.13 , η ^ i ​ ( t ) → η i for every ​ i . \widehat{\eta}_{i}(t)\to\eta_{i}\qquad\text{for every }i. Since the prior densities are continuous, ties have probability zero. Thus the best arm and the second-best arm are unique almost surely. On this probability-one event, b ^ t = b ⋆ \widehat{b}_{t}=b^{\star} eventually, and Δ ^ j , s ​ ( t ) → Δ j , s , Γ ^ s , t → Γ s . \widehat{\Delta}_{j,s}(t)\to\Delta_{j,s},\qquad\widehat{\Gamma}_{s,t}\to\Gamma_{s}. Therefore w i ( t ) → w i † ( η ) , p i ( t ) → p i † ( η ) a.s. for every i . w_{i}(t)\to w_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta),\qquad p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)\qquad\text{a.s. for every }i. Now fix an arm i i . We take four steps: Step 1: Forced exploration is negligible. Let F i ​ ( T ) F_{i}(T) be the number of forced-exploration pulls of arm i i up to time T T . If the m m -th forced pull of arm i i occurs before time T T , then immediately before that pull, N i ​ ( t ) ≥ m − 1 N_{i}(t)\geq m-1 . But a forced pull of arm i i can occur only if N i ​ ( t ) &lt; t + 1 ≤ T + 1 N_{i}(t)&lt;\sqrt{t+1}\leq\sqrt{T+1} . Hence m − 1 &lt; T + 1 m-1&lt;\sqrt{T+1} , and therefore F i ​ ( T ) ≤ ⌈ T + 1 ⌉ + 1 F_{i}(T)\leq\lceil\sqrt{T+1}\rceil+1 . Consequently, F i ​ ( T ) T → 0 . \frac{F_{i}(T)}{T}\to 0. Let F ⁡ ( T ) F(T) be the total number of forced-exploration rounds up to time T T . Since F ⁡ ( T ) = ∑ ℓ = 1 K F ℓ ​ ( T ) F(T)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}F_{\ell}(T) , we also have F ⁡ ( T ) T ≤ K ⁡ ( ⌈ T + 1 ⌉ + 1 ) T → 0 . \frac{F(T)}{T}\leq\frac{K(\lceil\sqrt{T+1}\rceil+1)}{T}\to 0. Step 2: Martingale concentration on non-forced rounds. Let ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} be the history before round t + 1 t+1 , and define B t := 𝟙 ​ { round ​ t + 1 ​ is non-forced } B_{t}:=\mathbbm{1}\{\text{round }t+1\text{ is non-forced}\} . The event { B t = 1 } \{B_{t}=1\} is ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} -measurable. On non-forced rounds, the algorithm samples arm i i with conditional probability p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) . Define D i , t + 1 := B t ( 𝟙 { A t + 1 = i } − p i ( t ) ) D_{i,t+1}:=B_{t}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t+1}=i\}-p_{i}(t)\Bigr) . Then 𝔼 ⁡ [ D i , t + 1 ∣ ℱ t ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[D_{i,t+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]=0 , so ( D i , t + 1 ) t ≥ 0 (D_{i,t+1})_{t\geq 0} is a martingale difference sequence. Also, | D i , t + 1 | ≤ 1 |D_{i,t+1}|\leq 1 . Therefore, by Azuma’s inequality, for every ε &gt; 0 \varepsilon&gt;0 , ℙ ⁡ ( | 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 D i , t + 1 | ≥ ε ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − ε 2 ​ T 2 ) . \mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}D_{i,t+1}\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}T}{2}\right). The right-hand side is summable in T T . Hence, by the Borel–Cantelli lemma, 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 D i , t + 1 → 0 almost surely . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}D_{i,t+1}\to 0\qquad\text{almost surely}. Step 3: Decomposition of the empirical pull count. The total number of pulls of arm i i can be decomposed as N i ( T ) = F i ( T ) + ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t 𝟙 { A t + 1 = i } . N_{i}(T)=F_{i}(T)+\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t+1}=i\}. Using the definition of D i , t + 1 D_{i,t+1} , we have B t 𝟙 { A t + 1 = i } = D i , t + 1 + B t p i ( t ) B_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t+1}=i\}=D_{i,t+1}+B_{t}p_{i}(t) . Thus N i ​ ( T ) T = F i ​ ( T ) T + 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 D i , t + 1 + 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}=\frac{F_{i}(T)}{T}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}D_{i,t+1}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t). By Steps 1 and 2, N i ​ ( T ) T − 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) → 0 almost surely . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t)\to 0\qquad\text{almost surely}. Step 4: Cesàro convergence of the target probabilities. Since p i ​ ( t ) → p i † ​ ( η ) almost surely , p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)\qquad\text{almost surely}, the sequence { p i ​ ( t ) } \{p_{i}(t)\} is Cesàro convergent, i.e., 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 p i ​ ( t ) → p i † ​ ( η ) almost surely . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)\qquad\text{almost surely}. Now write 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) = 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 p i ​ ( t ) − 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 ( 1 − B t ) ​ p i ​ ( t ) . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t)=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}p_{i}(t)-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-B_{t})p_{i}(t). Since 0 ≤ p i ​ ( t ) ≤ 1 0\leq p_{i}(t)\leq 1 , 0 ≤ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 ( 1 − B t ) ​ p i ​ ( t ) ≤ 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 ( 1 − B t ) = F ⁡ ( T ) T → 0 . 0\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-B_{t})p_{i}(t)\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(1-B_{t})=\frac{F(T)}{T}\to 0. Hence 1 T ​ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 B t ​ p i ​ ( t ) → p i † ​ ( η ) almost surely . \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}B_{t}p_{i}(t)\to p_{i}^{\dagger}(\eta)\qquad\text{almost surely}. Combining this with Step 3 gives the desired result. ∎ F.3.5 Posterior Ordering Large Deviations Let X i , 1 , … , X i , n X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,n} denote the first n n rewards observed from arm i i , and let 𝒢 i , n := σ ⁡ ( X i , 1 , … , X i , n ) \mathcal{G}_{i,n}:=\sigma(X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,n}) . Define the one-arm posterior Π i , n \varPi_{i,n} as the conditional distribution of η i = s ⁡ ( μ i ) \eta_{i}=s(\mu_{i}) given 𝒢 i , n \mathcal{G}_{i,n} : Π i , n ​ ( B ) := ℙ ⁡ ( η i ∈ B ∣ 𝒢 i , n ) , B ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) . \varPi_{i,n}(B):=\mathbb{P}(\eta_{i}\in B\mid\mathcal{G}_{i,n}),\qquad B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}). Intuitively, the next lemma proves that Π i , n ​ ( μ i ′ ≈ z ) = exp ⁡ { − n ​ D ​ ( μ i , z ) + o ⁡ ( n ) } \varPi_{i,n}(\mu_{i}^{\prime}\approx z)=\exp\{-nD(\mu_{i},z)+o(n)\} Lemma F.15 (One-arm Posterior Estimates for Ordering) . Fix an arm i i , and work on the event (that happens almost surely) 1 n ​ ∑ r = 1 n t ⁡ ( X i , r ) → A ′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ i ) ) . \frac{1}{n}\sum_{r=1}^{n}t(X_{i,r})\to A^{\prime}(\theta(\mu_{i})). Let Π i , n \varPi_{i,n} be the one-arm posterior defined above. Define I i ​ ( η ) := D ⁡ ( μ i , s − 1 ​ ( η ) ) . I_{i}(\eta):=D(\mu_{i},s^{-1}(\eta)). Then the following hold. First, for every compact interval K ⋐ ℋ K\Subset\mathcal{H} , lim sup n → ∞ 1 n log Π i , n ( K ) ≤ − inf η ∈ K I i ( η ) . \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\varPi_{i,n}(K)\leq-\inf_{\eta\in K}I_{i}(\eta). Second, for every nonempty open interval G ⊆ ℋ G\subseteq\mathcal{H} , lim inf n → ∞ 1 n log Π i , n ( G ) ≥ − inf η ∈ G I i ( η ) . \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\varPi_{i,n}(G)\geq-\inf_{\eta\in G}I_{i}(\eta). Third, for every a , b ∈ ℋ a,b\in\mathcal{H} with a &lt; η i &lt; b a&lt;\eta_{i}&lt;b , lim sup n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ⁡ Π i , n ​ ( η ≤ a ) ≤ − I i ​ ( a ) , \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\varPi_{i,n}(\eta\leq a)\leq-I_{i}(a), and lim sup n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ⁡ Π i , n ​ ( η ≥ b ) ≤ − I i ​ ( b ) . \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\varPi_{i,n}(\eta\geq b)\leq-I_{i}(b). Proof. Set η i = s ⁡ ( μ i ) , θ i := θ ⁡ ( μ i ) = β ⁡ ( η i ) , m i := A ′ ​ ( θ i ) , \eta_{i}=s(\mu_{i}),\qquad\theta_{i}:=\theta(\mu_{i})=\beta(\eta_{i}),\qquad m_{i}:=A^{\prime}(\theta_{i}), and write m ¯ n := 1 n ​ ∑ r = 1 n t ⁡ ( X i , r ) . \bar{m}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r=1}^{n}t(X_{i,r}). For η ∈ ℋ \eta\in\mathcal{H} , write p η ​ ( w ) := d ​ P s − 1 ​ ( η ) d ​ λ ​ ( w ) = exp ⁡ { β ⁡ ( η ) ​ t ​ ( w ) − A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) } ​ h ​ ( w ) . p_{\eta}(w):=\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{s^{-1}(\eta)}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}(w)=\exp\{\beta(\eta)t(w)-A(\beta(\eta))\}h(w). Consider a realized full adaptive history h t = ( a 1 , x 1 , … , a t , x t ) h_{t}=(a_{1},x_{1},\ldots,a_{t},x_{t}) , For a candidate parameter vector 𝜼 = ( η 1 , … , η K ) ∈ ℋ K \bm{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{K})\in\mathcal{H}^{K} , the density of this realized history under the policy π \pi , with respect to counting measure on actions and λ ⊗ t \lambda^{\otimes t} on rewards, is L t π ​ ( 𝜼 , h t ) = ∏ r = 1 t π r ​ ( a r ∣ h r − 1 ) ​ ∏ r = 1 t p η a r ​ ( x r ) . L_{t}^{\pi}(\bm{\eta};h_{t})=\prod_{r=1}^{t}\pi_{r}(a_{r}\mid h_{r-1})\prod_{r=1}^{t}p_{\eta_{a_{r}}}(x_{r}). Here π r ​ ( a r ∣ h r − 1 ) \pi_{r}(a_{r}\mid h_{r-1}) is the sampling probability assigned by the policy to the realized action a r a_{r} after the realized past h r − 1 h_{r-1} . Using the product prior in information coordinates, the posterior density of 𝜼 \bm{\eta} is therefore proportional to ∏ ℓ = 1 K f ¯ ℓ ( η ℓ ) ∏ r : a r = ℓ p η ℓ ( x r ) . \prod_{\ell=1}^{K}\bar{f}_{\ell}(\eta_{\ell})\prod_{r:a_{r}=\ell}p_{\eta_{\ell}}(x_{r}). Thus the posterior factorizes arm by arm. In particular, the marginal posterior of arm i i depends only on the rewards observed from arm i i . If these rewards are denoted X i , 1 , … , X i , n X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,n} , then, for every measurable set B ⊆ ℋ B\subseteq\mathcal{H} , Π i , n ​ ( B ) = ∫ B f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ ∏ r = 1 n p η ​ ( X i , r ) ​ 𝑑 η ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ ∏ r = 1 n p η ​ ( X i , r ) ​ 𝑑 η . \varPi_{i,n}(B)=\frac{\int_{B}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\prod_{r=1}^{n}p_{\eta}(X_{i,r})\,\mathrm{d}\eta}{\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\prod_{r=1}^{n}p_{\eta}(X_{i,r})\,\mathrm{d}\eta}. Substituting the exponential-family density gives ∏ r = 1 n p η ​ ( X i , r ) = exp ⁡ { β ⁡ ( η ) ​ ∑ r = 1 n t ⁡ ( X i , r ) − n ​ A ​ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) } ​ ∏ r = 1 n h ⁡ ( X i , r ) . \prod_{r=1}^{n}p_{\eta}(X_{i,r})=\exp\left\{\beta(\eta)\sum_{r=1}^{n}t(X_{i,r})-nA(\beta(\eta))\right\}\prod_{r=1}^{n}h(X_{i,r}). ∏ r = 1 n h ⁡ ( X i , r ) \prod_{r=1}^{n}h(X_{i,r}) does not depend on η \eta , so it also cancels between numerator and denominator. Bayes’ rule gives Π i , n ​ ( B ) = ∫ B f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ exp ​ { n ⁡ [ β ⁡ ( η ) ​ m ¯ n − A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) ] } ​ 𝑑 η ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ exp ​ { n ⁡ [ β ⁡ ( η ) ​ m ¯ n − A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) ] } ​ 𝑑 η . \varPi_{i,n}(B)=\frac{\int_{B}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\exp\{n[\beta(\eta)\bar{m}_{n}-A(\beta(\eta))]\}\,\mathrm{d}\eta}{\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\exp\{n[\beta(\eta)\bar{m}_{n}-A(\beta(\eta))]\}\,\mathrm{d}\eta}. Multiplying both numerator and denominator by exp ⁡ { − n ⁡ [ β ⁡ ( η i ) ​ m ¯ n − A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η i ) ) ] } \exp\{-n[\beta(\eta_{i})\bar{m}_{n}-A(\beta(\eta_{i}))]\} allows us to write Π i , n ​ ( B ) = ∫ B f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η Z n , \varPi_{i,n}(B)=\frac{\int_{B}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)e^{nL_{n}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta}{Z_{n}}, where L n ​ ( η ) := ( β ⁡ ( η ) − β ⁡ ( η i ) ) ​ m ¯ n − A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) + A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η i ) ) L_{n}(\eta):=(\beta(\eta)-\beta(\eta_{i}))\bar{m}_{n}-A(\beta(\eta))+A(\beta(\eta_{i})) and Z n := ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η . Z_{n}:=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)e^{nL_{n}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta. Note L n ​ ( η i ) = 0 L_{n}(\eta_{i})=0 and L n ​ ( η ) → ( β ⁡ ( η ) − β ⁡ ( η i ) ) ​ A ′ ​ ( β ⁡ ( η i ) ) − A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) + A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η i ) ) = − D ⁡ ( μ i , s − 1 ​ ( η ) ) = − I i ​ ( η ) L_{n}(\eta)\to(\beta(\eta)-\beta(\eta_{i}))A^{\prime}(\beta(\eta_{i}))-A(\beta(\eta))+A(\beta(\eta_{i}))=-D(\mu_{i},s^{-1}(\eta))=-I_{i}(\eta) on compact subsets of ℋ \mathcal{H} . We first prove that 1 n ​ log ⁡ Z n → 0 . \frac{1}{n}\log Z_{n}\to 0. (F.48) For the lower bound on Z n Z_{n} , fix τ &gt; 0 \tau&gt;0 . Since m ¯ n → m i \bar{m}_{n}\to m_{i} , L n ​ ( η ) → − I i ​ ( η ) L_{n}(\eta)\to-I_{i}(\eta) uniformly on compact neighborhoods of η i \eta_{i} . Since I i ​ ( η i ) = 0 I_{i}(\eta_{i})=0 , choose a compact interval J τ ⋐ ℋ J_{\tau}\Subset\mathcal{H} containing η i \eta_{i} such that, for all sufficiently large n n , inf η ∈ J τ L n ​ ( η ) ≥ − τ \inf_{\eta\in J_{\tau}}L_{n}(\eta)\geq-\tau . By continuity and strict positivity of f ¯ i \bar{f}_{i} , ∫ J τ f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η &gt; 0 . \int_{J_{\tau}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta&gt;0. Therefore Z n ≥ ∫ J τ f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η ≥ e − n ​ τ ​ ∫ J τ f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η . Z_{n}\geq\int_{J_{\tau}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)e^{nL_{n}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\geq e^{-n\tau}\int_{J_{\tau}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta. Thus lim inf n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ⁡ Z n ≥ − τ . \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log Z_{n}\geq-\tau. Letting τ ↓ 0 \tau\downarrow 0 gives lim inf n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ⁡ Z n ≥ 0 . \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log Z_{n}\geq 0. For the upper bound on Z n Z_{n} , for all large n n we have m ¯ n ∈ A ′ ​ ( Θ ) \bar{m}_{n}\in A^{\prime}(\Theta) . Define the pseudo-MLE θ n := ( A ′ ) − 1 ​ ( m ¯ n ) , η n := β − 1 ​ ( θ n ) . \theta_{n}:=(A^{\prime})^{-1}(\bar{m}_{n}),\qquad\eta_{n}:=\beta^{-1}(\theta_{n}). Then θ n → θ i \theta_{n}\to\theta_{i} and η n → η i \eta_{n}\to\eta_{i} . Moreover, using m ¯ n = A ′ ​ ( θ n ) \bar{m}_{n}=A^{\prime}(\theta_{n}) , L n ​ ( η n ) − L n ​ ( η ) \displaystyle L_{n}(\eta_{n})-L_{n}(\eta) = ( θ n − β ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ A ′ ​ ( θ n ) − A ⁡ ( θ n ) + A ⁡ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) \displaystyle=(\theta_{n}-\beta(\eta))A^{\prime}(\theta_{n})-A(\theta_{n})+A(\beta(\eta)) = D ⁡ ( s − 1 ​ ( η n ) , s − 1 ​ ( η ) ) . \displaystyle=D(s^{-1}(\eta_{n}),s^{-1}(\eta)). Therefore, by assumption ( F.34 ), L n ​ ( η ) ≤ L n ​ ( η n ) − c ~ ​ ( η − η n ) 2 . L_{n}(\eta)\leq L_{n}(\eta_{n})-\widetilde{c}\,(\eta-\eta_{n})^{2}. Since L n ​ ( η n ) = D ⁡ ( s − 1 ​ ( η n ) , μ i ) → 0 , L_{n}(\eta_{n})=D(s^{-1}(\eta_{n}),\mu_{i})\to 0, boundedness of f ¯ i \bar{f}_{i} gives Z n \displaystyle Z_{n} ≤ ‖ f ¯ i ‖ ∞ ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η n ) ​ ∫ ℋ e − n ​ c ~ ​ ( η − η n ) 2 ​ 𝑑 η \displaystyle\leq\|\bar{f}_{i}\|_{\infty}e^{nL_{n}(\eta_{n})}\int_{\mathcal{H}}e^{-n\widetilde{c}(\eta-\eta_{n})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta ≤ ‖ f ¯ i ‖ ∞ ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η n ) ​ π n ​ c ~ . \displaystyle\leq\|\bar{f}_{i}\|_{\infty}e^{nL_{n}(\eta_{n})}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{n\widetilde{c}}}. Hence lim sup n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ⁡ Z n ≤ 0 . \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log Z_{n}\leq 0. This proves ( F.48 ). Now fix a compact interval K ⋐ ℋ K\Subset\mathcal{H} . Since m ¯ n → m i \bar{m}_{n}\to m_{i} , L n ​ ( η ) → − D ⁡ ( μ i , s − 1 ​ ( η ) ) = − I i ​ ( η ) L_{n}(\eta)\to-D(\mu_{i},s^{-1}(\eta))=-I_{i}(\eta) uniformly on K K . Hence lim sup n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ​ ∫ K f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η \displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{K}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)e^{nL_{n}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta ≤ − inf η ∈ K I i ( η ) . \displaystyle\leq-\inf_{\eta\in K}I_{i}(\eta). Combining this numerator bound with ( F.48 ) gives the compact-interval upper bound. Next let G ⊆ ℋ G\subseteq\mathcal{H} be a nonempty open interval. Choose η 0 ∈ G \eta_{0}\in G such that I i ​ ( η 0 ) ≤ inf η ∈ G I i ​ ( η ) + τ . I_{i}(\eta_{0})\leq\inf_{\eta\in G}I_{i}(\eta)+\tau. Choose a compact interval J ⋐ G J\Subset G containing η 0 \eta_{0} and small enough that sup η ∈ J I i ​ ( η ) ≤ I i ​ ( η 0 ) + τ . \sup_{\eta\in J}I_{i}(\eta)\leq I_{i}(\eta_{0})+\tau. By uniform convergence of L n L_{n} on J J and strict positivity of f ¯ i \bar{f}_{i} on J J , lim inf n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ​ ∫ G f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η \displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{G}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)e^{nL_{n}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta ≥ lim inf n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ​ ∫ J f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ e n ​ L n ​ ( η ) ​ 𝑑 η \displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\int_{J}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)e^{nL_{n}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta ≥ − sup η ∈ J I i ( η ) ≥ − inf η ∈ G I i ( η ) − 2 τ . \displaystyle\geq-\sup_{\eta\in J}I_{i}(\eta)\geq-\inf_{\eta\in G}I_{i}(\eta)-2\tau. Using again the denominator rate ( F.48 ), and then letting τ ↓ 0 \tau\downarrow 0 , gives the open-interval lower bound. It remains to prove the one-sided estimates. Consider a &lt; η i a&lt;\eta_{i} . Since η n → η i \eta_{n}\to\eta_{i} , eventually a &lt; η n a&lt;\eta_{n} . The function θ ↦ θ ​ m ¯ n − A ⁡ ( θ ) \theta\mapsto\theta\bar{m}_{n}-A(\theta) is strictly concave and maximized at θ n \theta_{n} . Since β \beta is strictly increasing, L n ​ ( η ) L_{n}(\eta) is increasing on { η &lt; η n } \{\eta&lt;\eta_{n}\} . Therefore, for all large n n , sup η ≤ a L n ​ ( η ) = L n ​ ( a ) . \sup_{\eta\leq a}L_{n}(\eta)=L_{n}(a). Thus Π i , n ​ ( η ≤ a ) ≤ e n ​ L n ​ ( a ) Z n , \varPi_{i,n}(\eta\leq a)\leq\frac{e^{nL_{n}(a)}}{Z_{n}}, because ∫ { η ≤ a } f ¯ i ( η ) d η ≤ 1 \int_{\{\eta\leq a\}}\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\leq 1 . Since L n ​ ( a ) → − I i ​ ( a ) L_{n}(a)\to-I_{i}(a) and n − 1 ​ log ⁡ Z n → 0 n^{-1}\log Z_{n}\to 0 , we obtain lim sup n → ∞ 1 n ​ log ⁡ Π i , n ​ ( η ≤ a ) ≤ − I i ​ ( a ) . \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\varPi_{i,n}(\eta\leq a)\leq-I_{i}(a). The proof for b &gt; η i b&gt;\eta_{i} is identical: eventually b &gt; η n b&gt;\eta_{n} , L n L_{n} is decreasing on { η &gt; η n } \{\eta&gt;\eta_{n}\} , and hence sup η ≥ b L n ​ ( η ) = L n ​ ( b ) . \sup_{\eta\geq b}L_{n}(\eta)=L_{n}(b). This gives the upper-tail estimate. ∎ The following lemma shows that, if arms i i and j j receive asymptotic sampling proportions q i q_{i} and q j q_{j} , then the posterior probability of incorrectly ordering them decays with exponent inf z ∈ [ μ j , μ i ] { q i ​ D ​ ( μ i , z ) + q j ​ D ​ ( μ j , z ) } . \inf_{z\in[\mu_{j},\mu_{i}]}\{q_{i}D(\mu_{i},z)+q_{j}D(\mu_{j},z)\}. Lemma F.16 (Posterior Ordering Large Deviations) . Fix two arms i i and j j with μ i &gt; μ j \mu_{i}&gt;\mu_{j} . Suppose that N i ​ ( T ) T → q i &gt; 0 , N j ​ ( T ) T → q j &gt; 0 . \frac{N_{i}(T)}{T}\to q_{i}&gt;0,\qquad\frac{N_{j}(T)}{T}\to q_{j}&gt;0. Then almost surely − 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( μ j ≥ μ i ) → inf z ∈ [ μ j , μ i ] { q i ​ D ​ ( μ i , z ) + q j ​ D ​ ( μ j , z ) } . -\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(\mu_{j}\geq\mu_{i})\to\inf_{z\in[\mu_{j},\mu_{i}]}\{q_{i}D(\mu_{i},z)+q_{j}D(\mu_{j},z)\}. (F.49) Proof. Write η i = s ⁡ ( μ i ) , η j = s ⁡ ( μ j ) , η i &gt; η j . \eta_{i}=s(\mu_{i}),\qquad\eta_{j}=s(\mu_{j}),\qquad\eta_{i}&gt;\eta_{j}. Recall from the proof of Lemma F.15 that, conditional on the realized history, the arm-wise posterior of η i = s ⁡ ( μ i ) \eta_{i}=s(\mu_{i}) is Π i , T ​ ( d ​ η ) = f ¯ i ​ ( η ) ​ exp ⁡ { β ⁡ ( η ) ​ S i ​ ( T ) − N i ​ ( T ) ​ A ​ ( β ⁡ ( η ) ) } Z i , T ​ d ​ η , \varPi_{i,T}(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\frac{\bar{f}_{i}(\eta)\exp\{\beta(\eta)S_{i}(T)-N_{i}(T)A(\beta(\eta))\}}{Z_{i,T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta, where Z i , T := ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ exp ⁡ { β ⁡ ( u ) ​ S i ​ ( T ) − N i ​ ( T ) ​ A ​ ( β ⁡ ( u ) ) } ​ 𝑑 u . Z_{i,T}:=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\exp\{\beta(u)S_{i}(T)-N_{i}(T)A(\beta(u))\}\,\mathrm{d}u. Because the prior is a product prior and the likelihood factors arm by arm, the full posterior factorizes: Π T ​ ( d ​ η 1 , … , d ​ η K ) = ⨂ ℓ = 1 K Π ℓ , T ​ ( d ​ η ℓ ) . \varPi_{T}(\mathrm{d}\eta_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{d}\eta_{K})=\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{K}\varPi_{\ell,T}(\mathrm{d}\eta_{\ell}). Consequently, let U ∼ Π i , T U\sim\varPi_{i,T} and V ∼ Π j , T V\sim\varPi_{j,T} denote the posterior information coordinate qualities (arm parameter) of arms i i and j j , then U U and V V are independent under the posterior, and for Borel sets B , C ⊆ ℋ B,C\subseteq\mathcal{H} , Π T ​ ( U ∈ B , V ∈ C ) = Π i , T ​ ( B ) ​ Π j , T ​ ( C ) . \varPi_{T}(U\in B,\ V\in C)=\varPi_{i,T}(B)\varPi_{j,T}(C). In particular, Π T ​ ( μ j ≥ μ i ) = Π T ​ ( η j ≥ η i ) = Π T ​ ( V ≥ U ) . \varPi_{T}(\mu_{j}\geq\mu_{i})=\varPi_{T}(\eta_{j}\geq\eta_{i})=\varPi_{T}(V\geq U). Define I i ​ ( u ) := D ⁡ ( μ i , s − 1 ​ ( u ) ) , I j ​ ( v ) := D ⁡ ( μ j , s − 1 ​ ( v ) ) , I_{i}(u):=D(\mu_{i},s^{-1}(u)),\qquad I_{j}(v):=D(\mu_{j},s^{-1}(v)), and C i ​ j := inf w ∈ [ η j , η i ] { q i ​ I i ​ ( w ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( w ) } . C_{ij}:=\inf_{w\in[\eta_{j},\eta_{i}]}\{q_{i}I_{i}(w)+q_{j}I_{j}(w)\}. The one-arm bounds on log ⁡ Π i , n \log\varPi_{i,n} of Lemma F.15 hold along every subsequence n → ∞ n\to\infty on the arm-wise strong-law event. Therefore they also hold along the random subsequences N i ​ ( T ) N_{i}(T) and N j ​ ( T ) N_{j}(T) . Multiplying the one-arm exponents by N i ​ ( T ) / T → q i N_{i}(T)/T\to q_{i} and N j ​ ( T ) / T → q j N_{j}(T)/T\to q_{j} gives the corresponding T T -speed bounds: For intervals B , C B,C , lim sup T → ∞ 1 T log { Π i , T ( B ) Π j , T ( C ) } ≤ − q i inf u ∈ B I i ( u ) − q j inf v ∈ C I j ( v ) \limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\{\varPi_{i,T}(B)\varPi_{j,T}(C)\}\leq-q_{i}\inf_{u\in B}I_{i}(u)-q_{j}\inf_{v\in C}I_{j}(v) when B , C B,C are compact, and the corresponding lower bound when B , C B,C are open. We first prove the lower bound for ( F.49 ). Let w ⋆ ∈ [ η j , η i ] w^{\star}\in[\eta_{j},\eta_{i}] be a minimizer of w ↦ q i ​ I i ​ ( w ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( w ) . w\mapsto q_{i}I_{i}(w)+q_{j}I_{j}(w). If w ⋆ ∈ ( η j , η i ) w^{\star}\in(\eta_{j},\eta_{i}) , choose δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 so small that ( w ⋆ − δ , w ⋆ ) × ( w ⋆ , w ⋆ + δ ) ⊆ { ( u , v ) : v ≥ u } . (w^{\star}-\delta,w^{\star})\times(w^{\star},w^{\star}+\delta)\subseteq\{(u,v):v\geq u\}. Then, by posterior product decomposition and the open-interval lower bounds in Lemma F.15 , lim inf T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( V ≥ U ) \displaystyle\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(V\geq U) ≥ − q i inf u ∈ ( w ⋆ − δ , w ⋆ ) I i ( u ) − q j inf v ∈ ( w ⋆ , w ⋆ + δ ) I j ( v ) . \displaystyle\geq-q_{i}\inf_{u\in(w^{\star}-\delta,w^{\star})}I_{i}(u)-q_{j}\inf_{v\in(w^{\star},w^{\star}+\delta)}I_{j}(v). Letting δ ↓ 0 \delta\downarrow 0 gives lim inf T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( V ≥ U ) ≥ − C i ​ j . \liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(V\geq U)\geq-C_{ij}. If w ⋆ = η j w^{\star}=\eta_{j} , use instead the rectangle U ∈ ( η j − δ , η j ) U\in(\eta_{j}-\delta,\eta_{j}) and V ∈ ( η j , η j + δ ) V\in(\eta_{j},\eta_{j}+\delta) . Since inf u ∈ ( η j − δ , η j ) I i ​ ( u ) → I i ​ ( η j ) , inf v ∈ ( η j , η j + δ ) I j ​ ( v ) → I j ​ ( η j ) = 0 , \inf_{u\in(\eta_{j}-\delta,\eta_{j})}I_{i}(u)\to I_{i}(\eta_{j}),\qquad\inf_{v\in(\eta_{j},\eta_{j}+\delta)}I_{j}(v)\to I_{j}(\eta_{j})=0, the same lower bound follows. Similarly if w ⋆ = η i w^{\star}=\eta_{i} , use U ∈ ( η i − δ , η i ) U\in(\eta_{i}-\delta,\eta_{i}) and V ∈ ( η i , η i + δ ) V\in(\eta_{i},\eta_{i}+\delta) . Then inf u ∈ ( η i − δ , η i ) I i ​ ( u ) → I i ​ ( η i ) = 0 , inf v ∈ ( η i , η i + δ ) I j ​ ( v ) → I j ​ ( η i ) , \inf_{u\in(\eta_{i}-\delta,\eta_{i})}I_{i}(u)\to I_{i}(\eta_{i})=0,\qquad\inf_{v\in(\eta_{i},\eta_{i}+\delta)}I_{j}(v)\to I_{j}(\eta_{i}), and the same lower bound again follows. Thus lim inf T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( V ≥ U ) ≥ − C i ​ j . \liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(V\geq U)\geq-C_{ij}. For the upper bound, decompose { V ≥ U } ⊆ E − ∪ E 0 ∪ E + , \{V\geq U\}\subseteq E_{-}\cup E_{0}\cup E_{+}, where E − := { U ≤ η j } E_{-}:=\{U\leq\eta_{j}\} , E + := { V ≥ η i } E_{+}:=\{V\geq\eta_{i}\} and E 0 := { η j ≤ U ≤ V ≤ η i } E_{0}:=\{\eta_{j}\leq U\leq V\leq\eta_{i}\} . The one-sided estimates in Lemma F.15 give lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( E − ) ≤ − q i ​ I i ​ ( η j ) = − q i ​ D ​ ( μ i , μ j ) , \limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(E_{-})\leq-q_{i}I_{i}(\eta_{j})=-q_{i}D(\mu_{i},\mu_{j}), and lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( E + ) ≤ − q j ​ I j ​ ( η i ) = − q j ​ D ​ ( μ j , μ i ) . \limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(E_{+})\leq-q_{j}I_{j}(\eta_{i})=-q_{j}D(\mu_{j},\mu_{i}). Since C i ​ j ≤ q i ​ D ​ ( μ i , μ j ) C_{ij}\leq q_{i}D(\mu_{i},\mu_{j}) , by taking w = η j w=\eta_{j} , and C i ​ j ≤ q j ​ D ​ ( μ j , μ i ) C_{ij}\leq q_{j}D(\mu_{j},\mu_{i}) , by taking w = η i w=\eta_{i} , both endpoint events have exponent at least C i ​ j C_{ij} . It remains to control E 0 E_{0} . The set K 0 := { ( u , v ) : η j ≤ u ≤ v ≤ η i } K_{0}:=\{(u,v):\eta_{j}\leq u\leq v\leq\eta_{i}\} is compact. Fix τ &gt; 0 \tau&gt;0 . By continuity of ( u , v ) ↦ q i ​ I i ​ ( u ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( v ) (u,v)\mapsto q_{i}I_{i}(u)+q_{j}I_{j}(v) on the compact square [ η j , η i ] 2 [\eta_{j},\eta_{i}]^{2} , there exist finitely many compact rectangles R m = I m × J m , m = 1 , … , M , R_{m}=I_{m}\times J_{m},\qquad m=1,\ldots,M, whose union covers K 0 K_{0} , such that each R m ⊆ [ η j , η i ] 2 R_{m}\subseteq[\eta_{j},\eta_{i}]^{2} and inf ( u , v ) ∈ R m { q i ​ I i ​ ( u ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( v ) } ≥ inf ( u , v ) ∈ K 0 { q i ​ I i ​ ( u ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( v ) } − τ . \inf_{(u,v)\in R_{m}}\{q_{i}I_{i}(u)+q_{j}I_{j}(v)\}\geq\inf_{(u,v)\in K_{0}}\{q_{i}I_{i}(u)+q_{j}I_{j}(v)\}-\tau. By posterior product decomposition, Π T ​ ( E 0 ) ≤ ∑ m = 1 M Π i , T ​ ( I m ) ​ Π j , T ​ ( J m ) , \varPi_{T}(E_{0})\leq\sum_{m=1}^{M}\varPi_{i,T}(I_{m})\varPi_{j,T}(J_{m}), where Π i , T \varPi_{i,T} and Π j , T \varPi_{j,T} are the corresponding arm-wise posterior marginals. Applying the compact-interval upper bound from Lemma F.15 along the subsequences N i ​ ( T ) N_{i}(T) and N j ​ ( T ) N_{j}(T) , we get, for each m m , lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ { Π i , T ​ ( I m ) ​ Π j , T ​ ( J m ) } \displaystyle\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\{\varPi_{i,T}(I_{m})\varPi_{j,T}(J_{m})\} ≤ − q i inf u ∈ I m I i ( u ) − q j inf v ∈ J m I j ( v ) \displaystyle\leq-q_{i}\inf_{u\in I_{m}}I_{i}(u)-q_{j}\inf_{v\in J_{m}}I_{j}(v) = − inf ( u , v ) ∈ R m { q i I i ( u ) + q j I j ( v ) } . \displaystyle=-\inf_{(u,v)\in R_{m}}\{q_{i}I_{i}(u)+q_{j}I_{j}(v)\}. We now reduce this infimum to the diagonal ( u = v u=v ). Note the derivative ∂ ∂ z ​ D ​ ( μ , z ) = θ ′ ​ ( z ) ​ [ A ′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( z ) ) − A ′ ​ ( θ ⁡ ( μ ) ) ] , \frac{\partial}{\partial z}D(\mu,z)=\theta^{\prime}(z)\left[A^{\prime}(\theta(z))-A^{\prime}(\theta(\mu))\right], is negative for z &lt; μ z&lt;\mu and positive for z &gt; μ z&gt;\mu . Hence, on the interval [ η j , η i ] [\eta_{j},\eta_{i}] , the map u ↦ I i ​ ( u ) u\mapsto I_{i}(u) is non-increasing whereas v ↦ I j ​ ( v ) v\mapsto I_{j}(v) is non-decreasing. Therefore, if u ≤ v u\leq v , then for every w ∈ [ u , v ] w\in[u,v] , I i ​ ( w ) ≤ I i ​ ( u ) I_{i}(w)\leq I_{i}(u) and I j ​ ( w ) ≤ I j ​ ( v ) I_{j}(w)\leq I_{j}(v) . Thus every feasible off-diagonal point can be moved to a diagonal point with no larger objective. Hence inf η j ≤ u ≤ v ≤ η i { q i ​ I i ​ ( u ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( v ) } = inf w ∈ [ η j , η i ] { q i ​ I i ​ ( w ) + q j ​ I j ​ ( w ) } = C i ​ j . \inf_{\eta_{j}\leq u\leq v\leq\eta_{i}}\{q_{i}I_{i}(u)+q_{j}I_{j}(v)\}=\inf_{w\in[\eta_{j},\eta_{i}]}\{q_{i}I_{i}(w)+q_{j}I_{j}(w)\}=C_{ij}. Combining the bounds for E − E_{-} , E 0 E_{0} , and E + E_{+} by a union bound gives lim sup T → ∞ 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( V ≥ U ) ≤ − C i ​ j . \limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(V\geq U)\leq-C_{ij}. Together with the lower bound, − 1 T ​ log ⁡ Π T ​ ( V ≥ U ) → C i ​ j . -\frac{1}{T}\log\varPi_{T}(V\geq U)\to C_{ij}. Returning to z = s − 1 ​ ( w ) z=s^{-1}(w) proves the claimed formula. ∎ F.3.6 Asymptotic Behavior of the Certificate R T R_{T} Recall we defined C † ​ ( μ ) := min ⁡ inf z ∈ [ μ j , μ b ⋆ ] j ≠ b ⋆ ⁡ { p b ⋆ † ​ ( η ) ​ D ​ ( μ b ⋆ , z ) + p j † ​ ( η ) ​ D ​ ( μ j , z ) } . (F.50) Lemma F.17 (Terminal Certificate Limit) . Under Algorithm 2 , R T T → C † ​ ( μ ) a.s. \frac{R_{T}}{T}\to C^{\dagger}(\mu)\qquad\text{a.s.} Proof. We now prove that C † ​ ( μ ) C^{\dagger}(\mu) has the same small lower tail as Γ s 2 / 8 \Gamma_{s}^{2}/8 . Lemma F.18 (Top-Two Gap and Triple-Gap Expansions) . Under Assumption 4.2 , ℙ ⁡ ( Γ s ≤ ε ) = κ ​ ε + o ⁡ ( ε ) , ε ↓ 0 . \mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{s}\leq\varepsilon)=\kappa\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon),\qquad\varepsilon\downarrow 0. Moreover, define Γ 3 , s := η ( 1 ) − η ( 3 ) \Gamma_{3,s}:=\eta_{(1)}-\eta_{(3)} for K ≥ 3 K\geq 3 , and Γ 3 , s := + ∞ \Gamma_{3,s}:=+\infty for K = 2 K=2 , then ℙ ⁡ ( Γ 3 , s ≤ ε ) = O ⁡ ( ε 2 ) . \mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{3,s}\leq\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon^{2}). Proof. For the top-two gap expansion, condition on arm i i having information coordinate value u u . The event that arm i i is best and that the top-two gap is at most ε \varepsilon has conditional probability ∏ k ≠ i F ¯ k ​ ( u ) − ∏ k ≠ i F ¯ k ​ ( u − ε ) , \prod_{k\neq i}\bar{F}_{k}(u)-\prod_{k\neq i}\bar{F}_{k}(u-\varepsilon), where F ¯ k ​ ( t ) \bar{F}_{k}(t) is understood to be 0 0 below the left endpoint of ℋ \mathcal{H} and 1 1 above the right endpoint. Hence ℙ ⁡ ( Γ s ≤ ε ) = ∑ i = 1 K ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ [ ∏ k ≠ i F ¯ k ​ ( u ) − ∏ k ≠ i F ¯ k ​ ( u − ε ) ] ​ 𝑑 u . \mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{s}\leq\varepsilon)=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\left[\prod_{k\neq i}\bar{F}_{k}(u)-\prod_{k\neq i}\bar{F}_{k}(u-\varepsilon)\right]\mathrm{d}u. Let G i ​ ( u ) := ∏ k ≠ i F ¯ k ​ ( u ) . G_{i}(u):=\prod_{k\neq i}\bar{F}_{k}(u). Then G i ′ ​ ( u ) = ∑ j ≠ i f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F ¯ k ​ ( u ) G_{i}^{\prime}(u)=\sum_{j\neq i}\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{F}_{k}(u) where the derivative exists, and G i G_{i} is Lipschitz because the densities are bounded. Therefore G i ​ ( u ) − G i ​ ( u − ε ) ε → G i ′ ​ ( u ) \frac{G_{i}(u)-G_{i}(u-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\to G_{i}^{\prime}(u) for almost every u u , and the quotient is uniformly bounded. Dominated convergence gives ℙ ⁡ ( Γ s ≤ ε ) ε → ∑ i = 1 K ∑ j ≠ i ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j F ¯ k ​ ( u ) ​ d u = κ . \displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{s}\leq\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\to\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sum_{j\neq i}\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{F}_{k}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=\kappa. For the triple-gap bound, the case K = 2 K=2 is trivial. Assume K ≥ 3 K\geq 3 , and let M f ¯ := max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ ‖ f ¯ i ‖ ∞ &lt; ∞ . M_{\bar{f}}:=\max_{i\in[K]}\|\bar{f}_{i}\|_{\infty}&lt;\infty. If Γ 3 , s ≤ ε \Gamma_{3,s}\leq\varepsilon , then there exist three distinct arms i , j , k i,j,k whose information coordinate values all lie in an interval of length ε \varepsilon below the largest of the three. Hence, by a union bound over ordered triples, ℙ ⁡ ( Γ 3 , s ≤ ε ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Gamma_{3,s}\leq\varepsilon) ≤ ∑ i , j , k ​ distinct ∫ ℋ f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ [ ∫ u − ε u f ¯ j ​ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v ] ​ [ ∫ u − ε u f ¯ k ​ ( w ) ​ 𝑑 w ] ​ 𝑑 u \displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j,k\ {\rm distinct}}\int_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{f}_{i}(u)\left[\int_{u-\varepsilon}^{u}\bar{f}_{j}(v)\,\mathrm{d}v\right]\left[\int_{u-\varepsilon}^{u}\bar{f}_{k}(w)\,\mathrm{d}w\right]\mathrm{d}u ≤ K ⁡ ( K − 1 ) ​ ( K − 2 ) ​ M f ¯ 2 ​ ε 2 . \displaystyle\leq K(K-1)(K-2)M_{\bar{f}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}. ∎ Define the ordered top-pair tie manifold 𝒯 ↑ := ⨄ i ≠ j { ( i , j , u , ζ − i ​ j ) : u ∈ ℋ , ζ k ∈ ℋ , ζ k &lt; u ∀ k ≠ i , j } . \mathcal{T}^{\uparrow}:=\biguplus_{i\neq j}\left\{(i,j,u,\zeta_{-ij}):u\in\mathcal{H},\ \zeta_{k}\in\mathcal{H},\ \zeta_{k}&lt;u\ \forall k\neq i,j\right\}. Here i i denotes the best arm and j j denotes the second-best arm. On the component ( i , j ) (i,j) , define the finite measure λ ⁡ ( d ​ ξ ) := f ¯ i ​ ( u ) ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) ​ ∏ k ≠ i , j f ¯ k ​ ( ζ k ) ​ d ​ u ​ d ​ ζ − i ​ j . \lambda(\mathrm{d}\xi):=\bar{f}_{i}(u)\bar{f}_{j}(u)\prod_{k\neq i,j}\bar{f}_{k}(\zeta_{k})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\zeta_{-ij}. Then λ ⁡ ( 𝒯 ↑ ) = κ \lambda(\mathcal{T}^{\uparrow})=\kappa . Lemma F.19 (Compact Localization of Top-Two Gap Density) . For every ρ &gt; 0 \rho&gt;0 , there exists a compact set 𝒦 ρ ⊂ 𝒯 ↑ \mathcal{K}_{\rho}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\uparrow} such that, for every fixed R &lt; ∞ R&lt;\infty , ℙ ⁡ { Γ s ≤ R ​ ε , ξ ↑ ​ ( η ) ∈ 𝒦 ρ } ≥ ( κ − ρ ) ​ R ​ ε + o ⁡ ( ε ) , \mathbb{P}\{\Gamma_{s}\leq R\varepsilon,\,\xi^{\uparrow}(\eta)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rho}\}\geq(\kappa-\rho)R\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon), and ℙ ⁡ { Γ s ≤ R ​ ε , ξ ↑ ​ ( η ) ∉ 𝒦 ρ } ≤ ρ ​ R ​ ε + o ⁡ ( ε ) , \mathbb{P}\{\Gamma_{s}\leq R\varepsilon,\,\xi^{\uparrow}(\eta)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\rho}\}\leq\rho R\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon), as ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 . Proof. Since 𝒯 ↑ \mathcal{T}^{\uparrow} is a finite disjoint union of open subsets of Euclidean spaces and λ \lambda is a finite Borel measure with density with respect to Lebesgue measure on each component, λ \lambda is a finite Radon measure. By continuity of f ¯ j \bar{f}_{j} on the compact projection of 𝒦 ρ \mathcal{K}_{\rho} , ∫ u − R ​ ε u f ¯ j ​ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v = R ​ ε ​ f ¯ j ​ ( u ) + o ⁡ ( ε ) \int_{u-R\varepsilon}^{u}\bar{f}_{j}(v)\,\mathrm{d}v=R\varepsilon\,\bar{f}_{j}(u)+o(\varepsilon) uniformly over 𝒦 ρ \mathcal{K}_{\rho} . Therefore ℙ ⁡ { Γ s ≤ R ​ ε , ξ ↑ ​ ( η ) ∈ 𝒦 ρ } = R ​ ε ​ λ ​ ( 𝒦 ρ ) + o ⁡ ( ε ) ≥ ( κ − ρ ) ​ R ​ ε + o ⁡ ( ε ) . \mathbb{P}\{\Gamma_{s}\leq R\varepsilon,\,\xi^{\uparrow}(\eta)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rho}\}=R\varepsilon\,\lambda(\mathcal{K}_{\rho})+o(\varepsilon)\geq(\kappa-\rho)R\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon). As u ↓ 0 u\downarrow 0 , ℙ ⁡ ( C † ​ ( μ ) ≤ u ) = κ ​ 8 ​ u + o ⁡ ( u ) . \mathbb{P}(C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq u)=\kappa\sqrt{8u}+o(\sqrt{u}). Proof. We prove matching lower and upper bounds. By the uniform local expansion in Lemma F.21 , for all sufficiently small u u , { Γ s ≤ ( 1 − τ ) 8 ​ u , ξ ↑ ( η ) ∈ 𝒦 ρ } ⊆ { C † ( μ ) ≤ u } . \left\{\Gamma_{s}\leq(1-\tau)\sqrt{8u},\ \xi^{\uparrow}(\eta)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rho}\right\}\subseteq\{C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq u\}. Therefore, using Lemma F.19 with ε = u \varepsilon=\sqrt{u} and R = ( 1 − τ ) ​ 8 R=(1-\tau)\sqrt{8} , ℙ ⁡ ( C † ​ ( μ ) ≤ u ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq u) ≥ ℙ ⁡ ( Γ s ≤ ( 1 − τ ) ​ 8 ​ u , ξ ↑ ​ ( η ) ∈ 𝒦 ρ ) \displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\Gamma_{s}\leq(1-\tau)\sqrt{8u},\ \xi^{\uparrow}(\eta)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rho}\right) ≥ ( κ − ρ ) ​ ( 1 − τ ) ​ 8 ​ u + o ⁡ ( u ) . \displaystyle\geq(\kappa-\rho)(1-\tau)\sqrt{8u}+o(\sqrt{u}). For the upper bound, Lemma F.20 gives { C † ( μ ) ≤ u } ⊆ { Γ s ≤ R ⋆ u } . \{C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq u\}\subseteq\{\Gamma_{s}\leq R^{\star}\sqrt{u}\}. Fix ρ &gt; 0 \rho&gt;0 , and again use the compact set 𝒦 ρ \mathcal{K}_{\rho} . On { C † ​ ( μ ) ≤ u , ξ ↑ ​ ( η ) ∈ 𝒦 ρ } , \{C^{\dagger}(\mu)\leq u,\ \xi^{\uparrow}(\eta)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rho}\}, Lemma F.20 implies Γ s → 0 \Gamma_{s}\to 0 uniformly as u ↓ 0 u\downarrow 0 . Hence the uniform local expansion gives, for all sufficiently small u u , C † ​ ( μ ) ≥ ( 1 − τ ) ​ Γ s 2 8 . C^{\dagger}(\mu)\geq(1-\tau)\frac{\Gamma_{s}^{2}}{8}. Consequently, on the same event, Γ s ≤ 8 ​ u 1 − τ ≤ ( 1 + τ ) ​ 8 ​ u , \Gamma_{s}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8u}{1-\tau}}\leq(1+\tau)\sqrt{8u}, after decreasing u u if necessary and replacing τ \tau by a smaller slack constant. Combining the two bounds proves the claim. ∎ F.3.8 Lower Bound on the Calibration Threshold Lemma F.23 (Lower Bound on the Calibration Threshold) .
